ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
We extend our deepest gratitude to all those who contributed to the successful completion of 
this report. This endeavor would not have been possible without the collective effort, expertise, 
and commitment of numerous individuals and institutions who dedicated their time and shared 
their insights with us.
First, we extend our sincere appreciation to Madhav Pai, CEO of WRI India, for encouraging us to 
undertake this work, and to Pawan Mulukutla, Executive Director of Integrated Transport, Clean 
Air & Hydrogen, WRI India, for facilitating its realization. Their leadership, vision, commitment to 
advancing urban mobility and road safety, steadfast support, strategic guidance, and unwavering 
belief in this project ensured its successful completion.
We also offer our heartfelt thanks to the key informants who generously shared their knowledge 
and invaluable perspectives, strengthening the depth of this research. These include current and 
ex-government officials, civil engineers, urban planners, architects, urban designers, academics, 
and practitioners. Their expertise has been vital in shaping this evaluation, adding valuable 
real-world context to the analysis.
We are immensely grateful to our colleagues for their significant contributions: Akhila Suri 
for her foundational work in framing urban mobility challenges in Mumbai, and Tanushree 
Venkatraman for her support in conducting interviews, transcriptions, and fact collection, which 
provided crucial insights that enriched this report.
We are also deeply appreciative of our internal and external reviewers: Manu Mathai, Shahana 
Chattaraj, Robin King, Claudia Adriazola-Steil, Paula Santos, Pawan Mulukutla, Abhimanyu 
Prakash, Sonal Shah, Ratoola Kundu, and Dr. Ashish Verma. Their rigorous reviews and 
thoughtful critiques ensured the highest standards of analysis, clarity, and coherence in writing, 
achieving a greater impact of this evaluation report.
Finally, we express our heartfelt gratitude to BIGRS and our institutional partners, BMC, MTP, 
and MMRDA, whose collaboration, insights, and support made the last decade of processes 
and solutions to enable safer streets in Mumbai possible. Their contributions have been vital in 
advancing urban mobility and road safety initiatives in the city.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 5
Foreword
As I reflect on our decade-long work toward making 
Mumbai’s streets safer for everyone, I am filled with 
both immense pride and a sense of urgency. Over 
the last ten years, WRI India has worked tirelessly 
alongside its partners to address one of the most 
pressing challenges of our time—making our streets 
safer, inclusive, and accessible.
Mumbai is among the world’s most populous cities, 
with about 15 million people living in a 434 sq. km 
area. The city’s dense urban form is transit-oriented 
and mixed-use, leading to a high share of walking 
trips. More than half the trips are made on foot. 
With the rapid increase in motorization over the last 
three decades, Mumbai’s urban mobility efforts have 
prioritized large infrastructure projects to improve 
vehicular movement alone, often overlooking the 
critical need to enhance walking, cycling, and public 
transport. This approach has resulted in unsafe 
environments for vulnerable pedestrians.
Before 2015, global road safety efforts primarily 
targeted enforcement and legislation. Recognizing 
the need for a paradigm shift, Bloomberg 
Philanthropies extended support to WRI India 
(then EMBARQ), to reimagine road safety through 
the lens of infrastructure and design. In 2015, 
WRI India became the technical partner of the 
Brihanmumbai Municipal Corporation (BMC) 
under the Bloomberg Initiative for Global Road 
Safety (BIGRS). This acted as a catalyst to scale 
pedestrian infrastructure projects and reinforce the 
city’s commitment to safer streets. 
Through this engagement, we have worked toward 
the adoption of the globally successful Safe System 
approach for designing and constructing streets. 
Armed with this approach, we realized that Mumbai 
must embrace a holistic perspective of road safety 
by integrating civil engineering, traffic engineering, 
and urban design when planning, designing, and 
constructing roads. Additionally, Mumbai needs to 
mainstream three core traffic engineering principles 
to reduce fatalities and improve walkability: (i) 
addressing design speed; (ii) reducing traffic 
conflicts; (iii) minimizing shockwaves—that is, traffic 
congestion and queueing. The HP Petrol Pump 
Junction pilot in Bandra demonstrated that applying 
these principles reduced conflict points, improved 
traffic flow by 20 percent, and made roads more 
accessible for all users.These interventions underscore 
how designing streets for people improves safety and 
channelizes traffic flow better.
This report is a first-of-its-kind formative evaluation 
of the decade-long pedestrian-focused road safety 
initiatives undertaken by BMC, in partnership with 
WRI India and other partners, under the BIGRS 
program. As we look ahead, the scale and complexity 
of this challenge demand coalition building and 
collaboration. Governments, urban planners, 
civil society, private sector stakeholders, and local 
communities must come together to champion 
walking. Several big infrastructure projects, like 
the 330km of metro construction in the Mumbai 
Metropolitan Region, offer significant opportunities 
for creating pedestrian infrastructure in the city.
This report is a call to action. Let us commit to 
reducing road crashes and fatalities and ensuring that 
every journey—whether on foot, bicycle, or public 
transport—is safe. Together, we can humanize our 
cities, create equitable public spaces, and secure a 
brighter urban future.
Madhav Pai  
CEO, WRI India

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 7
Executive summary
Mumbai is the second-largest city in India. Despite 
a significant lack of safe pedestrian infrastructure, 
most trips in the city are made on foot. The poor, 
who make up a large portion of pedestrians, face a 
disproportionately higher risk of road crash injuries and 
fatalities. This makes road safety in Mumbai, as in other 
Indian cities, a critical equity issue. Mumbai has been 
working to improve road safety and develop pedestrian 
infrastructure using the Safe System approach for over 
a decade. This report is the first to conduct a formative 
evaluation, providing valuable insights into the evolving 
nature of road safety in Mumbai. These insights are 
crucial for scaling up walkable infrastructure in Indian 
cities and achieving systemic changes in planning and 
designing safe and resilient urban infrastructure.

8 I wri-india.org
HIGHLIGHTS
 ▪ This report presents a first-of-its-kind  
formative evaluation of a decade of initiatives 
promoting safer streets in Mumbai, with a 
focus on pedestrian safety.
 ▪ Between 2010 and 2024, about 2.3 percent of 
the Brihanmumbai Municipal Corporation's 
(BMC) budget was dedicated to pedestrian 
infrastructure, while pedestrians accounted for 
47 percent of road traffic fatalities. 
 ▪ In 2015, under the Bloomberg Initiative for 
Global Road Safety (BIGRS), WRI India became 
BMC’s technical partner, working to scale 
pedestrian infrastructure through systemic 
engagement, programmatic interventions, and 
evidence-based design.
 ▪ Over the past decade, WRI India has assisted 
the city in implementing pilot interventions 
and large-scale programs funded by 
municipal budgets, demonstrating how traffic 
engineering and urban design can greatly 
reduce pedestrian risks and save lives. 
 ▪ Road safety practice in Mumbai is gradually 
shifting from a traditional civil engineering-led 
approach to an urban design-led approach, 
reimagining streets as public spaces. However, 
the impact of these ideas remains under-
communicated to policymakers and the public.
 ▪ The sustainability of implemented projects 
and the future of safe-streets programs 
remain uncertain due to rapidly evolving 
transport infrastructure, shifting priorities of 
the government, and lack of coordination 
among agencies.
BACKGROUND
How does an Indian city make its streets safer for people? 
What kind of data and evidence supports decision-making 
and secures stakeholder buy-ins? How are the capacities 
and responsibilities of local authorities strengthened 
to deliver safe streets? Why do new investments in big 
infrastructure continue to disregard road safety? This report 
attempts to answer many such questions by evaluating a 
decade of road safety initiatives promoting safer streets for 
pedestrians in Mumbai.
Nearly 437 people die on Indian roads daily (National 
Crime Records Bureau 2022) despite India having 
low per capita vehicular ownership compared to many 
developed countries (Ministry of Road Transport and 
Highways 2023). A high proportion of on-road fatalities 
and injuries are among pedestrians, as walking is the 
predominant mode of travel, accounting for 63 percent of 
daily trips. Despite this, roads are primarily planned and 
designed for motorized transport, overlooking pedestrians, 
transit, and non-motorized transport (NMT) users. 
India’s road safety challenges result from poor road 
design and engineering, delayed trauma response and 
care, and inadequate capacity to enforce traffic rules. The 
National Urban Transport Policy, for the first time, brought 
a focus on the need for promoting walking and cycling 
infrastructure to ensure pedestrian and cyclist safety and 
set out its objective as “bringing about a more equitable 
allocation of road space with people, rather than vehicles” 
(Ministry of Urban Development 2006, 3). Recognizing 
road safety as a public health crisis, the Government of 
India introduced the National Road Safety Policy in 2010. 
However, there are no legally binding regulations for 
designing streets and public spaces to ensure pedestrian 
and cyclists’ safety. Between 2016 and 2020, more than 
30 percent of fatalities on Indian streets occurred at 
junctions with poor geometric alignment, leading to high 
speeds, inadequate channelization of traffic flows, and no 
consideration for vulnerable road users such as pedestrians 
(Ministry of Road Transport and Highways 2022). Traffic 
jams are seen as technical issues requiring engineering 
solutions. These solutions aim to increase road capacity to 
improve the flow of vehicles at the expense of pedestrians, 
cyclists, and transit users. 
The absence of regulations makes it challenging to 
implement safe-street designs and develop walking and 
cycling infrastructure at scale in Indian cities. This has 
also led different cities in various states to develop their

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 9
own guidelines. However, several questions remain: What 
processes are followed to move from a design concept to 
implementation? How are the implementations evaluated? 
Are one-off initiatives the norm, or are Indian cities 
successfully scaling safe-street interventions across each 
city? Furthermore, if so, what is enabling this shift within 
the cities’ social, economic, and political contexts?
ABOUT THIS REPORT
Mumbai has been improving road safety in line with 
international and national commitments by developing 
pedestrian infrastructure using the Safe System 
approach for over a decade. Mumbai’s induction into the 
BIGRS city network was pivotal in consistently elevating 
the need for safer streets for pedestrians on diverse 
policy platforms.
Having played a significant role in this process, WRI 
India conducted this formative evaluation to gain 
valuable insights from the evolving nature of road safety 
in Mumbai. We found the beginning of a shift from a 
purely civil-engineering-led approach to street design 
to a more urban-design-led approach, led by building 
a community of practice to reimagine streets as public 
spaces.
Using examples from projects undertaken by the BMC 
in partnership with WRI India and other partners, 
this report highlights how creating safer streets in 
Mumbai work at different systemic levels, leveraging 
policies, plans, budgets, legal frameworks, and city-
making and urban transformation agendas to promote 
pedestrian safety at scale. It discusses three key innovative 
approaches or pathways of change deployed by WRI India 
while coordinating with a wide range of stakeholders in 
framing Mumbai’s mobility challenges, centering street 
improvement practices in support of pedestrian access as 
an important part of transforming cities.
These three approaches also underpin the scope of this 
formative evaluation. They include systemic engagements 
to trigger a transformational change in urban mobility, 
programmatic interventions for street transformations, and 
design interventions for safer streets at 82 intersections 
and along 38 km of corridors. 
This learning-focused formative evaluation of the evolving 
nature of road safety in Mumbai is the first of its kind. It 
seeks to provide valuable insights critical for scaling up 
walkable infrastructure and achieving systemic changes 
in how we plan and design streets in Indian cities. We 
adopt the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and 
Development’s (OECD) evaluation criteria (OECD 
2021) to conduct a high-quality evaluation of safe-street 
interventions in Mumbai by selecting four criteria: 
relevance, coherence, effectiveness and sustainability.

KEY FINDINGS
Citywide action promoting safer streets by design 
has come a long way in Mumbai from the vision first 
articulated in the National Urban Transport Policy 
(NUTP) in 2006. In 2012, when WRI India started 
working on street design, road safety was a nascent issue 
in Mumbai. Through the three approaches of systemic 
engagement, programmatic interventions, and technical 
support for design interventions, the BMC and their 
partners managed to move the needle on making streets 
safer an “emerging issue” by 2018 and a more “mainstream 
issue” by 2022.
This evaluation highlights how this change was effected 
in both the processes and outcomes by acting on 
interdependent leverage points within complex urban 
governance systems. These changes included changes 
in policies, municipal budgets, institutional practices, 
stakeholder perceptions, physical changes, behavior, and 
perception changes. Initially, the safe-streets initiative 
scaled through a linear model, building on pilot projects 
across different parts of the city. These pilots impacted 
about 6 percent of intersections and 1 percent of Mumbai’s 
streets. In the next phase, WRI India developed the 
Mumbai Street Transformation Project (MSTP) to scale 
up through wide-ranging partnerships to cover 300 km 
of streets, potentially impacting 15 percent of Mumbai’s 
streets. However, this has yet to be implemented.
It took five to six years of sustained engagement with the 
city, from informing policy discussions to supporting 
on-ground implementation of data-driven design 
interventions, largely funded by municipal budgets, to 
see visible results. Factors that contributed to the success 
of scaling these new experimental ideas included securing 
the buy-in of diverse stakeholders. Though BMC is the 
most important player undertaking street improvements in 
Mumbai, other influential stakeholders with high power 
and high interest in transforming streets, such as the traffic 
police and elected representatives, were also on board with 
the idea and formed part of the feedback loop. Equally, 
citizens and other members with limited authority but 
high interest may have urgent claims and legitimate views 
that can drive or undermine change.
WRI India has mostly engaged with one clear change 
pathway— BMC’s annual street improvement process. 
But other critical pathways related to big infrastructure 
projects that have a significant and lasting impact on 
road safety and walkability in Mumbai have not yet been 
leveraged to the same extent. Similarly, new pathways 
This learning-focused formative evaluation has three 
phases with clear objectives for each phase.
1. Research phase: The objectives of this initial phase 
were to identify the main challenges to urban mobility 
in Mumbai and the issues related to pedestrian safety, 
the institutions/rules of the game that define the 
state of play on improving streets, the stakeholders 
involved in making streets safer by design, and the 
most prevalent pathways of change adopted by 
the government.
2. Evaluation phase: The main objective of this phase 
was to assess the relevance, coherence, effectiveness, and 
sustainability of identified approaches to improving 
road safety and creating safe walking environments in 
Mumbai, with a particular focus on WRI India’s role.
3. Learning phase: This phase had one key objective, 
which was to discuss key lessons learned from the 
work on safe streets for pedestrians in Mumbai and to 
understand the effectiveness of the current approaches 
to city transformation. 
Using a longitudinal case study as the primary 
method, this evaluation employs mixed methods 
incorporating both qualitative and quantitative data 
collection techniques.
Methods include document review, mapping stakeholders, 
key informant interviews, nested case studies of 
implemented projects, analyzing crash data from the 
Mumbai Traffic Police (MTP), mapping physical and 
behavioral changes, and conducting perception surveys.
The three phases of the evaluation employ different 
analytical approaches that feed into and reinforce each 
other: A political economy analysis for the research 
phase defines the problem and looks at institutional 
arrangements and the possible drivers for addressing 
the problem (this analysis was critical in evaluating the 
relevance of the three approaches to solving the problem); 
constructing a Theory of Change to analyze the modus 
operandi of the three approaches deployed by WRI 
India in making streets safer by design in Mumbai in the 
evaluation phase; and using a systems change framework 
for developing insights for urban transformations 
using safer streets in the learning phase. This report 
aims to enhance organization and ecosystem-wide 
learning using the case of Mumbai and contributes 
to the sparse literature on high-quality evaluations of 
urban interventions.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 11
emerging from the Mumbai Climate Action Plan and 
Mumbai’s Climate Budget require design interventions to 
look beyond geometric correction to build climate-resilient 
street infrastructure and enable a shift from increasing 
private motor vehicle use to public transportation and 
NMT .
WRI India deployed an integrated approach that 
combined urban design as a catalyst for change 
alongside traditional traffic engineering, enhancing the 
existing civil engineering approach. To comprehensively 
understand vehicular and pedestrian behaviors, the data-
driven method involved collecting and analyzing baseline 
data, including traffic volume counts, signal phase timing, 
and pedestrian movement patterns. The aim was to 
identify conflict points, preferred routes, and unauthorized 
movements caused by existing geometry. Queue lengths 
and activity mapping were used to contextually analyze 
streets and intersections, while crash data (wherever 
available) were examined to assess safety concerns. For a 
full list of traffic engineering and urban design features 
incorporated in safe-street designs in Mumbai, see 
Appendix E. The specifications of each of these were based 
on evolving IRC guidelines for urban roads and global best 
practices in traffic engineering and urban design (Welle et 
al. 2015).
Analysis of crash data undertaken by this evaluation 
at a sample of six intersections and three street 
corridors revealed that pedestrian involvement in 
crashes, fatalities, and serious injuries was reduced at 
sample sites, suggesting that the implemented changes 
contributed to saving lives. Physical changes resulting 
from traffic engineering and urban design, like those 
mentioned above and in Appendix E, greatly enhanced 
pedestrian safety in navigating intersections. The evaluation 
also found greater pedestrian compliance in following 
designed routes in streets where safe-street design features 
were more extensively implemented.
Perception surveys conducted for this evaluation at 
three intersections and two street corridors showed 
that overall, at intersections, 75 percent of surveyed 
pedestrians (n=455) felt “extremely safe” to “safe,” while 
along corridors (n=313), 73 percent felt “extremely 
safe” to “safe.” Key contributing reasons cited for feeling 
safe were mostly social factors, such as the presence of 
people (44 percent), familiarity with surroundings (9 
percent), physical street design interventions (9 percent), 
surveillance (8 percent), and street activities (5 percent).

12 I wri-india.org
Furthermore, the survey results showed that social 
factors determine people’s perception of safety more 
than physical design features. However, when the 
physical features of a setting support behaviors associated 
with the social life of public spaces, they often become 
imperceptible—the hallmark of a successful intervention 
and a measure of the effectiveness of physical change.
One of the most noteworthy results achieved by the 
interventions was the visible changes to the built 
environment of the streets to provide safer access to 
pedestrians. A total of 90,338 square meters of public 
space was reclaimed across all 21 intersections and 20 km 
of corridors where WRI India implemented safe-street 
designs. Despite this significant increase in pedestrianized 
public spaces in the city, several implementation challenges 
remain that often compromise the quality of design.
Safer streets for pedestrians in Mumbai remain an 
unfulfilled promise; they are not self-sustaining and 
have not achieved population scale, despite significant 
tangible influence on citizens’ daily lives. Despite having 
municipal budgets and other public funds to support this 
work, significant programs like Safe Access to Schools 
(SATS) and the Mumbai Street Transformation Project 
(MSTP) did not advance as expected. Design-based 
technical engagements can leave many important pathways 
of change unexplored, particularly those related to 
transition management within politically charged complex 
governance systems. The lack of sustainability of scaled-up 
initiatives when the political or administrative priorities 
shift, requires a deeper understanding of system transitions 
and political change management.
The current pathways of street improvements, focused 
primarily on geometric corrections, do not effectively 
embed equity in terms of adequately including the 
marginalized urban poor, who are disproportionately 
affected by crashes, as they rely on walking much more 
than others. This is a gap, as equitable street improvement 
requires accommodating the needs of all users through 
solutions arrived at by consulting all stakeholders.

LESSONS LEARNED
Several significant lessons emerge from this evaluation.
 ▪ The first lesson, drawn from the decade of processes 
and solutions, is that change is possible, but it requires 
long-term engagement and investment to play the 
long game, as is typically the case with any urban 
transformation work.
 ▪ The second lesson is that purely technical engagements 
can leave many important pathways of change 
unexplored. The scale and complexity of the work 
requires coalition building to engage with diverse 
stakeholders, address the challenges of inter-agency 
coordination and political economy challenges of any 
context, explore new pathways, and scale up the work.
 ▪ Third, a lack of clear strategies for change management 
within complex urban governance systems can lead 
to uncertainty about scaled-up initiatives and project 
sustainability.
 ▪ Fourth, a lack of mandatory guidelines to support 
work at the local level through established non-
negotiable principles of safe-street design for all types 
of streets, makes it difficult for the approved programs 
and projects to progress when the priorities of the 
government shift.
 ▪ Fifth, in the absence of established principles and 
mandatory regulations for safe-street design, there 
is a reluctance to adopt new ideas and processes, 
which requires championing by key individuals 
within the government who can support and advance 
innovative design solutions and navigate bureaucratic 
hurdles. However, frequent personnel transfers tend 
to undermine advocacy by champions and capacity-
building efforts by non-state actors such as WRI India.
 ▪ Sixth, to improve streets at scale and sustain the 
initiatives, long-term engagement is needed to build 
transformational capacities within the system and 
influence mental models that consider the impact on 
people’s lives by building a sustainable transport system 
beyond external funding timelines and political cycles.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 15
RECOMMENDATIONS
This report lays out two sets of recommendations for 
decision-makers within municipal governments, urban 
practitioners, and policymakers, particularly in Mumbai. 
While Mumbai has already made significant progress and 
is looking to scale up and institutionalize these efforts, 
these recommendations may also apply to other cities, 
including cities with smaller budgets and less government 
capacity, in India and beyond. 
TABLE ES-1  |  Recommendations stemming from the formative evaluation

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 17

18 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 1.
  
Introduction
India faces a road safety crisis, with 437 lives lost 
daily, especially impacting pedestrians and cyclists 
despite low vehicle ownership. Rapid motorization, 
poor enforcement of the National Urban Transport 
Policy, and a focus on vehicular flow neglect non-
motorized users. However, new urban design 
perspectives are reimagining streets as public 
spaces. This introductory chapter presents a 
framework for evaluating Mumbai’s decade-long 
efforts to enhance pedestrian safety. It sets the stage 
for this report that highlights key practices to inform 
strategies for scaling safe, inclusive, and walkable 
streets across Indian cities.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 19

BACKGROUND
India is the most populous country in the world and one of 
her fastest-growing economies. The transportation sector 
has played a crucial role in India’s economic development 
by enabling the movement of goods and people within and 
between cities. Though the average decadal growth rate 
of motorized vehicles in India is around 10 percent, the 
growth tends to concentrate in large cities where vehicular 
ownership is high due to higher economic activities and 
inadequacies in public transportation systems (Singh 
2012). Only 8 percent of Indian households, or 1 in 12, 
own cars, with most Indians (54 percent) still owning two-
wheelers (International Institute for Population Sciences 
and ICF 2021). Despite having low vehicular ownership 
per thousand population compared to many developed 
countries, though this has been rising (see Figure 1), India 
ranks third in the world for the number of crashes, first for 
the number of persons killed, and third for the number of 
persons injured in road crashes (PTI 2022).
Nearly 437 people were killed on Indian roads daily in 
2021 (National Crime Records Bureau 2022) and almost 
70 percent of these fatalities were of people aged 18–45 
(Ministry of Road Transport and Highways 2022). 
Between 2010 and 2019 instances of crashes, deaths, and 
injuries stabilized at a reduced level from the pre-2010 
levels, coinciding with the adoption of the National Road 
Safety policy in 2010. However, even though overall deaths 
decreased, crash-related deaths increased, particularly 
among users of non-motorized modes of transport, due 
to an increase in motorized traffic and the absence of 
non-motorized transport infrastructure (Verma et al. 
2021). Of the 155,622 road traffic fatalities in India in 
2021, and the 371,884 serious injuries in the same year, 
40.3 percent occurred in urban areas (National Crime 
Records Bureau 2022).

C. Ray Jeffries, and Donald Appleyard championed this 
shift, emphasizing the importance of streets in fostering 
community, promoting pedestrian safety, and supporting 
vibrant street-based activities. For example, Jacobs 
highlighted how the “ballet of the sidewalk” depends on 
diverse uses, fostering informal surveillance and a sense 
of safety. Gehl focused on creating walkable, human-scale 
environments that encourage interaction and enhance 
urban life. In a recently published book, The Social Life of 
Streets in India, the editors argue that streets are not merely 
infrastructural entities but deeply social and cultural 
landscapes that reflect the histories of contestation, power, 
and community engagement. They are sites of negotiation, 
where formal planning and informal practices intersect, 
creating spaces of dynamism, diversity, and often friction 
( Jha and Bharat 2023). 
India has developed several policies and passed acts to 
regulate the transport sector since the late 1970s. However, 
it was the first iteration of the National Urban Transport 
Policy (NUTP) in 2006 that, for the first time, brought 
a focus on the need for promoting walking and cycling 
infrastructure to ensure pedestrian and cyclist safety on 
streets. Very much in line with the Complete Streets 
rhetoric that challenges the autonormativity of streets, 
the NUTP set out its objective as “bringing about a more 
equitable allocation of road space with people, rather than 
vehicles, as its main focus,” having diagnosed that Indian 
roads “are designed badly and without fully recognizing the 
limitations and problems faced by cyclists or pedestrians” 
(Ministry of Urban Development 2006, 3). Two of the 
problems cited were encroachment on footpaths (sidewalks 
are called footpaths or pavements in India; these three 
terms are used interchangeably in this report) by public 
and private interests and a lack of safe crossing facilities at 
busy intersections and corridors with high traffic volumes. 
However, there is an actual lack of footpaths in Indian 
cities, and the problem is worse in smaller cities and 
peri-urban areas.
Beyond the fatalities, the lack of safe streets significantly 
affects mobility, especially for vulnerable groups like 
children, older people, and people with disabilities. It 
also limits access to essential services, such as schools, 
healthcare, and markets, for those who rely on walking. 
Furthermore, unsafe streets deter walking and cycling 
and encourage the purchase and use of private vehicles, 
exacerbating traffic congestion and pollution and 
deepening inequality in urban mobility. Given the 
increasing numbers of fatalities from road crashes and the 
disproportionately greater impact on the vulnerable groups 
India’s road safety challenges typically relate to poor 
enforcement of traffic rules, poor road design and 
engineering, and delayed trauma care (Verma et al. 2021). 
Indian cities see a wide range of motorized and non-
motorized vehicles moving at different speeds on urban 
streets and often coming into conflict with each other. A 
survey on urban mobility in India revealed walking to be 
the preferred mode of travel for daily trips for 63 percent 
of respondents, while only 6 percent of respondents used 
cycling as a mode of travel (Soman et al. 2019). Between 
2016 and 2020, more than 30 percent of fatalities in 
Indian streets occurred at junctions (Ministry of Road 
Transport and Highways 2022) that had poor geometric 
layout, inadequate channelization of flows for different 
kinds of vehicles, high speeds, and inadequate pedestrian 
infrastructure. Despite this sobering reality, roads—the 
quintessential infrastructure for mobility and a symbol 
of development—are primarily planned and designed for 
motorized transport, particularly for private automobiles, 
as part of the program for the modernization of Indian 
cities ( Joshi 2023).
Globally, the rise of automobiles from the middle of the 
twentieth century had seen cities changing the planning 
paradigms by cementing the domination of cars in 
road infrastructure and optimizing streets for smoother 
movement of cars at the expense of pedestrians, cyclists, 
and transit users (Golub 2015). Emerging problems 
such as traffic jams were seen as a technical issue and 
engineering solutions were sought to increase road 
capacity to improve traffic flow. Similarly, speed, which 
was considered dangerous for pedestrians, was seen as a 
problem solvable only through harsher punishments for 
reckless driving and road design that restricted pedestrians 
to the sidewalk and at crossings (Norton 2015).
Many of these are standard policies even today, including 
in India. Historically, transport engineering was 
dominated by civil engineers who viewed roads primarily 
as physical assets with finite capacity, focusing on their 
distribution among different kinds of vehicles and 
narrowly emphasizing the right of way or the road reserve 
(Karndacharuk et al. 2014). This perspective treated streets 
as utilitarian conduits for movement, prioritizing vehicle 
flow over other considerations.
In contrast, the emerging field of urban design reimagined 
streets as public spaces—multi-dimensional environments 
that cater to a wide array of social, economic, and cultural 
activities—and therefore a prime subject of place-making 
to create more livable, walkable cities that cater to the 
needs of diverse users. Thinkers like Jane Jacobs, Jan Gehl, 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 21

for whom walking is the main way of getting around the 
city, the Government of India recognized road safety as 
a public health crisis and brought out the National Road 
Safety Policy in 2010 (Ministry of Road Transport and 
Highways 2010).
However, unlike building bylaws that allow municipal 
authorities to monitor compliance and hold violators 
accountable, there are no legally binding regulations for 
designing streets and public spaces to support pedestrians 
and cyclists. This absence of regulations makes it 
challenging to implement safe-street designs and develop 
walking and cycling infrastructure at scale in Indian cities. 
This has also led different cities in various states to develop 
their own guidelines. For instance, Mumbai developed 
the Universal Footpath Policy in 2023 (Ch. E. [Roads & 
Traffic] 2023) by building on the 2001 manual Planning 
and Design Guidelines for Street Furniture for Mumbai and 
Its Suburbs Part 1 (Surve and Surve 2001). Delhi adopted 
the Unified Traffic and Transportation Infrastructure 
(Planning and Engineering) Centre pedestrian design 
guidelines in 2009, while Bangalore civil agencies have 
followed a revised version of the Tender SURE guidelines 
since 2011. At first Tender Sure was implemented only 
for a select number of streets but Bruhat Bengaluru 
Mahanagara Palike found it difficult to implement the 
full Tender Sure suggestions, including unifying all 
utilities under footpaths in a single duct in all streets. 
They therefore adopted what has been termed “Tender 
Sure Lite.” This only retains the surface-level guidelines 
from Tender Sure, which are being implemented in new 
street improvements. In 2020, the Delhi Urban Arts 
Commission introduced another set of Street Design 
Guidelines, embracing the principles of Complete Streets 
and contextualizing them for Delhi (Delhi Urban Arts 
Commission 2020). In Tamil Nadu, cities like Chennai 
and Coimbatore adopted non-motorized transport 
(NMT) policies in 2014 and 2017, respectively, to 
ensure the provision of high-quality walking and cycling 
infrastructure. Similarly, Pune adopted a pedestrian policy 
in 2016 to promote walking.
Road safety for pedestrians and cyclists has become the 
central focus of the various city-level guidance documents. 
Two guidelines that are often cited by cities include the 
2013 Footpath Design guidelines by the Institute for 
Transportation and Development Policy (ITDP) based 
on the Indian Roads Congress (IRC) Guidelines for 
Pedestrian Facilities (IRC 2012), and the Harmonized 
Guidelines and Standard for Universal Accessibility 
in India (Raheja et al. 2022) released by the Ministry 
of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) working 
with IIT Roorkee for the sensitization and guidance of 
diverse stakeholders responsible for designing, planning, 
and implementing accessibility in built environments. 
Unfortunately, as none of the guidelines discussed above 
are statutory, they are difficult to enforce.
Despite these efforts, little is known about how Indian 
cities have attempted to make streets safer for people. 
Other than how to design safe streets, for which many 
examples exist even in cities in India, what processes 
are followed to move from a design concept to 
implementation? What kind of data and evidence supports 
decision-making and helps secure stakeholder buy-in? 
Who is responsible for making streets safer for people? 
How are the capacities of these authorities built to deliver 
safe streets? Are one-off initiatives the norm, or are Indian 
cities successfully scaling safe-street interventions across 
the city? And, if so, what is enabling this shift to happen?
22 I wri-india.org

PURPOSE OF THE REPORT
This report aims to enhance organization and ecosystem-
wide learning by reflecting on the questions posed above 
using the case of Mumbai. The city has been engaged 
in improving road safety and developing pedestrian 
infrastructure using the Safe System approach (promoted 
by the UN General Assembly in the Global Plan for 
the Decade of Action for Road Safety 2011–2020; see 
“Coherence with external and internal environments: 
International Opportunities and Commitments”) 
underpinning different initiatives over a decade to deliver 
safe streets at scale.
This report is one of the first evaluations of its kind. It 
seeks to provide valuable insights into the evolving nature 
of road safety in Mumbai that are critical for scaling up 
walkable infrastructure in Indian cities and achieving 
systemic changes in how we plan and design infrastructure 
in the city. It highlights the practices that enable a shift 
from a purely civil-engineering-led approach to a more 
urban-design-led one to address the more significant 
issues connected to safer streets and reimagine streets 
as public spaces. It will also spotlight the challenges 
that remain and are yet to be overcome, such as those 
stemming from the city’s complex political economy and 
the institutional arrangements that underpin different 
change pathways. The report will be valuable for planning 
and design professionals engaging with municipal actors 
and key stakeholders for street improvements and city 
transformation.
OBJECTIVES
The main objective is to conduct a formative evaluation 
of pedestrian-focused road safety initiatives undertaken 
in the last decade by Mumbai in partnership with WRI 
India and other partners supported by the Bloomberg 
Initiative for Global Road Safety (BIGRS). These 
collaborative initiatives broadly aimed to create safer, 
inclusive, accessible, and more resilient streets at scale 
across the city as key sustainability transitions to fulfill the 
Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). This formative 
evaluation of a decade of road safety initiatives promoting 
safer streets through an urban design approach in 
Mumbai is in response to the increasing calls for learning 
through evaluations of implemented programs within 
WRI’s Strategic Plan (2023–27) and in the literature for 
sustainability transition experiments and initiatives that 
recommend using comprehensive formative approaches 
(Luederitz et al. 2017).
A formative evaluation supports learning through a process 
of reflexive monitoring and evaluation across actors seeking 
to contribute to sustainable development by working on 
system innovation and deepen understanding of complex 
socio-technical system change that connects ecological, 
social, and technical domains (Molas-Gallart et al. 2021). 
The safer streets efforts in Mumbai over 10 years worked 
at different systemic levels, leveraging policies, plans, 
budgets, legal frameworks, and city-making and urban 
transformation agendas to promote pedestrian safety at 
scale through innovative pathways of change coordinating 
with a wide range of stakeholders. The complexity and 
ongoing nature of the work means it lends itself well 
to this kind of evaluation to strengthen organizational 
learning, accountability, and program evolution. 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 23

SCOPE
Three different approaches 
The formative evaluation of safer streets is focused on 
one city—Mumbai—and looks at more than a decade’s 
work from 2011 till mid-2024. Transforming even a small 
subset of Mumbai’s streets into safe walking environments 
involves work at many levels. Three main approaches with 
different processes were identified by mapping the state of 
play and discussions with key stakeholders and the WRI 
India team working on road safety and promoting safer 
streets. These are as follows:
 ▪ Systemic engagements to trigger a transformational 
change in urban mobility imagination
 ▪ Programs for street transformation
 ▪ Design interventions for street improvements as proof 
of concept from a pedestrian safety lens
The three approaches overlapped in time, often working 
in tandem, reinforcing each other, and creating new 
opportunities through continued engagement at the 
local level but with broader goals of impacting policy and 
practice (see Figure 2).
The conceptualization of systems transitions as an 
interaction between three levels—landscape, regime, 
and niche (Rip and Kemp 1998; Geels 2002; Grin et 
al. 2011)—was adopted to understand the processes 
underpinning the three approaches within the evaluation’s 
scope. Table 1 explains the three levels and their relevance 
to the approaches.
This framing of systems transition informs the 
Theory of Change and frames the discussions on the 
challenges and lessons learned from the evaluation (see 
“Lessons Learned”).

TABLE 1  |   The three-part scope of the evaluation and how they relate to systems transition ideas
   LEVELS OF SYSTEMS TRANSITIONS   
  (MOLAS-GALLART ET AL. 2021)
APPROACHES IN THE SCOPE OF THE EVALUATION
      
Niches:  protective spaces where different ideas, models,  
configurations, and alternative processes are developed and            
 strive for uptake by the regime (broadly the government). 
 
Design Processes: Locally specific design experiments in specific streets using 
data-based design and tactical trials as basis for scale up and mainstreaming, driven 
by competent non-governmental organizations with domain expertise, such as WRI 
India, among others, and implemented by the municipal corporation.
 
Regime: Work with different principles and technologies 
that shape prevalent government models, have relationships 
with multiple stakeholders, and have the power to privilege 
different sources of knowledge and adopt alternative  
ways of doing. 
 
Programmatic interventions:  A set of projects and activities that work together 
towards a common goal are aggregated under a program of interventions for scaling  
and mainstreaming ideas to bring about desired change through the system. In this case,  
WRI India’s programs targeting safer access to schools and mainstreaming urban design 
as the main tool for delivering on the promise of safe streets as pedestrian-friendly  
public spaces will fall under this scope.
Landscape: Regimes are constantly exposed to external 
pressures—powerful lobbies and long-term economic, 
social, cultural, or environmental trends that comprise the 
landscape. Windows of opportunity for niches open up for 
influencing systems transitions when the pressure of the 
landscape on the regime is strong enough to disrupt existing 
models and practices to drive a system change. 
Systemic engagements: Any change in policies or modus operandi of city-making 
processes requires deep policy engagements at various levels to align different interests 
with similar objectives, fill gaps in the process through better articulation of the demand 
side, and champion tested solutions and innovations for policy experimentations. In this 
case, the evaluation will focus on the role played by WRI India in engaging at different 
systemic levels to iteratively work on improving road safety in Mumbai by focusing on 
pedestrian safety through street design and scaling innovations through the system.
Source: WRI India.

EVALUATION CRITERIA
To ensure a high-quality evaluation of implemented safe-
street interventions, selected criteria from the Organisation 
for Economic Co-operation and Development’s evaluation 
criteria (OECD 2021) have been used to guide the 
evaluation. The selected criteria include:
 ▪ Relevance: Are the interventions doing the right thing?
 ▪ Coherence: How well do the interventions fit with 
external and internal opportunities?
 ▪ Effectiveness: Are the interventions achieving the 
stated objectives?
 ▪ Sustainability: Will the benefits last?
The criterion of efficiency has been intentionally excluded 
as the purpose of the formative evaluation was not to 
understand organizational efficiency in achieving results 
and outcomes. Instead, the focus is on understanding the 
relevance, coherence, effectiveness, and sustainability of the 
diverse processes involved in identifying and capitalizing 
on opportunities in the landscape to implement 
and scale niche ideas through the regime and drive 
transformative change.
This learning-focused formative evaluation has three 
phases with clear objectives for each phase. The phases and 
their objectives are as follows:
Research phase
 (for documenting the status quo) 
The objectives of this initial phase were to identify
 ▪ the main challenges to urban mobility in Mumbai and 
the issues related to pedestrian safety;
 ▪ the institutions/rules of the game that define the state 
of play on improving streets for people in Mumbai;
 ▪ the stakeholders involved in making streets safer by 
design in Mumbai; and
 ▪ the most prevalent pathways of change adopted by 
the government.
Evaluation phase 
The main objective of this phase was to assess the 
relevance, coherence, effectiveness, and sustainability 
of identified approaches to improving road safety and 
creating safe walking environments in Mumbai, with a 
particular focus on WRI India’s role.
Learning phase (to synthesize the lessons from the 
three approaches)
This phase had one key objective, to discuss key lessons 
learned from the work on safe streets for pedestrians in 
Mumbai to understand the effectiveness of the current 
approaches for city transformation.
The evidence generated by this evaluation will inform 
adjustments needed to enhance the ongoing efforts and 
strategic approaches of WRI India and its partners to drive 
transformative change in cities by improving walkability 
and road safety.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 27

RESEARCH QUESTIONS
The research questions are grouped according to 
the phases and objectives of the learning-focused 
formative evaluation.
Research phase
What are the main challenges to urban mobility 
and pedestrian safety in Mumbai and how are 
solutions framed? 
 ▪ What are the biggest challenges to pedestrians and why 
do they happen?
 ▪ What are the main drivers of street improvements 
and city transformations leading to pedestrian 
infrastructure?
 ▪ Which actors and stakeholders are instrumental in 
making streets safe in Mumbai?
 ▪ What are the current pathways to street improvements 
that lead to safer streets at scale and what are 
the limitations?
 ▪ What are the different approaches for creating safer 
streets for pedestrians through citywide action by urban 
government institutions?
Evaluation Phase
What were some of the results achieved?
 ▪ How relevant and coherent were the approaches used?
 ▪ How effective were the results in making streets safer?
 ▪ What were some of the unintended consequences of 
the implemented projects?
 ▪ How sustainable were the results?
Learning phase
What are the lessons learned?
 ▪ What lessons can be learned to mainstream 
citywide action on street transformation across large 
cities in India?
 ▪ What are the gaps and challenges that remain?
METHODOLOGY
A pragmatic worldview shapes the methodology 
of formative evaluation (Murphy and Rorty 1990; 
Cherryholmes 1992; Patton 2015). This methodology 
is concerned with applications—what works based 
on actions, situations, and consequences, and using all 
approaches available to understand the problem and the 
solutions to the problem. Using a longitudinal case study as 
the primary method—an approach considered suitable for 
the evaluation of programs, events, activities, and processes 
to solve a problem (Creswell and Creswell 2023)—this 
evaluation employs mixed methods incorporating 
qualitative and quantitative data collection techniques. 
A case study is a method for learning about a complex 
intervention based on a comprehensive understanding 
of what is happening and why (Yin 2018). Given the 
interest in understanding complex systemic issues that 
contribute to shaping processes and solutions for safer 
streets in Mumbai, the case study approach enables the 
evaluation phases to 
 ▪ capture the complexity of associated systems, including 
relevant changes over time in the specific directions, 
through detailed narratives;
 ▪ attend fully to contextual conditions that enable or 
challenge innovation, and new pathways of change 
within the political economy and cultural preferences of 
the context; and
 ▪ explain how various approaches outlined in the scope 
contribute to systemic change for city transformation 
by creating safer, more walkable streets.
A comparative case study of two or more cases 
produces generalizable knowledge about how or why 
a specific program or policy works (Goodrick 2014). 
As street improvement work for pedestrian safety 
in Mumbai involves a range of approaches, policies, 
and programs, a comparative case study approach 
will help examine the many smaller nested cases 
across interventions to identify common and unique 
factors that contribute to results and outcomes. 
28 I wri-india.org

Methods for data collection
To develop a fuller understanding of street transformations 
in Mumbai (how changes happened, whether they were in 
line with desired outcomes, and unintended consequences), 
the report uses multiple sources of evidence pertaining to 
the three main types of interventions and the processes 
underpinning them as outlined in the scope. It will build 
on existing research and primary and secondary data 
analysis. As formative evaluations are, by design, flexible 
and adaptable in selecting different methods based on the 
context and nature of interventions, a variety of methods 
were used.
Document review
An extensive review of road safety and street improvement 
initiatives was undertaken utilizing WRI India’s program 
planning documents related to road safety, along with 
evidence generated by development partners, academics, 
and civil society organizations. This review helped frame 
the challenges of pedestrian safety in Mumbai, document 
the state of play over the last decade, and identify different 
approaches to improving streets for pedestrians. In 
addition, existing policies, regulations, bylaws, Mumbai’s 
city development and city mobility plans, and media stories 
were examined to comprehend the broader ecosystem 
for framing solutions, the goals and drivers of different 
initiatives and pathways, and the public perception of 
street improvements in Mumbai.
Mapping stakeholders
As diverse stakeholders representing private and 
public interests are involved in making streets safe 
for pedestrians, an initial mapping was conducted to 
identify important stakeholders and key informants 
for interviews. The list included technical staff and 
administrators from the BMC, traffic police, corporators/
elected representatives for Mumbai wards, members 
of high-level committees associated with street 
improvements, academics, urban designers, journalists/
media professionals, and activist architects working 
on claiming streets as public spaces for people.
Key Informant Interviews (KIIs) 
Semi-structured and structured KIIs were conducted with 
various key informants to understand the relevance, results, 
and impact of diverse efforts to create safer streets in 
Mumbai (see Appendix A for a full list of key informants 
interviewed). Selected key informants ranged from high-
level decision-makers and public administrators in the 
municipal government to junior engineers responsible for 
street design, as well as elected representatives, civil society 
representatives, and private-sector professionals involved 
in street-design projects. High-level decision-makers 
provided insights into the policy landscape, its impact, 
institutional arrangements, and budget allocations. At 
the same time, municipal government staff and engineers 
illuminated the nuances and challenges of on-the-
ground implementation of street transformation projects. 
Elected representatives provided perspectives on their 
engagement with marginalized groups, addressing long-
standing grievances and political support for safer streets. 
Discussions with designers and activists focused on their 
role in the design and execution of safer streets, shedding 
light on their limited representation in the overall 
decision-making process.
Nested case studies
Ten key street design projects, including seven 
intersections and three corridors, along with three citywide 
programs and systemic engagements, were documented 
and analyzed as nested case studies using both primary and 
secondary data sources. The selected project sites represent 
prime locations surrounded by mixed land and building 
use and a high volume of pedestrian and vehicular traffic. 
Each case study analyzed the process of implementing the 
intervention from the point of receiving a request from a 
government agency to its rationale, stakeholders engaged, 
challenges encountered and overcome, the outcomes 
achieved, and the unintended consequences. In addition, 
three case studies were developed on programmatic 
interventions and one on systemic engagements that 
focused on scaling design-led safe-street programming 
across the city.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 29

Crash data 
City-wide crash, fatality, and injury data were obtained 
from the Mumbai Traffic Police (MTP) for each year from 
2018 to 2022. Crashes, fatalities, and injuries for specific 
sites were extracted from the data for seven intersections 
and three corridors, including the three intersections and 
two corridors where physical changes were mapped. This 
was used to measure the impact on crashes and injuries by 
comparing pre- and post-implementation incidence data.
Lives saved estimates
WRI annually presents the Lives Saved Report (World 
Resources Institute 2023) to showcase the progress of 
monitoring and evaluation efforts from WRI’s work on 
safer streets in 16 cities, including Mumbai, which are part 
of the BIGRS. This internal report estimates the number 
of lives saved by 2040 as a direct result of specific road 
safety interventions based on several variables, including 
site-level historical crash data, citywide collision trends, 
baseline traffic counts in each city, and the type of new 
road safety infrastructure built because of  WRI’s support.
(See Appendix F for a detailed note on the methodology 
and sources supporting the calculation of the lives saved 
estimates.)
Mapping physical changes 
Physical changes were mapped at three intersections and 
two junctions where the BMC had implemented the 
designs proposed by WRI India to make streets safer 
for pedestrians. The criteria for selecting sites involved 
considering different types of intersections and corridors 
where improvements had been scheduled to be undertaken, 
availability of data on vehicular and pedestrian volumes at 
the sites, and documentation of physical conditions before 
the onsite implementation. The post-implementation 
mapping reviewed the physical changes brought about by 
traffic engineering and urban design measures like reduced 
crossing distance, reclaiming carriageway space as refuge 
areas or as public spaces, and the redistribution of street 
space in general to prioritize walking.
Mapping pedestrian and motorist 
behaviors in improved streets 
The behavior of pedestrians and vehicles at the same 
three intersections and two corridors (where physical 
changes were documented) was mapped to understand 
the fit with the design intent, ease of use, and challenges. 
Three architecture student interns from the Rachana 
Sansad Academy of Architecture were trained to conduct 
behavior mapping at street intersections, with one student 
responsible for one intersection observing each arm for 
one signal cycle up to 50m along each arm. The observers 
were positioned at curb extensions along each arm 
and mapped the movement of one pedestrian over one 
signal cycle on a spatial map. For mapping behaviors on 
corridors, each corridor was divided into approximately 
three equal segments between intersections, and each 
observer was assigned one segment for mapping pedestrian 
movement. Three observers mapped at least 150 pedestrian 
movement patterns in each intersection and in two 
corridors on regular weekdays. In this way, the movement 
patterns of 761 pedestrians were mapped across five sites. 
Similarly, the three observers were positioned at the three 
intersections rotating between the arms of the intersection 
with signal cycles, documenting vehicular behavior to 
understand compliance with the stop signal only, the stop 
line only, and both together, for all vehicles waiting at the 
stop sign light. They repeated the exercise at the signalized 
intersection in each corridor segment assigned to them. 
The behaviors of 767 motorists were documented in this 
way. The purpose of this method was not to understand the 
movement pattern of every pedestrian or the compliance 
with rules by every motorist (only the foremost ones 
waiting at the stoplight) but to understand a sample of 
behaviors that the observers could capture.
Perception survey 
Data was collected from street users at the same three 
junctions and two corridors where physical changes and 
behaviors were mapped using an intercept survey to 
understand user perceptions about safety, accessibility, 
and ease of movement. The perception surveys were 
conducted by the same three architecture students, with a 
total sample size of 768 participants (minimum 150 per 
location). Surveys were conducted from 9 a.m. to 10 p.m. 
on weekdays and weekends in October, November, and 
December 2022.
See Table 2 for methods used in each of the 
14 nested cases.

Analytic framework(s)
The research questions provided the main analytic 
framework across all phases of the study. However, 
the approach to analysis in each phase differed, as 
captured in Table 3. 
As the main purpose of the research phase was to under-
stand the challenges of urban mobility and pedestrian 
safety in Mumbai, the ecosystem, and prevalent processes 
for framing solutions, a political economy analysis was 
considered the appropriate approach to analysis due to its 
focus on analyzing institutional arrangements; rules of the 
game or the landscape of policies, laws, rules (formal and 
informal); roles of stakeholders and actors; and pathways 
of change for policy implementation. The findings of 
the research phase directly contributed to evaluating the 
relevance of the three approaches.

A.  Political economy analysis (PEA) 
The PEA analysis we conducted in the research phase is 
structured around three key questions: 
What is the problem?
 ▪ We frame this as the safety issues related to walking 
in the streets of Mumbai.
Why does it happen?
 ▪ Foundational factors: the range of contextual factors 
that shape the problem
 ▪ Institutions/rules of the game: the written rules or 
regulations, or the
 ▪ “unwritten rules,” social norms, and practical 
guidelines that seek to improve
 ▪ road safety and create pedestrian-friendly 
infrastructure
 ▪ Actors: the roles and motivations of key actors and 
how these shape safer streets
How can it be addressed?
 ▪ Pathways of change: analyzing current practices 
of the municipal government–led efforts of street 
improvements as the most significant pathway for 
bringing about safer streets in Mumbai at scale
For the formative evaluation phase, a reflexive Theory of 
Change (ToC) was constructed and subsequently refined 
to articulate the expected process through which three 
different approaches are working toward the intended 
change. The ToC pathways across the three approaches 
are not causal as the contexts, activities, and outcomes 
are co-evolving (Molas- Gallart et al. 2021) and work 
cumulatively on outcomes.
B. Theory of Change (ToC)
The Theory of Change was constructed to capture 
the modus operandi of the three approaches from the 
perspective of WRI India’s contribution to the overall 
vision (see Figure 3). The approaches had individual goals/
outcomes to which diverse efforts by WRI and other 
stakeholders contributed. However, they were all part of 
an overarching vision of increasing the focus on the design 
aspects of safer streets for pedestrians through citywide 
action by the government on transforming streets. This 
vision, in turn, is aligned with WRI’s impact targets in its 
current strategy plan and the relevant SDGs.
The ToC of this formative evaluation is a working model 
created by the authors to evaluate the processes and results 
of ongoing interventions in Mumbai that started in 2011 
to achieve transformative outcomes on road safety and 
pedestrian access to streets. The ToC is guided by SDG 
impact targets and the impact targets in WRI’s strategic 
plan. The ToC does not necessarily establish a causal 
model of interventions through a “chain of assumptions 
explaining how activities lead step by step to the expected 
outcomes” (Weiss 1998, 57). It is difficult to establish 
causal chains in a ToC for complex urban environments 
such as Mumbai streets, especially when no street saw the 
implementation of 100 percent of the design interventions. 
Instead, it builds on the multi-level perspective on socio-
technical transitions—landscape, regime, and niche 
(Rip and Kemp 1998; Geels 2002)—to show probable 
pathways of change. In this framing, experimental policy 
engagements can support transformative outcomes, (Schot 
et al. 2019; Ghosh et al. 2021) which in this case include
 ▪ building and nurturing niches to shape alternative 
processes through experimental pilot projects;
 ▪ expanding and mainstreaming niches into the wider 
world by scaling pilot projects through programs and 
systemic engagements; and
 ▪ opening up and unlocking regimes to drive 
actionable policies and inform budgetary decisions 
on citywide design-led safer-street transformations. 
Together, these three types of interventions provided 
a direction of transformative outcomes that aimed to 
deliver on global impact targets on improving road safety 
and public health (SDG 3.6, and WRI impact target on 
implementing safer streets) and expanding safe access to 
transportation for vulnerable groups (SDG 11.2).

C.  Data analysis for the evaluation phase
An integrative analysis of different kinds of data involved 
within-case analysis for each of the three main types of 
interventions, and a cross-case analysis across the three 
types of interventions was undertaken to answer the 
evaluation questions. The data analysis was done by
 ▪ coding and clustering KII data and data from surveys 
and observational mapping studies in line with the 
research questions;
 ▪ preliminary analysis of the findings from primary 
data;
 ▪ identifying themes and sub-themes within and across 
the different categories of projects;
 ▪ creation of graphic data displays, including tables and 
timelines;
 ▪ triangulation of preliminary findings with findings 
from secondary data; and
 ▪ drafting of findings on processes, outcomes, and 
impact and sharpening of analysis about contextual 
conditions and assumptions.
D.  Systems change framework for 
developing insights during the 
learning phase
In the learning phase, the analysis uses an inductive 
perspective to find new insights from the evaluation 
based on systems thinking to help shape the report’s 
recommendations. We use Donella Meadows’ Leverage 
Points: Places to Intervene in a System (Meadows 
1999) as the analytical framework to understand how 
the approaches have engaged with change processes as 
interventions in complex systems (Batty et al. 2006; Bai 
et al. 2016) to achieve intended outcomes, and scale up, 
mainstream, and integrate road safety and citywide safe 
walking environments in larger urban transformation 
projects. Table 4 explains the framework.
QUALITY 
CONTROL AND ETHICS
The research and evaluation apply a rigorous 
methodological approach to ensure that the results, 
conclusions, and recommendations are grounded in 
objective, reliable, and valid data and thorough analysis. 
Furthermore, the evaluation used triangulation; that is 
using different data sources and collection techniques to 
gather different perspectives and test the consistency of 
information. In the absence of triangulation options, any 
findings that come exclusively from a particular source 
or group of respondents are clearly indicated. Particular 
attention is paid to avoiding harm and stress to research 
and evaluation participants. Informed consent was 
obtained before conducting surveys and KIIs, their privacy 
was protected, and no compensation was offered. All data 
collected from human subjects was kept confidential and 
anonymized before analysis. The research was exempted 
from a Human Subject Protection review by WRI India 
after a review of the methodology; the research tools 
do not collect any personal information or ask sensitive 
questions and only collect perception data on street safety 
or factual data on processes and solution framing.
LIMITATIONS
The biggest limitation for conducting a formative 
evaluation of safe-street interventions stems from 
the limited availability of consistent data on crashes, 
injuries, and fatalities over a fixed period before and 
after the interventions across the selected sites. Given 
the difficulties arising from partial implementation of 
design interventions, and their completion only toward 
the end of the 2018–2022 period, attribution of changes 
in crash and fatality data to implemented design features 
is particularly tricky. This makes it difficult to carry out 
any statistical analysis whose results could be considered 
definitive and even the average incidences pre- and post-
implementation are difficult to establish. However, visible 
patterns in the data are discussed and elaborated upon in 
the “Effectiveness” chapter. 
Second, the lack of systematic documentation of 
pedestrian and motorist behaviors before and after the 
implementation further compromises the ability to 
speak to the impact of these interventions. In the case 
of this evaluation, behavior mapping of pedestrians and 
motorists at the selected sample sites is used to illustrate 
their respective compliance with the improved design 
36 I wri-india.org

and traffic rules as a proxy measure of the effectiveness of 
interventions on road-user behavior (see “Behavior changes 
and impact on traffic management: Pedestrian behavior 
mapping and Motorist behavior mapping ”).
Third, in cases where maps from secondary sources were 
unavailable or authors lacked adequate data to generate 
primary maps, they supplemented them with photographic 
evidence. While maps are helpful tools for communicating 
scales or providing a broad overview, their limitations 
become apparent when dealing with smaller spatial 
units such as intersections and corridors, which were the 
primary sites of design interventions. Here, photographic 
data better illustrates the nature and effectiveness of the 
interventions. 
Fourth, few of the key informants identified through 
stakeholder mapping, particularly engineers and municipal 
government staff, were open to discussing the interventions 
undertaken by incumbent administrations owing to 
ongoing political changes at the state and local government 
levels. Some BMC staff and engineers refrained from 
engaging in a formal critical discussion on interventions 
undertaken by the municipal body or key high level 
decision-makers within local and state governments. In 
such cases, inputs and insights were gathered through 
informal interactions and engagements during ongoing 
safe-street projects. In addition, to overcome these 
challenges arising from organizational dynamics, the list of 
key informants was adjusted to include retired officials and 
others knowledgeable about the projects and processes.

CHAPTER 2.
  
Context
This chapter addresses the urban mobility challenges 
in Mumbai, focusing on pedestrian safety from a 
political economy perspective and critiques the 
limited traffic and civil engineering approach to road 
improvements that contribute to these challenges. 
Pedestrians make up 50 percent of road accident 
fatalities, yet walking remains the most common 
mode of transport. Despite being public transport-
friendly, factors like induced demand, free parking, 
and prioritizing large infrastructure projects over 
pedestrian safety worsen the situation, especially for 
the urban poor. 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 39

This chapter frames the urban mobility challenges of 
Mumbai by using a political economy analysis framework 
that asks the following questions
What is the problem
? 
Why does it happen?
How can it be addressed?
THE PROBLEM: A CITY 
UNSAFE FOR WALKING
Mumbai, the second largest city in India with a population 
of 12.44 million (Office of the Registrar General & 
Census Commissioner, India 2011), is the capital of the 
western state of Maharashtra. It is one of the densest 
cities in the world and a major economic center attracting 
large numbers of people from across the state and country. 
Mumbai is believed to be the most public-transport-
friendly city in India, where about 80 percent of all 
motorized trips are undertaken on public rail and bus 
services (Moneycontrol News 2023). More than half of 
these trips are undertaken on the suburban rail system, 
which is the city’s lifeline (World Bank 2015). However, 
the city’s first mobility plan showed that walking is still the 
most prevalent mode, with 51 percent of Mumbai residents 
walking to fulfill their everyday mobility needs within the 
city (LEA Associates South Asia 2016). Nevertheless, safe 
walking environments in Mumbai’s streets remain mostly 
unrealized (see Figure 4).
According to data from the Mumbai Traffic Police, 
pedestrians account for 50 percent of road accident 
fatalities in Mumbai over the last five years, including 
2022 when 272 people lost their lives in road crashes 
in Mumbai, of which 50 percent, or 136 people, were 
pedestrians (Sood 2023). In 2019, road-crash-related 
deaths occurred at the rate of 3.5 deaths per 100,000 
population in 2019, accounting for 447 fatalities, of which 
47 percent, or 210, were pedestrians (Bloomberg Initiative 
for Global Road Safety 2019). This was followed by 
motorcycle riders (both drivers and pillion riders) at 41 
percent. Overall, 90 percent of all road deaths in Mumbai 
involved vulnerable road users—pedestrians, motorcyclists, 
40 I wri-india.org
FIGURE 4  |   Lack of safe walking environments on congested streets in Mumbai
Photo Credit:  Santiago Fernandez Reyes.

and cyclists. The disaggregation of fatalities due to 
road crashes for different kinds of road users in 2019 is 
shown in Figure 5. 
Men accounted for 80 percent of total road traffic deaths, 
and among this group men aged 20–29 years were at 
highest risk with one hundred dying on Mumbai’s streets 
in 2019, 12 of them pedestrians. Injuries also followed a 
similar pattern with 620 men aged 20–29 being injured, 
much higher than in other age groups (Bloomberg 
Initiative for Global Road Safety 2019). Similarly, men 
accounted for 74 percent of total pedestrian fatalities. 
However, of these 156 male pedestrian fatalities, older 
men were found to be at much higher risk compared to 
the 20–29 age-group with 30–39-year-olds, 40–49-year-
olds, and 50–59-year-olds accounting for 22 percent, 
20 percent, 21 percent, respectively. Overall, 90 percent 
of all road deaths in Mumbai involved vulnerable road 
users—pedestrians, motorcyclists, and cyclists—with older 
pedestrians being at greater risk relative to overall numbers.
A 2005 World Bank study showed that poorer households 
in Mumbai, regardless of location, made fewer vehicle trips 
overall compared to wealthier residents and relied heavily 
on walking (61 percent). This was evident in both work 
and non-work commutes. Shorter journeys dominated 
travel patterns, with 40 percent of workers (half from 
poor households) commuting less than 2 kilometers, 
while only 11 percent of poor workers, compared to 19 
percent overall, traveled over 10 kilometers (Baker et al. 
2005). The average commute distance of 3.9 kilometers 
for the poor and 5.3 kilometers for everyone else reflects 
the reliance of poorer households on walking and non-
motorized transport (NMT), highlighting the significant 
impact of income on transportation choices within the 
city (see Figure 6). Despite this, walking infrastructure in 
Mumbai’s streets gets little policy attention or investment, 
making the city unsafe for walking, especially for the most 
vulnerable. The problem can be summarized as follows: 
Despite most trips in Mumbai being undertaken by 
walking, there is a significant lack of safe pedestrian 
infrastructure across the city. The poor, a majority 
of whom walk, are at a disproportionately higher 
risk of road crash injuries and fatalities. This makes 
road safety in Mumbai an important equity issue.

WHY DOES THE 
PROBLEM PERSIST?
Foundational factors
A. Geography
The city was born by connecting seven marshy islands 
through land reclamation to form the Island City, which 
at its widest is still only a narrow 3.5-kilometer peninsula. 
This reclamation project was undertaken during colonial 
times, when the islands passed from the Portuguese 
to the British, and was eventually completed in 1845. 
It transformed the erstwhile Bombay (the city’s name 
changed to Mumbai in 1995) into a major seaport on the 
Arabian Sea. The confined geography of the Island City 
puts a very high premium on livable space and restricts the 
city’s ability to adequately expand the road network.
B. Urbanization
Later extensions into the mainland created suburban 
Mumbai. The Island City, or South Mumbai, and its 
suburbs form two different districts—Mumbai City 
District and Mumbai Suburban District—and together 
they form Greater Mumbai (see Figure 7). Most of the 
resident population lives in the suburbs connected by 
suburban rail. As Mumbai’s exploding population had 
poor access to planned infrastructure, it necessitated the 
creation of the Mumbai Metropolitan Region (MMR) 
to direct growth away from the core city. This allowed the 
Island City of 157 square kilometers (km
2) to grow into 
the modern-day sprawling MMR of 6,355 km2 (Asian 
Development Bank 2021). The planning area of MMR 
encompasses five districts: Mumbai District, Mumbai 
Suburban District, and parts of Thane, Raigad, and Palghar 
Districts. MMR comprises eight municipal corporations, 
nine municipal councils, 17 town–municipal urban centers, 
and over 900 villages.
Through the two phases of the Mumbai Urban Transport 
Project, the World Bank supported Mumbai in planning, 
expanding, and modernizing the suburban railway system. 
This was done to enable people living in the suburbs and 
surrounding districts to commute more easily to central 
Mumbai, where many jobs are concentrated. The high-
profile Mumbai Urban Infrastructure Project (MUIP) 
launched in 2003 (MMRDA 2023) aimed to improve the 
road network across the MMR but focused heavily on 
elevated roads and flyovers, not pedestrian infrastructure.
C. Inadequate spatial allocation for roads, 
leading to congestion
The Revised Draft Development Plan 2034 (RDDP), 
also known as the Development Control and Promotion 
Regulations 2034, recognized that Indian cities allocate 
very little space to roads, with only 8.16 percent of 
Mumbai’s total area and around 14 percent of its developed 
area given to roads (Government of Maharashtra 2017).
This is significantly lower than global benchmarks for 
car-dependent cities, where 35–50 percent of urban land 
allocated to roads (Guerra et al. 2025). In contrast, cities 
with high public transport usage, such as Hong Kong, 
Singapore, Shanghai, and Tokyo, have demonstrated that 
efficient urban mobility does not require excessive road 
space. Hong Kong, for example, dedicates around 12 
percent of its land to roads, yet achieves one of the world’s 
highest public transport usage rates with 90 percent 
of daily passenger trips made through different public 
transport services (Transport and Housing Bureau 2017). 
Similarly, Singapore allocates 12 percent of its land to 
roads, but 60 percent of daily passenger trips in Singapore 
are made through public transport, while 36 percent 
are made through car-based modes (Land Transport 
Authority 2008).
Even though the number of vehicles on Mumbai’s roads 
more than doubled during the decade 2009–2019, road 
length remained the same at around 2,000 kilometers 
(The Times of India 2019). Despite having a good 
public transport network and investing in rail and road 
infrastructure, Mumbai was the sixth most congested 
metropolitan city in the world in 2022 (TomTom 
International B.V. 2022). Traffic jams cost Mumbai labor-
hour losses; emission-related air pollution adds to health 
costs, and crashes have many additional associated costs. 
Together, these were estimated to cost INR 420.60 crore 
(US$4.8 billion) per year by the Boston Consulting Group 
in a study commissioned by Uber (Chin et al. 2018).
Mumbai’s development plan allocated space for streets and 
proposed methods of acquiring land, but it did not provide 
any guidance on street design or creating pedestrian 
infrastructure. For that, the BMC adopted the Pedestrian 
First policy for footpaths (BMC 2014b) following the 
IRC guidelines for pedestrian facilities (IRC 2012). This 
was a welcome move since studies conducted in other 
geographies continue to show that the likelihood of 
crashes involving pedestrians along roads with no sidewalk 
is 1.67 times greater than when there is a sidewalk (Abou-
Senna et al. 2022). 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 43
