However, compliance with policies and standards remains 
a significant challenge, mainly due to complex institutional 
arrangements for intervening in streets with multiple 
overlapping jurisdictions, an issue explored further in the 
section “Pathways for Street Improvements.”
D. Induced demand and what it means 
for road networks
Should Mumbai increase its road network to meet the 
rising demand? In Stuck in Traffic: Coping with Peak-
Hour Traffic Congestion, Anthony Downs was the first to 
articulate that on urban commuter expressways, peak-hour 
traffic congestion rises to meet maximum capacity (Downs 
1962). Essentially, building new roads or widening existing 
streets often results in more vehicles using the expanded 
infrastructure, nullifying the benefits of reduced congestion 
and paradoxically leading to more traffic over time. In 
2011, a study on U.S. road networks found a near one-
to-one relationship between road capacity increases and 
traffic growth; that is when road capacity was increased, 
vehicle kilometers traveled grew proportionally (Duranton 
and Turner 2011). More roads almost always lead to more 
traffic. This is known as the mechanism of induced demand 
or the idea that by increasing roadway capacity, travel 
becomes perceptibly more convenient for motorists by 
reducing the perceived cost of driving or riding (in terms 
of time and effort). This in turn encourages more people 
to drive, to shift from alternative modes, and sometimes 
even induces additional or longer trips that would not 
have been made otherwise, failing to improve congestion 
(Schneider 2018). Several experts and studies have 
repeatedly shown how policies like congestion pricing or 
parking fees along with investments in transit and NMT 
infrastructure are more effective at discouraging driving 
and reducing congestion, especially during peak hours, 
than expanding road capacity (Barter 2011; Spears et al. 
2017; Shoup 2021). Many factors contribute to high levels 
of road congestion ranging across high population and 
employment densities in several areas of Greater Mumbai: 
limited availability of land for development, narrow 
street networks that also cater to informal commercial 
activities, poorly planned and managed on-street 
parking, encroachment on streets, public transport 
demand outstripping supply, and sub-optimal pedestrian 
environments, among others (LEA Associates South Asia 
2016). The RDDP 2034 proposed increasing the provision 
of roads by almost 48 percent of the existing road area to 
establish regional and intra-city connectivity and to build 
roads proposed in previous development plans but never 
built. It did not propose any new road widening. 
E. Free parking on roads and parking 
encroachments on sidewalks
Only a few roads prohibit parking, while most others have 
parking along them in single or multiple rows. Almost 
all on-street parking is loosely regulated and mostly 
free. Unregulated parking takes away one or more lanes 
of streets and, often, even parts of sidewalks, further 
compromising the pedestrian environment. Among 
Indian cities, Mumbai may not have the largest number of 
automobiles but does have the highest vehicle density per 
kilometer. As per the Economic Survey of Maharashtra, 
the total number of vehicles registered in Greater 
Mumbai in 2022 stood at over 4.3 million (Directorate 
of Economics & Statistics 2023). With only 1.28 square 
meters of open space per capita as per the RDDP 2034 
(Government of Maharashtra 2017) and sky-high real 
estate prices, it is difficult for Mumbai to implement 
large-scale at-grade or multi-level parking. Mumbai’s 
Comprehensive Mobility Plan (LEA Associates South 
Asia 2016) estimates that the BMC supplies only one-
tenth of the city’s parking demands. All these factors add 
to Mumbai’s parking woes.
However, various initiatives have been undertaken 
over the years, including the promotion of multi-story 
public parking lots by the BMC and MMRDA, and 
underground, stilt, and podium parking facilities through 
the Development Control Regulations (Government of 
Maharashtra, Urban Development Department 1991), 
particularly in conjunction with property development 
or redevelopment projects. While on-street pay-and-
park sites at prominent locations were effectively utilized 
despite new parking charges, many large-scale off-street 
public parking lots remained severely underutilized 
because road parking remained free.
46 I wri-india.org

F. Disproportionately high investment in 
large road infrastructure projects
Despite half of all trips in Mumbai being undertaken 
by walking (LEA Associates South Asia 2016), and 
pedestrians being the most vulnerable and cleanest low-
carbon road users in the city, attempts to improve urban 
mobility tend to ignore improvements to the city’s walking 
and cycling ecosystem and typically focus on larger 
infrastructure projects to improve vehicular movement. 
Since the establishment of the Maharashtra State Road 
Development Corporation (MSRDC) in 1996, Mumbai 
has witnessed the construction of multiple high-capacity 
road infrastructure projects, including 50 flyovers, two 
expressways, a freeway, a sea-link, and the widening 
of multiple arterial streets in the city, such as Andheri 
Ghatkopar Link Road and S.V. Road. Currently, Mumbai 
is witnessing the construction of over 300 kilometers 
of metro, a series of flyovers, and a large coastal road 
on its western seafront. It has recently inaugurated the 
21.8-kilometer-long Trans Harbor Bridge connecting Navi 
Mumbai to Mumbai (see Figure 9).

As evident in the above graph (Figure 9), the total number 
of on-road vehicles in Mumbai is only increasing—Greater 
Mumbai had 4.47 million vehicles on its roads by 2023 
(4.18 million excluding public transport, ambulances, and 
tractors) (Directorate of Economics & Statistics 2023). 
Consequently, the city’s idea of improving mobility has 
primarily focused on prioritizing the ease of movement of 
private motor vehicles by decongesting the city’s roads for 
them. Notably, only 2 percent of the city uses private cars 
for daily commutes (LEA Associates South Asia 2016).
These large infrastructure projects, realized at a 
considerable cost of about INR 370 billion ($4.36 billion), 
variously aim to decongest, beautify, or rebrand the city 
and improve connectivity with the suburbs or within 
the suburbs (from east to west and vice versa) for faster 
movement of vehicular traffic and reduced travel times. 
An analysis of municipal budgets between 2010 and 
2024 revealed that approximately INR 36,000 crores 
($428.2 million) were allocated to the Roads & Traffic 
Department for widening, concretization, pothole filling, 
etc. In comparison, only INR 820 crores ($97.5 million) 
or a mere 2.3 percent of the entire Roads & Traffic 
Department budget was specifically allocated for footpath 
improvement or resurfacing and walking infrastructure. In 
addition, while the BMC’s Roads & Traffic Department 
also constructs and maintains footpaths as part of routine 
annual improvement work, significantly more is spent 
on road infrastructure where the focus continues to be 
on widening, resurfacing, and beautification, all resulting 
in poor walking infrastructure. Despite commerce and 
community being pedestrian activities, Mumbai remains 
a pedestrian-unfriendly city. For a summary of the state 
of play of street improvement projects in Mumbai (1872–
2023), see Figure 10.
Institutions/rules of the game 
for road safety and pedestrian 
infrastructure 
The need to improve road safety and create pedestrian-
friendly infrastructure has been embedded within several 
city-making narratives in Mumbai and operationalized 
through various plans, policies, and processes over the 
years. We elaborate on the institutions or rules of the 
game (formal and informal) underpinning the four 
important drivers (discussed below) that triggered work 
on pedestrian safety in streets with different outcomes in 
Appendix B. The rules are typically based on laws, policies 
and regulations, financial resources, social demand, city 
visions, and multi-sectoral technological solution framing 
(planning, design, and engineering). Some rules are 
common to two or more themes, while some are unique.
A. Improved connectivity and 
decongestion befitting a world-class city
The developmental vision of Mumbai has aimed to achieve 
“world-class” city standards and has often referenced 
Shanghai as the aspirational model since at least the time 
of the Vision Mumbai report (McKinsey & Company 
Inc. and Bombay First 2003) that influenced the political 
imagination of the city and triggered infrastructure 
spending (Anand 2006). Much of the funding was 
allocated to constructing new roads to improve city 
connectivity. Streets are imagined as segregated spaces 
of smooth flows, with vehicles on roads and people on 
pavements. Anything obstructing or slowing down that 
flow is believed to produce congestion and contribute to 
the city’s negative image. 
48 I wri-india.org

The words of an assistant municipal commissioner 
involved in implementing safer street projects reflect how 
entrenched the decongestion narrative is in the public and 
private imagination:
Most engineers, traffic police, local representatives, and 
citizens think that wider roads make it easier for vehicles 
to move. However, this isn’t always true.—Interview 
with an Assistant Commissioner, BMC, July 27, 2022.
In 2003, MMRDA’s MUIP , initiated with World 
Bank funding, sought to improve the road network in 
Mumbai by building elevated roads, flyovers, pedestrian 
and vehicular subways, and the eastern and western 
express highways. Later, in 2007, the Station Area Traffic 
Improvement Scheme (SATIS) was launched under 
MUIP to ease commuter and pedestrian movement around 
the suburban railway stations by building skywalks, foot 
overbridges, and separate parking areas for auto-rickshaws 
(Rawal 2007). The Bandra Kurla Complex (BKC) 
skywalk was the first skywalk built under this program in 
the suburbs. The focus on pedestrian infrastructure was 
triggered largely by the Comprehensive Transportation 
Study (CTS) for Mumbai published around that time, 
which showed that over 40 percent of trips in Mumbai 
were exclusively taken by walking. Our interviews with 
planners at MMRDA highlighted that point:
The situation shocked all of us for two main reasons. First, 
we questioned why such a large population would walk on 
the streets. W as this behavior desirable or undesirable? This 
warranted much more investigation, which unfortunately 
did not take place. Second, regardless of the reasons behind 
the large-scale pedestrian movement on streets, we were 
concerned about the attention that both the public sector 
[referring to the Public Interest Litigation (PILs) and 
the High-Powered Executive Committee (HPEC) as 
discussed in Appendix B] and the general population were 
paying to the design and maintenance of these streets. 
This raised alarms at the time. Subsequently, the city of 
Mumbai began to wake up to these issues.—Interview 
with ex-Chief, Planning Division, MMRDA, 
April 10, 2023. 
Later, around 2014, SATIS also undertook building 
footpaths under elevated metro and monorail station 
areas and up to 50 meters on either side of the 
stations. Various plans, policies, and processes have 
also consciously addressed the issues of improved 
connectivity, decongestion, and city branding, as discussed 
in Appendix B. 
B. City beautification and city branding 
The visions of city beautification in India are as much 
influenced by the aspirations of the middle classes for 
cleaner and more aesthetic urban environments (Fernandes 
2004; Gooptu 2016) as they are by the dominant 
planning vision in the neoliberal era of remaking the 
largest and most important cities, such as Mumbai and 
Delhi, as world-class cities (Ghertner 2011, 2015). But 
the reality is that most city beautification efforts are 
small-scale, fragmented, and locally driven and, in the 
context of streets, “take the shape of ornate concrete 
fountains and commemorative sculptures that then get 
placed in traffic islands, defeating the very purpose of a 
traffic island that is meant to serve as a pedestrian refuge 
space on busy roads” (Pai and Thoopal 2023). Various 
policies, projects, funds, and initiatives that have driven 
city beautification from hyper-local to city scale are 
discussed in Appendix B. The BMC launched the 2022 
Mumbai Beautification Project (MBP) on the directive 
from the Chief Minister of Maharashtra to change the 
aesthetic quality and visual appeal of the city’s public 
infrastructure (HT Correspondent 2022). This citywide 
public project was the first to allocate additional funds 
directly to the ward apart from the annual two crores 
for regular maintenance and repair works. Senior ward 
officers see this as an opportunity to improve pedestrian 
infrastructure in the city. Our interviews with BMC 
officials highlighted that point: 
We need initiatives like the Mumbai Beautification 
Project, guided by the Honorable Chief Minister. We 
can expedite the process with direct funding to the ward, 
a substantial budget, and the flexibility to appoint 
consultants at the ward level.—Interview with an 
Assistant Commissioner, BMC, February 16, 2023.
Initiatives under MBP 2022 aim to enhance the city’s 
overall appeal and also include tasks like road resurfacing 
and pothole filling, adding greenery to medians and 
footpaths, installing street furniture and attractive 
streetlights, illuminating skywalks, removing unnecessary 
overhead wires, beautifying traffic islands, and artistically 
enhancing public walls.

C. Improving road safety and creating 
pedestrianized public spaces
The set of rules guided by this key factor ranges across the 
desire for public open spaces in high-density Mumbai and 
safe walking environments, driven mainly by the middle 
class, and legal directives from courts, policies, and local 
and international programs working toward the common 
goal of road safety and better pedestrian infrastructure 
in Mumbai. A routine process of street improvement is 
carried out by the BMC’s Roads Department and each 
of the 24 BMC wards every year. This is one of the most 
significant drivers of street improvement and road safety 
that other interested stakeholders, including WRI India 
and BIGRS, try to engage with to bring about citywide 
change. The process involves identifying streets for 
improvement, budgeting for them, planning and tendering 
for projects, executing projects, and maintenance by the 
municipal corporation.
D. Climate resilience
The Mumbai Climate Action Plan (MCAP) launched in 
March 2022 highlights sustainable mobility as one of the 
six priority action areas for Mumbai to become a net-
zero and climate-resilient city. According to the MCAP 
report, on-road transportation accounts for 16 percent 
of Mumbai’s GHG emissions (BMC, WRI India, and 
C40 Cities 2022). Improving NMT infrastructure while 
ensuring increased pedestrian safety and inclusive access 
to mass transit through better last-mile connectivity 
are identified as some of the action tracks to prioritize 
sustainable mobility, which can considerably reduce GHG 
emissions in the city. However, it is relevant to note that 
the MCAP , unlike the DP , is not a statutory plan, and the 
city’s prioritizing of and push for big infrastructure projects 
remains unchanged. Figure 11 summarizes the key drivers 
of road safety and pedestrian infrastructure in Mumbai.
FIGURE 11  |   Summary of key drivers of road safety and pedestrian infrastructure in Mumbai
Setting up no-hawking zones 
outside railway stations
Mahatma Gandhi Pathkranti

Source: WRI India.

Actor analysis
The blueprint of urban streets is informed by multiple 
stakeholders, often playing overlapping roles from creating 
broad visions and policy agendas to providing local insights 
or developing detailed plans and geometric designs 
(GDCI, NACTO, and Island Press 2016). Pedestrian 
environments in particular are governed by national and 
local regulations, with responsibilities fragmented across 
multiple agencies. This fragmentation, coupled with

Management (ALMs)  
• High-powered Committees

• Planners and architects

(RWAs) 
• Media

(PWD)

• Mumbai Metropolitan Region

HIGH POWER, HIGH INTEREST  
Powerful stakeholders with high 
interest and influence on making 
streets safer and better across 
the city.  
Engagement with this group is 
critical to bring about the 
desired change.
HIGH POWER, LOW INTEREST  
Powerful stakeholders with low 
interest and influence in making 
streets safer and better within 
the city.
This group has the power but may 
lack urgency to act due to low 
interest but can influence group A.
LOW POWER, LOW INTEREST  
Upstream stakeholders with low 
interest and power over civic 
issues within the city. However, 
they can influence decisions 
through national and state-level 
programs and funding.  
This group has the power to 
influence policies at the city level 
but has low interest in doing 
so being tied up in many other 
competing priorities across the 
state and country.
LOW POWER, HIGH INTEREST  
Stakeholders with high interest 
but low power. They need to 
influence other powerful actors to 
drive change.  
This group may have urgent 
claims and legitimate views but 
lack the power to implement 
change. But can hold Group A 
accountable through advocacy 
and  demanding desired change.
Stakeholder analysis: Broader Agency Level
52 I wri-india.org
Source: WRI India.

differences in coordination, institutional structures, and 
financing systems, poses significant challenges to the 
design, construction, and upkeep of pedestrian spaces. 
Moreover, the lack of institutions empowered to make 
decisive interventions in urban infrastructure further 
complicates the management of pedestrian environments 
(World Bank 2018).
The authors mapped the diverse range of actors—
individuals, organizations, and groups directly or indirectly 
responsible for making streets safe in Mumbai. These 
include national government ministries and organizations, 
state government departments and organizations, regional 
government agencies, local government institutions at 
city and ward levels, courts, civil society organizations, 
corporate groups, media, and citizens (see Appendix 
C for a detailed discussion of the role of key actors in 
transforming streets in Mumbai). Besides institutions 
themselves, some key champions within institutions 
have helped catalyze the work on safer streets. Examples 
of the key champions within the government include 
Commissioners of the BMC, MMRDA, and MTP , Chiefs 
of Planning Divisions Ward Officers, and Chief and 
Executive Engineers at the Roads & Traffic Department 
of the BMC. Other key change agents from across 
the political spectrum include elected representatives 
such as the Guardian Minister of Mumbai Suburban 
District, Members of the Legislative Assembly (MLAs), 
Area Locality Management Committees (ALMS), and 
BMC corporators of various electoral wards where street 
transformation projects were implemented. Some of the 
civil society-based change agents included representatives 
from AIILSG, the Mumbai Environmental Support 
Network, The Urban Project, Project Mumbai, PK Das 
and Associates, Studio POD, Studio POM, the Observer 
Research Foundation, Abraham John Architects, and the 
Walking Project.
A broad agency-level actor analysis was carried out based 
on the actors discussed in Appendix C and summarized 
in Figure 12. The analysis assesses the level of power and 
interest among different actors to influence the success of 
any street design or improvement intervention. 
The BMC is the most important player in street 
improvements by virtue of its institutional funds. However, 
the success of new experimental ideas proposed by 
technical experts to the BMC is not guaranteed unless 
other influential stakeholders are on board with the idea 
and part of the feedback loop. No new street design 
can be implemented without securing a no-objection 
certificate (NOC) from the MTP . Elected representatives 
and ward officers are instrumental in recommending 
streets for improvement works that get prioritized by 
the BMC. The courts have played a significant role in 
repeatedly upholding the rights of citizens to good and 
obstruction-free footpaths and urged the government to 
frame policies and guidelines and allocate budgets for 
street improvements with safer pedestrian access. The 
importance of buy-in by key stakeholders is crucial 
for implementing and sustaining experimental design 
ideas and processes that deviate from business-as-usual 
models. This point is poignantly highlighted by an expert 
urban planner who has worked with several government 
agencies on public projects:
Let’s take the example of the attempt at pedestrianization 
around the CST Station. I don’t remember the agency, 
but they had done a lot of groundwork that only became 
visible when they conducted a tactical trial. One fine day, 
the roads were painted, the turning radii were reduced 
for vehicles and pedestrian waiting areas were increased 
along each of the 4–5 arms of the junction. It was quite 
exciting at the time. But exactly a week later, in a meeting, 
the MTP revealed that they were under immense pressure 
from local corporators because the roads were all VIP 
roads. Somehow it wasn’t clear to them that the number 
of lanes had not been reduced by increasing the width of 
the waiting areas and walking areas. That message was 
not delivered properly to all the stakeholders. The local 
shop owners and corporators went to MTP , and the MTP 
called for a meeting with the consultant and the BMC 
Roads & Traffic Department. In that meeting, MTP said 
they could not permit this because the corporators and shop 
owners would take them to court. The trial must’ve lasted 
for a maximum of 15–20 days. So, I would say it failed 
because not all the stakeholders were on board. While we 
as professionals may be convinced of the benefits, the gap 
in the understanding and assumptions of common people 
and political actors is huge. Convincing all the stakeholders 
I would say it is the biggest challenge.—Senior Urban 
Planner, Mumbai Parking Authority, June 24, 2022
While design professionals engage with the BMC to 
advance improvements in street design, the perspectives 
of other influential actors—such as the police and 
elected representatives—can significantly shape 
project outcomes. At the same time, citizens and other 
stakeholders with strong interest but limited formal 
authority bring important concerns and insights that 
may support or challenge change efforts. Moreover, 
there have also been several individuals both inside and 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 53

outside of government who have championed the cause 
of safer streets for pedestrians and streets as vital public 
spaces of the city. Their roles are discussed in detail 
in Appendix C. 
PATHWAYS FOR 
STREET IMPROVEMENTS
Most street improvements in Mumbai are routinely 
undertaken by the Brihanmumbai Municipal Corporation 
(BMC), Mumbai’s governing civic body. The BMC, 
through its Roads & Traffic Department, with subdivisions 
of road traffic and road planning, maintains around 2,050 
km of city streets. The department has a massive task force, 
with each zone comprising roughly 6–8 wards and several 
designated project engineers to manage construction 
work.  Table 5 presents Mumbai’s total road length data 
disaggregated by width under BMC’s jurisdiction. The road 
traffic and road planning subdivisions work closely with 
each other to lead most road construction and maintenance 
work in the city, following an established annual process 
of improvements.
The institutional priorities of the Roads & Traffic 
Department are as follows (BMC, n.d.):
 ▪  To maintain roads in a motorable condition and 
improve deteriorated stretches of roads using materials 
such as asphalt and concrete
 ▪ To control traffic through the construction of traffic 
islands and traffic amenities, such as speed breakers and 
road markings, and implement parking policies
The institutional priorities have a clear focus on improving 
access conditions for vehicles, including improving road 
surfaces for enhanced driving experience, reducing traffic 
speed, and reducing the incidence of potholes (especially 
during the monsoon season).

Crash data collection and 
blackspot identification
The primary source for crash, injury, and fatality data in 
Indian cities is the law-and-order police, who routinely 
attend crash events, file a punchnama or police diary entry, 
and, in case of a fatality, file a First Information Report 
(FIR). The FIR is not very specific about the location and 
the road features involved in a crash and only identifies a 
500-meter radius of where the crash happened. The law-
and-order police send a summary of FIR data to the traffic 
police every month, which is used by them to compile 
statistics for the city. These data are then used to identify 
a list of blackspots that are submitted to the BMC to 
address as part of their routine street improvement process 
(see the later section “BIGRS opportunity” for a detailed 
discussion).
BMC’s routine street improvement 
process
The BMC typically follows a civil engineering-led annual 
process of street improvements by mobilizing its massive 
task force of engineers, contractors, and consultants. 
However, these improvements are often limited to road 
surface improvements, including footpaths, such as 
concretization or re-asphalting for surface improvements, 
and sometimes improving stormwater drain capacity. They 
rarely focus on improving the street geometry, increasing 
footpath capacity, or providing contiguous pedestrian 
access. The processes can be broken down into four steps.
Step 1: Identifying projects and 
budgeting for them
Roads are identified for development or improvement 
based on the following criteria (non-exhaustive):
 ▪ Proposed as new roads or missing links in the city’s 
blueprint and road-widening Development Plan (DP)
 ▪ Proposed in the Comprehensive Mobility Plan (CMP) 
or any other relevant citywide study
 ▪ Demands or grievances raised by ALMs, residents’ 
associations, or elected representatives
 ▪ Recommendations of the MTP
 ▪ Streets that have reached the end of design life
 ▪ Streets experiencing severe distress in the 
carriageway or footpath
 ▪ Streets where sub-surface utility works also require the 
remaking or restoration of the surface
The identified roads are first vetted by the zonal-level 
Deputy Municipal Commissioner (DMC), who receives 
the list of roads from all wards within the zone. Mumbai 
has seven zones. The vetted list is then forwarded to the 
DMC for Infrastructure within the BMC Roads & Traffic 
Department. After this, the list is then submitted to the 
BMC’s Standing Committee (statutory executive organ) 
for approval. Finally, a priority list is created, categorizing 
streets based on the city district and eastern and western 
suburbs of Mumbai before being assigned to the respective 
road departments.
Step 2: Planning and tendering process
Based on the approved and subdivided list (city, eastern 
suburbs, and western suburbs), tenders for contracting the 
execution work are issued for roads. Ahead of execution, 
a person is appointed to oversee trenching and the 
correct laying of utilities to prevent damage to existing 
utilities on the street. For each utility, the concerned 
BMC department (garden, sewerage, etc.) and utility 
providers are contacted for approval of the plans and 
necessary action, such as the rerouting of buses by BEST . 
For the removal of encroachments, the BMC coordinates 
with the ward. 
The BMC’s routine process also includes onboarding civil 
engineering consultants to focus on pavement structure 
and typical cross-sections, an approach that often results 
in road and intersection projects being more about surface 
refurbishment than geometric improvements that focus on 
function and accessibility. Urban designers are occasionally 
engaged in projects that focus on landmark streets with 
heritage value but are not included in the routine process. 
Upon receiving the necessary NOCs from the traffic 
police, the projects move into the execution phase. 
Step 3: Execution of projects
The BMC’s zonal offices appoint contractors to execute 
the work based on drawings prepared by empaneled 
consultants and by coordinating with all other agencies. 
This includes obtaining the NOC from the MTP , who 
assess the plan’s feasibility and develop a traffic diversion 
strategy. Road department engineers in each ward 
supervise the execution of the works.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 55

Step 4: Maintenance of implemented 
projects
After the construction/improvement of a road, the 
assigned contractor is responsible for maintaining it 
under the supervision of an engineer from the road 
department of the respective ward for a certain period 
called the Defect Liability Period (DLP). The DLP can 
last from 2 to 10 years depending on the road material. 
The DLP clause is included in all BMC projects under the 
General Conditions of Contract and is regularly updated. 
Beyond the DLP period, BMC ward-level maintenance 
departments are responsible for maintaining the roads, 
typically through floating tenders and appointing 
contractors for road maintenance.
Pathways for creating pedestrian 
infrastructure alongside big 
infrastructure projects and 
citywide road concretization
As discussed in the section on foundational factors, 
policymakers tend to prioritize specific metrics of urban 
transformation, such as decongestion, reduced travel 
time, and enhanced connectivity that cater to the needs 
of affluent car owners and elite urban residents (Anand 
2006). This results in the streamlining of financing 
toward implementing large infrastructure projects like 
metros, flyovers, and sea bridges. These projects, in turn, 
have a significant and lasting impact on road safety and 
walkability in Mumbai. The Coastal Road, the newest 
addition to Mumbai’s infrastructure, has been designed 
without any consideration for pedestrian safety and ease 
of use (Gajara 2024), causing much distress to pedestrians 
(see Figure 13).
As Mumbai gears up to implement several large 
infrastructure projects over the next 10 to 15 years, 
constant changes to its streets due to these big 
infrastructure projects will shape the city in the next 
FIGURE 13  |    Coastal road railings strand pedestrians on road, forcing a lengthy and dangerous crossing
Photo Credit : Ramesh R Nair .
56 I wri-india.org

two decades. The opportunities for creating pedestrian 
infrastructure along them are quite significant, as 
discussed below. 
A. Multi-Modal Integration Plan 2023
The MMRDA is working to increase metro coverage 
within the MMR to 337 kilometers by 2026 (Express 
News Service 2020) and 435 kilometers by 2031 (Lea 
Associates South Asia 2021). Of these, 172 kilometers 
lie within the administrative boundaries of Greater 
Mumbai (Mumbai Metropolitan Planning Committee 
2021). Lines 1, 2A, and 7 are currently operational while 
the rest are under construction, the majority being above 
existing streets while a few are underground. While most 
streets in Mumbai are under the BMC’s jurisdiction for 
construction, maintenance, and repair, for the period of 
implementation of the metro, streets under and above 
the metro lines are handed over to the MMRDA for 
construction and maintenance. Once the metro has been 
constructed, the streets are handed back to the BMC for 
maintenance. Only the shadow area (the street below 
the metro, mainly the divider) is maintained by the 
Maha Mumbai Metro Operation Corporation Limited, 
established by the MMRDA. Similar to the Station Area 
Traffic Improvement Scheme under MUIP , the MMRDA 
launched the Multi-Modal Integration (MMI) plan in 
November 2023 at a cost of INR 223 crore ($26.5 million) 
(Adimulam 2023). The plan initially focused on improving 
accessibility and last-mile connectivity for a 250-meter 
radius along 20 stations of Mumbai metro line 2B to meet 
projected ridership by prioritizing NMT . This included 
widening the footpath, providing crossings, cycle-parking, 
feeder bus services within 100 meters, and intermediate 
public transport (IPT) within 150 meters. The scheme also 
included system-related interventions such as installing 
CCTV cameras and developing apps for booking feeder 
bus and IPT rides, among others. Challenges of inter-
governmental coordination between the MMRDA, 
BMC, and MTP notwithstanding, the MMI plan is a 
critical pathway for creating pedestrian infrastructure in 
the city at scale.
B. Grade-separated infrastructure(s) 
recommended in CMP 2016, RDDP 2017, 
and CTS 2021
The CMP for Mumbai proposes building 18 flyovers, eight 
elevated roads, and 28 road-overbridges/road underbridges 
(LEA Associates South Asia 2016). See Table 6 for a 
summary of short-, medium-, and long-term proposals in 
CMP 2016 for Greater Mumbai. Similarly, the RDDP 
proposes an ambitious undertaking of increasing the 
existing 3830.4 ha of road area in Mumbai by 48 percent: 
1050.1 ha in the form of new roads and 801.5 ha in the 
form of road widening (Government of Maharashtra 
2017). The road links proposed in the RDDP include the 
recently inaugurated Mumbai Trans Harbour Link, the 
under-construction Sewri-Worli Bridge, the Goregaon-
Mulund Link Road, and the Coastal Road.
In addition to 435 kilometers of metro rail proposed 
for Mumbai, both the CMP 2016 and CTS 2021 also 
propose the creation of dedicated bus lanes (DBLs) in 
the larger MMR on streets with a right of way of more 
than 45 meters. The CTS 2021 also proposes building 
1,740 kilometers of highway or major road networks and 
248 kilometers of suburban rail in MMR by 2041 (Lea 
Associates South Asia 2021) (see Figure 14). Similar 
proposals are also put forward by the Final Regional 
Plan for MMR but for the horizon year 2036 instead of 
2041 (Mumbai Metropolitan Planning Committee 2021) 
(See Figures 15A and 15B).
Each of these projects will result in multiple metro 
stations, railway stations, and bus stops along the street 
network served by last-mile services, including walking, 
offering a critical pathway to creating pedestrian-friendly 
infrastructure. On the other hand, highways and major 
roads will interface with at-grade streets, which could 
prove dangerous for pedestrians and NMT users unless 
designed to prioritize their safety.
Barring suburban rail, with all these other projects on 
the horizon, the number of streets under grade-separated 
infrastructure in Mumbai will increase. Currently, 
9.4 percent of Mumbai’s streets are under elevated 
infrastructure, including flyovers, bridges, metro and 
mono rail. WRI India’s research shows that this mere 
9.4 percent accounted for more than 49 percent of road 
crash fatalities in Mumbai between 2018 and 2020.
(Figure 16A and 16B).

-
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 63

Grade-separated infrastructure, while easing vehicular 
traffic, often creates complex intersections and poses safety 
risks for pedestrians (see Figure 17). In Mumbai’s case, 
each of the top 20 high-risk intersections identified by 
the BMC for improvement are under grade-separated 
infrastructure and, therefore, offer a clear pathway for 
improvement through creating pedestrian infrastructure. 
More than one major infrastructure project passes over 
the same street at several locations. For instance, along the 
Western Express Highway, there is a foot over bridge and 
two flyovers, all crossing above the grade over the same 
junction, planned and executed ad hoc at different points 
in time without adequate cross-consultations between the 
different implementing agencies. This results in irregular 
geometry at grade, creating unclear travel paths that 
confuse users regarding how to navigate the intersection 
and increasing the number of conflict points, compounding 
their exposure to risk and injury. The improper alignment 
of traffic lanes increases the possibility of collision with 
the columns of the different above-grade infrastructures, 
which are also visual barriers that further increase the 
likelihood of crashes.
One of our key informants, a retired senior planner with 
long experience of working with MMRDA, cautioned 
against scaling large infrastructure projects without 
evaluating a few pilot projects first:
I see many projects of a similar kind are being taken up 
on mission mode. For example, skywalks. You implement 
thousands of them in one go without the opportunity 
for constructing one and learning from user experience. 
Large-scale building of large infrastructure such as metros, 
skywalks, pedestrian foot overbridges is an attack on the 
city. If one of these has any systemic failure, design failure, 
idea failure, or approach failure, then the whole city 
suffers. So, I think many relevant infrastructure projects 
are far more dangerous than one or two standalone 
irrelevant projects as they can potentially multiply the 
faults without learning from one or two examples.          
—Interview with ex-Chief Planner, Planning 
Division, MMRDA), April 10, 2023
The challenges of implementing multiple grade-separated 
infrastructure in the city are best illustrated through the 
example of Amar Mahal junction in Chembur (see Figure 
18). The construction of Metro Line 2B and Amar Mahal 
junction station on the line has been delayed due to the 
junction having an existing flyover on part of the Eastern 
Express Highway and numerous underground utilities. 
Without adequate and consolidated data on utilities, 
determining the optimal location for metro columns 
became problematic, requiring multiple meetings between 
stakeholders and extensive on-the-ground assessments. 
Despite these challenges, opportunities for multi-modal 
integration exist and offer a clear pathway to create 
pedestrian infrastructure.

C. Concretization
To rid Mumbai of potholes created on asphalt roads due 
to intense rainfall, the BMC, in 2022, released a circular 
(AMC Projects 2022) to concretize every kilometer of 
road surface by 2027 (Pinto 2022). Previously, junction 
areas and side strips were constructed using flexible 
materials like asphalt, but with this updated approach, 
all elements, including junctions and side strips, were 
now to be concretized. Until 2020, the defect liability 
period of concretized roads was five years as opposed to 
the three years for asphalt (DMC Improvement 2016). 
From 2021 onwards, the DLP for concretized roads was 
increased to 10 years (BMC 2021b). This means that once 
concretized, no changes can be made to the design of the 
road for 10 years as opposed to the earlier 5. While 50 
percent of Mumbai’s streets have already been concretized, 
for the rest, the BMC released a tender of INR 6,000 
crore ($713.7 million) in January 2023 to concretize 397 
kilometers of road but had managed to complete only 
25 percent of the target by June 2024 (Acharya 2024b). 
The same circular also addressed the issue of access to 
underground utilities for maintenance and directed all new 
cement concrete roads to be developed with underground 
utility ducts on both sides. Notwithstanding the challenges 
of implementing pedestrian infrastructure on already 
concretized roads, there is a clear opportunity to build 
pedestrian infrastructure at scale across the road lengths 
that have yet to be concretized by the BMC.
Pathways for street improvements 
as part of Mumbai’s Climate 
Action and Resilience Plan
Building on the BIGRS experience, WRI India 
collaborated with the C40 Cities network and BMC in 
2020 to produce the first climate action plan for an Indian 
city—the MCAP—with a 30-year horizon, following 
a detailed vulnerability assessment of the city. Released 
in 2022, the MCAP sought not just to build upon 
Mumbai’s extensive public transport network through 
multi-modal integration but also to promote equitable 
access for women, children, and low-income groups. The 
recommendations were based on evidence that congestion 
in Mumbai leads to 25 to 30 percent more CO
2 emissions 
from vehicles using petrol and diesel, while even electric 
vehicles consume 26 percent more electricity due to 
congestion-induced increased travel times.
The MCAP identified six priority sectors, including 
integrated mobility, urban greening, air-quality 
management, and urban flooding, with 24 discrete action 
tracks to achieve complete carbon neutrality by 2050. 
These included key actions like improving public transport 
ridership and access to NMT infrastructure, increasing 
permeable surfaces and equitable access to green open 
spaces, curbing pollution, and developing flood-resilient 
infrastructure. Four out of six priority areas and several 
action tracks are directly dependent on the design and 
maintenance of street infrastructure, thus offering a new 
and yet-to-be-adequately explored pathway for creating 
safe, accessible, and resilient street networks in Mumbai. 
As a former municipal commissioner of BMC pointed out:
In this day and age, when we are confronted with 
a problem like climate change, safer streets play an 
important role. If we just walk for shorter trips 
without being dependent on a motorized vehicle, then 
it’s a big win.—Interview with Former Municipal 
Commissioner, BMC December 7, 2022.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 67

68 I wri-india.org
LIMITATIONS OF 
CURRENT PROCESSES
The BMC’s approach to road improvement has 
traditionally relied on civil-engineering, focusing primarily 
on resurfacing rights of way (RoWs). This approach 
often does not fully address underlying design and safety 
challenges. Key limitations of the process include:
 ▪ Lack of an evidence-based approach: There is no 
requirement/regulation for conducting safety audits 
or studying existing conditions for pedestrians and 
vulnerable users before designing the improvements.
 ▪ Surface improvements prioritized over geometric 
corrections: The scope focuses solely on surface 
improvements according to the BMC’s specifications 
for different categories of roads based on their RoWs. 
The geometric design of the site based on traffic 
engineering considerations is rarely addressed.
 ▪ Arbitrary footpath allocation: Footpaths are typically 
provided based on a rule of thumb, occupying 10 
percent of the RoW. This approach disregards the 
larger ecosystem, including utilities, ease of walking, 
landscaping needs, and climate considerations, 
rendering the allocation ineffective.
 ▪ Lack of alignment between corridor improvement 
and intersection improvement projects: Tenders for 
corridors and intersections are often issued separately 
and not to the same entities. Several intersections are 
improved as standalone priority projects, resulting 
in misaligned lanes, discontinuous paving, and 
inconsistent design. This disjointed approach arises 
from projects designed and executed by various 
contractors and designers at different times. 
 ▪ Civil engineering–centric design: The road section 
must adhere strictly to the BMC’s circulars, which 
provide guidelines primarily from a civil engineering 
perspective. These guidelines, while important, are 
limited in scope and neglect consideration of traffic 
engineering and urban design. This results in a lack of 
an integrated approach to street design, prioritizing 
vehicles over non-motorized transport.
Gaps in data collection processes and lack of systematic 
crash investigations: A recent publication from the 
Transportation Research and Injury Prevention Centre at 
IIT Delhi (Tiwari et al. 2023) highlights significant gaps 
in current data collection processes, particularly for non-
fatal injuries. In dense urban areas like Mumbai, while fatal 
cases are well-documented with FIR reports, serious and 
minor injuries are not, leading to severe underreporting of 
such cases. Police reporting is driven by the Indian Penal 
Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure, focusing on 
fault assignment rather than understanding road safety 
circumstances. Crucial data points, such as precise crash 
locations and the cause and type of collision, are often 
missing or imprecisely recorded. In addition, there is a lack 
of systematic crash investigation procedures, and traffic 
police are often not involved in crash investigations, which 
are handled by general law-and-order police, who lack 
specialized training. This systemic issue results in limited 
and biased information on crash causes and other relevant 
details, hindering effective road safety measures.
 ▪ Defect Liability Period (DLP): The Standard Bid 
Document specifies a DLP for all civil works in 
street improvement projects. The DLP represents the 
period following project completion during which 
the contractor is held responsible for addressing 
defects. No additional work can be performed on the 
specific part or element in question during the DLP . 
Consequently, certain elements or parts of the street 
under improvement may remain incomplete, or further 
enhancements may be stalled entirely until the DLP 
expires. The DLP for asphalt road works is 3 years, 
whereas for cement concrete road works it is 5 years 
(DMC Improvement 2016) and was later increased to 
10 years (BMC 2021b).
 ▪ Materials: Cement concrete, valued for its strength and 
durability, is the primary construction material utilized 
in street improvement projects in recent times. The 
DLP for concrete roads with a thickness of 280 mm 
and above is 10 years. This lengthy DLP complicates 
design proposals that require breaking the concrete 
and poses biased implementation challenges as the city 
must justify the public expenditure involved. This often 
leads to the abandonment of such proposals due to 
potential adverse audit comments and public scrutiny. 
The BMC’s Unified Schedule of Rates also specifies 
a limited range of materials. Introducing new items 
is a complex and time-consuming process requiring 
multiple approvals and strong justification, limiting 
experimentation and innovation in material use.
 ▪ Complexity due to multiple underground utilities: 
Mumbai’s extensive network of underground utilities, 
many of which date back to historic times, is not 
accurately mapped. This complicates road construction 
and repair work, as disruption to these services can lead

FIGURE 19  |   Illustration of diverse departments and agencies responsible for different elements of the street
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 69
to public issues. To expedite projects and minimize 
public inconvenience, implementing agencies may 
often move ahead without full coordination with 
others. While this helps them address their immediate 
concerns, it can also result in repeated disruptions 
of the street surface, underscoring the need for more 
integrated planning and execution.
 ▪ Insufficient inter- and intra-departmental 
coordination: The responsibility for various street 
components is divided among multiple BMC 
departments and external agencies (see Figure 19), 
complicating coordination for street improvement 
projects. This lack of proper coordination often leads to 
redundant work and duplication by different agencies 
and, at times, even within different departments of 
the same agency. Despite circulars intended to ensure 
uniformity and proper coordination—by notifying 
each BMC department of upcoming works and 
seeking their inputs—the process remains slow, and 
coordination remains problematic. For instance, 
the recently improved Senapati Bapat Marg under 
the “One Green Mile” project was excavated for 
utility work within a month of its implementation 
(Kamble 2024). However, beyond the problem of the 
multiplicity of responsible departments and agencies 
and coordination between them, departments and 
agencies often measure, are rated on, and prioritize the 
total number of contracts awarded and the associated 
expenditures, both of which are outputs rather than 
desired outcomes in terms of public safety or delivering 
safer streets and universal accessibility in Mumbai. 
Source: WRI India.

70 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 3.
  
Relevance of the 
approaches 
Since 2013, WRI India has partnered with the BMC 
and MTP to promote safer streets for pedestrians 
and non-motorized transport users in Mumbai. This 
chapter highlights WRI India’s three key approaches: 
systemic engagements, programmatic activities, and 
design interventions. This integrated strategy not 
only aimed to improve safety in Mumbai’s congested 
streets but also shifted the focus towards prioritizing 
pedestrians and non-motorized transport users.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 71

All three approaches evolved from a deep discontent with 
the inadequate street infrastructure in Mumbai that causes 
extensive traffic congestion and jeopardizes non-motorized 
transport users. This applies in particular to pedestrians and 
their ability to access various places in the city, including 
public transit stations. The predominant global approach 
to road safety emphasizes prevention and enforcement 
measures related to speed, drunk driving, use of helmets, 
seatbelts, and child restraints. It does not, however, address 
critical issues faced by the vast numbers of pedestrians and 
non-motorized transport users in Indian cities, who often 
lack safe walking and cycling infrastructure.
In 2006, the NUTP for the first time created a new 
narrative by shifting the focus from “roads for vehicles” to 
“streets for people” ( Joshi 2023). However, this vision of 
sustainable transport is typically not reflected in transport 
sector investment priorities (Revi et al. 2011). From 2009, 
EMBARQ India (later WRI India) was supported by 
Bloomberg Philanthropies to work on various projects 
and studies in multiple cities (Pune, Indore, Mumbai, 
and Delhi) focusing on creating safer infrastructure as 
a key component of road safety in India to save lives. 
The expertise developed through these initiatives meant 
EMBARQ India and later WRI India were invited to 
strategic meetings and committees, where they were 
engaged as technical and knowledge experts advising the 
BMC on finding solutions to decongestion and road safety. 
In Mumbai, WRI India used three distinct approaches to 
frame the problem of road safety as a major design issue to 
create streets for people.
The three approaches offered a roadmap for solving the 
problem at scale and aligned with the four pathways for 
street improvements identified in the context section.
APPROACH 1 – SYSTEMIC 
ENGAGEMENTS
Since 2013, WRI India has engaged at a systemic level to 
shape the narrative of safer streets for people in Mumbai. 
The various efforts are summarized in Table 7.
High-Powered Committee on 
Mumbai’s traffic
Despite the NUTP being in existence since 2006, the 
first opportunity for systemic engagement on the latent 
issue of safer streets for people in Mumbai presented itself 
when the Honorable High Court of Bombay (HHCB), 
in responding to a PIL, directed the Home Department, 
Government of Maharashtra, on May 7, 2012, to 
constitute a high-powered committee on Mumbai’s traffic 
issues. The directive came after 17 hearings had been held 
by the HHCB with the petitioners and the responders 
from the MTP in which it became clear that the problems 
of Mumbai’s streets cannot be solved by traffic police 
alone since the issues go beyond enforcement to matters 
of regulation, policy, planning, financing, budgeting, and 
operations. Following this directive, a High-Powered 
Committee (HPEC) was constituted via a Home 
Department order (Rajan et al. 2013) dated June 6, 2012, 
under the chairmanship of the Additional Chief Secretary 
(Home) and including members from the Transport 
Department, Urban Development Department, BEST , 
the MMRDA, BMC, MTP , and civil society stakeholders, 
including the Western India Automobile Association, 
Mumbai Environment Social Network, and EMBARQ 
India. EMBARQ was already working on road safety in 
a few cities with support from Bloomberg Philanthropies 
with the explicit goal of “saving lives” and had conducted a 
few studies to promote sustainable mobility in India.

Studies, technical inputs to the 
DP, and guidelines by EMBARQ 
and WRI India
In 2013, EMBARQ’s study on MIDC Marol highlighted 
the need to invest in comfortable and convenient access 
to metro stations as part of the investment case for metro 
rail in this district and recommended projects improving 
streets and intersections in the district in a phased 
manner. In 2013, EMBARQ, which eventually became 
WRI India, was invited to be a part of the HPEC. At 
this point, there were very few solutions in the system for 
solving the complex mobility challenges in Mumbai, and 
WRI India was part of a limited group of stakeholders 
engaged in creating awareness of the mobility challenges 
of non-motorized transport users and proposing localized 
solutions. Further, to support the Development Plan of 
Mumbai 2014–34, EMBARQ studied the Ghatkopar 
Railway Station area to rethink off-street parking 
around station areas in Mumbai. The study evolved with 
continuous engagement with BMC officials and the 
consultants producing the Mumbai Development Plan 
2014–34 to give technical inputs on transit-oriented 
development (TOD), parking regulations, and safe access 
to railway stations.
In 2014 WRI India formulated the Street Design 
Guidelines for Greater Mumbai (Shah and Jain 2014) in 
accordance with the 2006 NUTP to prioritize pedestrian-
oriented designs to support an NMT strategy and to 
enhance NMT’s modal share in Greater Mumbai. These 
guidelines proposed criteria for identifying NMT-only 
streets, priority streets, and NMT zones. They also 
provided recommendations for various street elements and 
traffic-calming measures, along with templates for more 
equitable allocation of road space for various road widths.
From 2015, WRI India leveraged relationships it had built 
through sustained engagement with different stakeholders 
and became the technical partner to the BMC for 
implementing the BIGRS program in Mumbai. Over five 
years, Bloomberg Philanthropies collaborated with various 
stakeholders, including WRI India, to address road safety 
challenges in Mumbai. WRI India specifically focused on 
safer streets and safer mobility, aiming to enhance safety 
through improved road engineering and public transit.
In 2016, WRI India developed the Street Design 
Guidelines for the LBS Road Improvement Project, 
which involved the 21-km-long LBS road spanning five 
wards, as an internal document to be circulated within the 
BMC. The pilot phase designed by WRI India included 
9.5 km across three wards. The pilot execution and scaling 
required guidance as different engineers and contractors 
managed each section. To ensure uniformity across the 
entire 21 km stretch, these guidelines were comprehensive 
and covered both pilot and non-pilot sections of LBS 
Marg. In addition, recurring issues in implementing the 
granular details specified in intersection redesigns led 
to the creation of the Intersection Design Guidelines in 
2018. These guidelines were shared through capacity-
building workshops for engineers, contractors, and other 
stakeholders. The guidelines were based on various IRC 
codes and best practices but contextualized for the specific 
site and city needs.
Task force to address blackspots
From 2017 onwards, WRI India actively provided 
technical design inputs for redesigning many of the 
50 critical intersections identified in the CMP , such as 
Namaha and Poddar intersections, while supporting 
implementation of on-ground trials. A significant 
milestone in this initiative occurred in July 2018 when a 
joint task force was formed, consisting of representatives 
from the PWD, the Municipal Corporation of 
74 I wri-india.org

Greater Mumbai (MCGM), and the MTP . Under the 
leadership of key officials, the task force aimed to address 
blackspots—locations marked by a high incidence of 
crashes resulting in death or serious injury. The task 
force thoroughly inspected all 52 identified blackspots in 
Mumbai, providing technical inputs and recommendations 
for corrective measures.
In 2022, the MTP identified the top 20 blackspots 
based on three years of crash data analysis. This list was 
submitted to the MCGM for engineering and traffic 
flow studies, and WRI India and the Global Designing 
Cities Initiative (GDCI) subsequently submitted 
recommendations for improvement.
Policy/circular landscape analysis
In 2023, WRI India undertook a landscape analysis 
of all circulars issued by the BMC’s Roads & Traffic 
Department from 2010 to 2022 to understand how they 
affect the design of safer streets in Mumbai and where the 
gaps were for delivering quality road infrastructure. These 
circulars provided technical guidance with respect to the 
construction and maintenance of various street elements 
such as footpaths, carriageways, side strips, traffic islands, 
and utilities (see Appendix D). The analysis was designed 
to inform WRI India’s future engagement with the BMC 
by understanding the universe of technical guidance and 
procedures to better align future activities with relevant 
stakeholders and reduce friction in collaborative efforts. 
The analysis also revealed many gaps regarding compliance. 
There are too many circulars in each department for 
information and action, causing staff to lose track or be 
unaware of them. Circulars are also flexible, as they can 
be modified, superseded, revoked, or even canceled from 
time to time. Implementing the guidelines in circulars 
becomes even more difficult when other guidelines 
need to be referenced, such as ITDP guidelines or IRC 
codes for street improvement that often require collation 
and interpretation. Streamlining all guidance on street 
improvement and design embedded in different circulars 
is a task waiting to be undertaken in the service of safer 
streets for Mumbai.
MCAP 2022 and Climate 
Budget Report 2024 
Following the launch of the MCAP in 2022, the BMC 
established a climate action cell in the form of an 
Environment and Climate Change Department in 2024 
(Devasia 2024). With technical assistance from WRI 
India, the BMC spearheaded climate literacy meetings 
with several departments to encourage them to align their 
activities and expenditures with the goals of the MCAP . 
This resulted in the BMC releasing its first-ever Climate 
Budget Report for 2024–25, highlighting budget items 
aligned with the MCAP’s recommendations based on 
their climate impact assessment. It is estimated that INR 
10,224.24 crores ($1.21 billion)—that is, 32.2 percent of 
the BMC’s capital budget expenditure—is allocated to 
six priority sectors and 24 action tracks identified in the 
MCAP (BMC 2024).
Most of this allocation—that is, INR 9,707.97 crores 
($1.15 billion), or 95 percent—is toward addressing 
urban flooding and water resource management through 
activities such as the widening and maintenance of 
stormwater drains to build flood-resilient infrastructure, 
reducing disaster risk and impact, and curbing pollution of 
aquatic ecosystems and restoring them. The remaining 5 
percent was split up between five priority sectors, including 
integrated mobility (INR 8.4 crores, $1 million)      , 
urban greening and biodiversity (INR 177.8 crores, 
$21.2 million), and air-quality management (INR 35.38 
crores, $4.2 million).
A key action track identified in the MCAP is improving 
NMT infrastructure while ensuring increased pedestrian 
safety and inclusive access to mass transit through better 
last-mile connectivity to reduce GHG emissions in the 
city. In alignment with this action track, BMC’s Road 
& Traffic Department pledged to improve footpaths 
to ensure safe and inclusive pedestrian infrastructure. 
Similarly, the Bridges Department pledged to fulfill 
its duties of construction, reconstruction, repairs, and 
maintenance of foot overbridges and pedestrian subways. 
Other action items discussed included integrating cycle 
tracks in road design, upgrading intersections with LED 
lights and permeable materials, beautifying traffic islands 
with vegetation, and providing pedestrian refuges at the 
mouth of grade-separated infrastructure.
Since the Climate Budget embeds climate goals into 
departmental activities through the budgeting process, it 
only categorizes existing departmental capital expenditures 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 75

toward the MCAP’s goals and does not allocate any new 
funding toward these activities. Nonetheless, it offers a 
viable, though underexplored, new pathway to integrate 
safe, accessible, and resilient NMT infrastructure into the 
city’s routine processes at scale.
Process relevance of approach #1: 
Systemic engagements
From 2013 onward, WRI India’s approach of systemic 
engagements in Mumbai played a pivotal role in 
shaping street safety and design policies, primarily 
through collaborations with the BMC, MTP , and 
other stakeholders. The approach spearheaded several 
engagements shaping the various pathways for street 
improvements in the context of the broader levels of 
systems transformations while also contributing to the 
formulation of programmatic engagements on safer streets 
and demonstrations of safe-street design interventions at 
specific sites covered in approaches #2 and #3. Through 
systemic engagements, niche organizations like WRI 
India, specializing in design processes, were able to 
identify pathways within the BMC’s well-established, 
civil-engineering-led, vehicle-centric, annual process of 
street improvements and introduce a critical shift toward 
prioritizing the safety of pedestrians and non-motorized 
transport users.
APPROACH 2 – 
PROGRAMMATIC 
ENGAGEMENTS
WRI India’s programmatic engagements involved solution 
framing by learning from data-driven pilot projects, 
overseeing the execution of effective solutions by the 
relevant government stakeholders using municipal budgets, 
generating awareness through media outreach, building 
the capacity of municipal technical staff, and building 
a community of practice involving urban designers for 
scaling solutions. Table 8 summarizes the diverse programs 
and projects under this approach.
Equal Streets campaign
In 2014, a locally driven citizen movement called Equal 
Streets allowed people to experience a street without cars 
on specific days. Various iterations of this movement are 
also known as open streets and car-free days. Mumbai’s 
Comprehensive Traffic and Transport Study (CTTS) 
showed that despite 42 percent of all trips in Mumbai 
being walking trips and 80 percent of people accessing 
public transport by walking, Mumbai had poor-quality 
pedestrian infrastructure (LEA International Ltd. 2008). 
PILs shaped the public discourse regarding safe mobility 
in the city in 2010 and 2013, and the recommendations 
of the HPEC committee urging the city to find long-
term solutions to Mumbai’s traffic problems resulted in 
the Pedestrian First Footpath Policy (BMC 2014b). All 
these developments inspired the first Equal Streets Day in 
Mumbai. EMBARQ India, the driving partner, developed 
proposals, conducted site visits, drafted temporary traffic 
rerouting and management plans, and convened diverse 
stakeholders to work toward the shared vision of better 
and safer walking and cycling infrastructure in the city. 
WRI India built relationships with the BMC and the local 
police in implementing Equal Streets, which stood them in 
good stead with the system.
76 I wri-india.org

BIGRS opportunity
In 2015, when BIGRS selected Mumbai as one of the 10 
cities for road safety interventions, WRI India was the 
technical partner assigned to the BMC, alongside GDCI 
and the World Bank, to deliver the program. Through the 
course of this program, WRI India and its partners closely 
interacted with all the actors within the BMC who have 
significant authority and a pressing mandate to work on 
making streets safer for pedestrians, resulting in several 
projects that triggered street transformations in the city 
and helped further the agenda of road safety from an urban 
design lens. The BMC was the implementing partner. 
There were five significant programs under BIGRS in 
which WRI India took a leading role:
A. Capacity-building program (2015 
onwards)
WRI India has been conducting several capacity-
building workshops for the BMC, wards, Roads & Traffic 
Department staff, and the traffic police to enable safer 
streets for all in Mumbai. With the COVID-19 pandemic, 
since physical workshops were not possible, WRI India 
developed a virtual capacity-building platform called 
MSL Learn, comprising 13 modules with experts from 
across WRI’s global network. Even after the pandemic, 
WRI India continued to deliver these capacity-building 
workshops to familiarize participants with street 
design principles, raise awareness about the challenges 
encountered by various users on streets, and empower them 
to implement design solutions within the city.

Over 800 MTP officers and an equivalent number of 
BMC engineers have been trained through these programs.
B. Blackspot Mitigation (BSM)
A blackspot is defined by the Ministry of Road Transport 
and Highways (MoRTH) as a stretch of 500 meters on a 
national highway/state highway/urban road that, for the 
past three consecutive years, recorded five serious road 
crashes or crashes leading to death or recorded a total of 
10 or more deaths. In July 2018, a joint task force was 
formed to address blackspot mitigation in Mumbai based 
on the directives of the Supreme Court of India to all state 
governments. In Mumbai, the BSM task force involved 
WRI India and the World Bank as partners of BIGRS 
and key government agencies: the PWD, the MCGM, 
and the MTP . The task force was led by the then-Principal 
Secretary of Transport and the Joint Commissioner of 
Police (Traffic) for Mumbai. The BSM project adopts a 
systematic approach to mitigate risks involving crash data 
analysis, site inspections, and the suggestion of location-
specific engineering measures and enforcement strategies 
(see Figure 20).
The BSM task force visited all 52 identified blackspots 
in Mumbai and recommended corrective measures. WRI 
India and the World Bank, as BIGRS partners, provided 
technical expertise for the project. The World Bank, which 
subcontracted some of the work, expanded the scope to 
include additional blackspots. Specifically, WRI India 
focused on nine locations, five blackspots on the Eastern 
Express Highway (managed by the PWD), and others on 
corridors under the MCGM’s jurisdiction. Following the 
task force’s inspections, a detailed road safety inspection 
report containing findings and recommendations was 
submitted to the relevant authorities. Short-term measures 
like rumble strips and road markings were implemented on 
two Eastern Express Highway locations. 
Crash data collection is a government function, but the 
use of the data is currently limited to reporting through 
MoRTH and NCRB annual reports. Beyond this, the only 
thing crash and injury data are used for is creating a list 
of blackspots, as defined above, in urban areas. However, 
experts argue that this definition, in particular the 500-
meter distance and three-year timeframe, are highway-
oriented criteria that are not particularly suitable for urban 
settings. Best practices suggest using weighted formulas 
where deaths and injuries get assigned different weights 
or ranking all events to identify crash intensity based on 
recognizing risk as a spectrum rather than a fixed number

Source: WRI India.

C. Mumbai Street Lab (MSL)
As requests from BMC to produce designs for safer streets 
and intersections across the city increased, WRI India 
started working with a small group of urban designers to 
introduce them to the system so they could take on some 
of the work. To introduce the right system capacity and 
scale up street-design work through urban design, WRI 
India submitted a formal proposal for MSL, originally 
called the Mumbai Street Design Initiative, to the BMC 
in August 2018. MSL was launched by the BMC in 
2019, top twenty entries, including five winning proposals 
were exhibited at the BMC office in Worli for engineers, 
higher-level officials, and elected representatives up to 
the state’s Chief Minister. A trial was conducted on 
selected portions of all five streets to demonstrate the 
effectiveness of the proposed interventions. The exhibition 
and tactical demonstration had a significant effect, leading 
the Municipal Commissioner to announce Phase II of 
MSL, which aimed to improve 20 additional streets. 
However, Phase II and the implementation of the winning 
designs did not materialize due to several administrative 
challenges, including the transfer of officials and the 
disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. Despite 
this, MSL was crucial in introducing more urban designers 
to the local government. Many of these designers, 
including the five MSL winners, collaborated with the city 
on street transformation projects after the competition.
D. Safe Access to School (SATS)
WRI India conceived the Safe Access to School (SATS) 
program as part of the BIGRS engagement to facilitate 
safe commutes to schools in Mumbai by improving 
pedestrian infrastructure, introducing speed management 
measures, and building the capacity of city engineers, 
along with effective communication and outreach. The 
rationale for the program is based on strong evidence. 
Geospatial analysis by WRI India of schools located 
within the MCGM administrative boundary showed that 
60 percent of roads in Mumbai are within a 500-meter 
ped shed (walkable distance) of a school (see Figure 21), 
which means that these roads are likely to have a higher 
presence of children at school opening and closing hours. 
According to the MTP , 69 percent of 2,786 daytime 
crashes in 2018–20 occurred within a government school’s 
ped shed. Furthermore, 89 percent of government schools 
in Mumbai have at least one road crash within 500 m of 
their entrance, and 74 percent of all road traffic crashes 
involving children occurred within 500 m of a govern-
ment school, indicating the high probability of a student 
witnessing or experiencing a crash while accessing their 
school. Apart from physical injuries, witnessing or being 
involved in a road traffic crash can lead to conditions like 
post-traumatic stress disorder and travel anxiety that affect 
children’s mental health.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 79

and Literacy, Ministry of Education, Government of India); fatal crashes 2017–19 from MTP Department; road network data from Open Street Maps and Google Maps 2023.
80 I wri-india.org

The SATS program sought to improve road safety, reduce 
crashes involving children, improve children’s health and 
development, and reduce traffic congestion and pollution 
around schools by promoting safe walking environments. 
Recognizing that each school environment is unique, 
the goal was to enable the city administration to tailor 
site-specific design solutions by leveraging the expertise 
of street-design professionals in the city. It also brought 
together students, parents, teachers, administrators, city 
officials, and design professionals to create context-
sensitive child-friendly street design solutions. To address 
road safety issues and improve children’s commuting 
experience, the program proposed a systematic 
methodology to identify, assess, design, and monitor school 
zones in Mumbai (see Figure 22).
In October 2021, the trial for Mumbai’s first-ever school 
zone occurred at Mirza Ghalib Road in Byculla. As 
the trial was low cost, using mostly paint and featuring 
vibrant, visually appealing patterns and colors, it was well 
received by the media and the city administration and 
triggered many conversations at the ward, city, and state 
levels for scale-up.

Following the trial’s success, the SATS program 
was launched in Mumbai by the Minister of Urban 
Development, Government of Maharashtra. Under this 
program, 200 municipal schools were identified in the 
city (see Figure 23) and allotted to five urban design 
consultants to redesign street space around them to ensure 
safe travel to school. The selection criteria for the 200 
schools involved identifying municipal schools in the 
city distributed across the 24 wards, prioritizing schools 
for differently abled children that were in high-risk 
locations based on the analysis of crash data. The BMC 
also allocated INR 50 crore ($6 million) for SATS under 
Tactical Urbanism in the BMC 2022–2023 budget.

E. Mumbai Streets Transformation 
Program (MSTP)
WRI India’s sustained advocacy for including competent 
architects and urban designers to work alongside BMC 
engineers on street transformation projects and the positive 
feedback from MSL led to the larger Mumbai Streets 
Transformation Program (MSTP). In July 2021, WRI 
India engaged with senior BMC officials and proposed 
key reforms concerning street improvement projects. The 
most important was the need for urban designers to be 
empaneled within the system to ensure holistic improve-
ment of streets and public spaces in the city on a larger 
scale. This necessitated a programmatic approach to street 
design instead of a few pilot projects. The idea was pitched 
as a citywide innovative street transformation program to 
achieve maximum impact with minimum intervention over 
five years. Case studies of similar efforts in other cities, 
such as the Pune Streets Program, Chennai Mega Streets 
Project, and Delhi Streetscape Project (by the PWD), were 
also presented. The Additional Municipal Commissioner 
for Planning asked WRI India to work with the Deputy 
Chief Engineer (Roads, Western Suburbs) and prepare 
a proposal for a four-year street improvement program 
to retrofit streets in the city. This was called the Mumbai 
Street Transformation Program (MSTP).
The BMC asked WRI India to consider the following 
while working on the program proposal:
 ▪ Identifying a network of 400 km of streets across the 
city to be retrofitted in the first phase, prioritizing 
major arterial and sub-arterial streets
 ▪ Dividing the 400 km of streets into packages that can 
be given to urban design consultants for design
 ▪ Estimating the approximate cost of retrofitting 400 km 
of street network
 ▪ Identifying the number of urban design consultants 
required for the retrofitting of 400 km of streets
 ▪ Establishing criteria for the empanelment of 
urban designers
 ▪ Developing guidelines to ensure design standards and 
quality are maintained
WRI India identified a potential network of 300 km of 
roads that the BMC could retrofit based on available open 
streets map data. Using WRI India’s recommendations, 
the BMC initiated a formal listing process of urban design 
consultants under MSTP . Figure 24 illustrates the different 
elements of the program.
MSTP was the first BMC program where the local body 
issued a Request for Proposal (RFP) for empaneling archi-
tects and urban designers for street improvement works. 
WRI India’s key contribution was drafting the RFP for 
the MSTP , which significantly differed from the BMC’s 
traditional approach regarding scope, prequalification 
criteria, and payment schedule. This involved conducting 
multiple case studies to understand RFPs for similar street 
programs in other metropolitan cities and engaging in 
informal discussions with architects and urban designers 
to draft the RFP meticulously. One notable evaluation 
criterion introduced in the RFP , in addition to past project 
experience, was the request to include a proposed vision 
by the applicants for one of Mumbai’s streets. The idea 
was to attract applicants who possessed relevant experi-
ence and also demonstrated relevant design thinking 
about safer streets.
