Process relevance of approach 
#2: Programmatic interventions
The programmatic interventions were built on the 
foundation of systemic engagements and successful 
data-driven pilots that scaled linearly based on requests. 
WRI India’s key strategy for street improvements has 
been to provide evidence-based solutions to road safety 
challenges faced by NMT users and overseeing their 
implementation by the system, as in the BSM project. 
However, to successfully implement the strategy at a city-
wide scale, key actors and agencies needed to recognize 
the challenges faced by pedestrians on streets and buy into 
the recommended solutions. This capacity building of key 
stakeholders on safer-street design and implementation 
played a critical role, ensuring continuous engagement 
with BMC actors involved in routine street improvement 
processes, thereby making WRI India a trusted technical 
and knowledge partner for safe-street design. But to 
aggregate localized interventions and achieve citywide 
impact, WRI India designed four context-relevant 
programs—capacity building of key stakeholders, 
MSL, SATS and MSTP .
A. Capacity building to enable the 
execution of new ideas by the system
The stakeholder analysis in the previous chapter has 
shown that the most powerful stakeholders with high 
levels of interest in and influence on making streets 
safer and better across Mumbai are the BMC (Central 
Agency and Wards), MTP , MMRDA, MSRDC, and the 
elected representatives. Among these stakeholders, the 
jurisdictions of some critical departments and actors and 
the urgency of their mandates to deal with the challenges 
of street improvement differ considerably. The motivation 
to act could range from wanting to save lives on a daily 
or seasonal basis to maintaining law and order on special 
occasions. Table 9 discusses the roles of different actors 
working on street improvement in the city and the need to 
build the capacity of these actors.
WRI India conducted workshops for the BMC Roads 
& Traffic Department, ward-level engineers, and the 
contractors responsible for implementing street redesigns 
to familiarize them with the intent and many elements of 
the proposed street designs. The engagement process also 
helped WRI India staff better understand the reality on 
the ground and the issues faced during execution, enabling 
them to revise and update design and construction details. 
Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, physical workshops 
were not possible, leading WRI India to develop a 
capacity-building platform called MSL Learn for 
municipal engineers on safe-street design. A total of 13 
modules (videos) were released and hosted by WRI India 
staff and key sectoral experts from within and outside 
WRI’s global network.
In 2021, WRI India started a training engagement with 
the MTP , conducting bi-weekly and monthly workshops 
for traffic constables and inspectors on best practices 
in traffic management, design, and road safety. These 
were conducted over multiple batches as part of MTP’s 
annual two-week training session for officials. As of June 
2022, around 800 police officers have attended these 
training sessions.
In 2023, WRI India piloted the “Enabling Safer Streets 
for All in Mumbai” – Capacity-Building Workshop series 
in collaboration with GDCI under BIGRS. The workshop 
included sessions on road safety, street design and the use 
of signages and road markings. Participants, including 
representatives from the BMC, the ward office, and 
zonal and local MTP officials, were given an exercise to 
navigate a 500-meter stretch around the area to experience 
the street through the lens of a physically challenged 
individual. Some were given wheelchairs, while others were 
blindfolded. These workshops introduced participants to 
street design principles and practices, sensitized them to 
the challenges faced by different users while navigating 
streets, and sought to enable them to come up with data-
driven design solutions. The workshop received positive 
feedback from officials, leading to proposals for ward-level 
workshops from the Chief Engineer (Roads & Traffic) 
and Deputy Chief Engineer (Traffic). Subsequently, WRI 
India submitted a proposal to conduct these workshops at 
the zonal level. This was readily approved, and three out 
of seven zones have already taken up these workshops and 
positive feedback has been received. 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 85

86 I wri-india.org
TABLE 9  |   Roles of different actors and stakeholders with respect to the problem of road safety in Mumbai
STAKEHOLDER ROLE OF STAKEHOLDER WHY IS CAPACITY BUILDING NEEDED?
Brihanmumbai 
Municipal 
Corporation (BMC)
Central Agency (Roads & Traffic Dept.) The BMC is responsible 
for creating Mumbai’s development plan, constructing and 
maintaining roads, managing parking, providing vending 
licenses, coordinating with corporates for CSR funding 
for municipal projects, and redressing public grievances, 
including about the conditions of streets. It improves several 
km of major and minor streets in Mumbai every year. The 
scope for such improvements is clearly defined by technical 
documents, and they typically prioritize smooth, unobstructed 
vehicle movement. Funds for improvements are allotted in the 
BMC annual budget.
Street improvement projects within the BMC are primarily 
handled by civil engineers, resulting in a strong emphasis on 
structural integrity and construction materials. Unfortunately, 
crucial aspects of traffic engineering, such as conducting 
traffic studies and analyzing traffic volumes, and urban design 
principles that involve inclusive and holistic approaches to 
street design are often overlooked.
Wards 
Wards are the lowest level of decentralization in urban local 
government. Ward offices are present in all the BMC’s 24 
administrative wards. Ward officers are responsible for all 
municipal services in their areas. Every ward has a specific 
time allotted during the week where citizens can meet the 
ward officer regarding grievances such as bad footpaths, 
crashes, potholes, drainage issues, etc. which the BMC then 
addresses. The wards usually undertake projects related to 
beautification projects initiated by the ward councilors or 
based on proposals initiated by ALMs, Residential Welfare 
Associations (RWAs), and CSR initiatives.
Building the capacity of BMC engineers is critical to
 ■build sensitization about the needs of diverse users, 
especially the vulnerable; 
 ■highlight the critical role of design in not only ensuring the 
safety of vulnerable users but also ensuring the efficient 
movement of vehicular traffic; 
 ■promote holistic and evidence-based planning, design, and 
implementation of street improvement projects; 
 ■encourage judicious use of central and local funds (wards, 
CSR, etc.); 
 ■foster collaboration and alignment between different 
stakeholders.
Mumbai Traffic Police 
(MTP)
The MTP enforces traffic rules in Greater Mumbai and manages 
on-road traffic in the city. They also conduct road safety 
awareness campaigns through all forms of media.
With the city’s infrastructure development often prioritizing 
vehicular mobility over other modes of mobility (e.g., walking 
and cycling), the smoothness of vehicular traffic is considered 
to be the primary daily objective for traffic police. While 
ensuring safe pedestrian movement remains a key concern, 
the traffic police rely on localized measures to address safety. 
Thus, capacity building for the MTP is essential to raise 
awareness and build consensus on
 ■the needs of diverse users, especially the vulnerable; 
 ■the critical role of design in not only ensuring the safety of 
vulnerable users, but also ensuring the efficient movement of 
vehicular traffic; 
 ■the importance of correctly implementing meaningful design, 
which will not only influence user behavior but also improve 
compliance with traffic rules. This common understanding 
would also ensure that the process of providing an NoC for 
the MTP’s on-the-ground interventions will be smoother and 
quicker.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 87
STAKEHOLDER ROLE OF STAKEHOLDER WHY IS CAPACITY BUILDING NEEDED?
Elected 
representatives (MP/
MLA/MLC Councilors)
Mumbai elects 227 municipal councilors (corporators) 
for the electoral wards every five years. Every corporator 
gets INR 10 million ($118,944) as an infrastructure fund (not 
limited to streets) and an additional INR 6 million ($71,367) 
as councilor funds. These funds are routed through the 
BMC wards based on proposals forwarded by the councilor 
and approved by the ward committee. Councilors can also 
secure additional funding from the municipal corporation 
based on the extent of the work proposed. The BMC executes 
these projects. Each MP and MLA is allocated INR 50 million 
($594,722), and INR 20 million ($237,889) annually by the 
district authority for development projects. These projects 
are generally implemented by state agencies like the Slum 
Redevelopment Authority (SRA) or Maharashtra Housing and 
Area Development Authority (MHADA).
A few street improvements in the ward may be funded through 
councilor, MP , or MLA funds. Hence, building the capacity of 
elected representatives is essential to ensure meaningful 
improvements are implemented and these funds used 
judiciously. Raising awareness and sensitizing the elected 
representatives about improving the safety of vulnerable users 
on the street through design also offers the opportunity to 
use the allocated funds holistically (i.e., beyond the traditional 
approach of beautification and greening) to address the key 
concerns of safety, inclusivity, and accessibility.
Media/Journalists The media play an important role in shaping public opinion 
on the condition of the city’s streets by analyzing expenditure 
on street improvements with respect to the quality achieved; 
publishing data on road crashes, fatalities, and injuries; and 
holding the government accountable for street works.
Capacity building of media is important to 
 ■shape public opinion by fostering informed discussions about 
the state of street infrastructure in the city; 
 ■increase government accountability for providing safe-street 
infrastructure in the city and ensuring transparency in the 
use of public funds;
 ■bridge the gap between technical knowledge and public 
understanding of how design can ensure the safety of users 
on the street;
 ■ equip citizens with the knowledge to demand better street 
infrastructure in the city;
 ■advocate for the needs of vulnerable users by showcasing 
citizen stories; and
 ■highlight best practices and success stories in street design 
to inspire action from both government and nongovernment 
stakeholders.
Citizens The initiatives undertaken by the Bandra West Residents’ 
Association, Bandra Bandstand Residents’ Trust, veteran 
actor Shabana Azmi, and architect P . K. Das brought together 
residents, activists, and professionals to collaborate and 
work toward a common goal of reclaiming public spaces 
along the coastline and creating safe and pleasing walking 
environments. This community-driven approach fostered a 
sense of ownership and pride among the local community, 
leading to long-term stewardship of the public spaces. Citizen-
initiated campaigns led to the Equal Streets movement and 
Ranwar Village festival when the residents of Ranwar Street in 
Bandra (West) celebrate Ranwar Village’s Portuguese heritage 
with a pre-Christmas street festival by temporarily making the 
street car-free.
Capacity building of citizens is important to 
 ■equip citizens with the knowledge to demand better street 
infrastructure in the city; 
 ■increase government accountability for providing safe street 
infrastructure in the city and ensure transparency in the use 
of public funds; and 
 ■encourage active participation in public consultations, 
fostering a sense of ownership and collaboration in street 
improvement projects.
Contractors Contractors are responsible for on-the-ground implementation 
of all street improvement works undertaken by the BMC.
Capacity building of contractors is essential to 
 ■understand new approaches to street design that may depart 
from business-as-usual approaches; 
 ■develop an awareness of design features that lead to greater 
pedestrian safety; and 
 ■ensure correct implementation of design details related to 
pedestrian safety.
TABLE 9  |    Roles of different actors and stakeholders with respect to the problem of road safety in Mumbai 
(cont’d.)

B. Building a community of practice for 
scaling
Since 2018, the demand for safer-street design 
interventions across the city from the BMC and its 
wards has increased. WRI India is a mission-driven 
organization; its role is not to become design consultants 
for every project but to build and run programs that will 
serve as engines of innovation and scale by building a 
strong community of practice with aligned objectives. The 
MSL program was designed to introduce more urban 
designers to the system to serve as design consultants 
for various safe-street projects identified by the BMC. 
During SATS, a similar strategy was introduced to scale 
up the SATS project across Mumbai, where in the first 
phase of execution, two schools each were allotted to five 
urban designers. MSTP furthered the goal of building 
a community of practice for scaling safe-street work 
in Mumbai by empaneling urban designers within the 
system for retrofitting a potential network of 300 km of 
roads. The MSTP demonstrated a new way of working 
on street improvements at scale in a city, but because it 
Source: WRI India.
88 I wri-india.org
STAKEHOLDER ROLE OF STAKEHOLDER WHY IS CAPACITY BUILDING NEEDED?
Academic 
institutions/ students
They help conduct studies and surveys of the conditions 
different users have to negotiate on the street and are 
essential collaborators in framing the problems and setting 
the agenda for design.
Investing in the capacity of academic institutions and students 
is crucial for 
 ■addressing evidence gaps, as there is a significant lack of 
research on road safety interventions, especially in low- and 
middle-income countries (LMICs); 
 ■enhancing technical knowledge through grounded learning 
so academics and students can translate theoretical 
knowledge into practical applications that reduce road traffic 
injuries and fatalities; 
 ■researching and developing strategies that align with the 
Safe System approach to protect road users; 
 ■advocating for road safety policies and initiatives, influencing 
behavior change among road users, and raising awareness 
about safety issues by serving as hubs of community 
engagement; 
 ■achieving the SDG goals on road safety and health by 
providing data-driven insights that inform policy decisions; 
and 
 ■equipping and sensitizing future professionals to contribute 
to shaping sustainable urban practices.
TABLE 9  |    Roles of different actors and stakeholders with respect to the problem of road safety in Mumbai 
(cont’d.)

APPROACH 3 – DESIGN 
INTERVENTIONS
Mumbai has 2,050 kilometers of roads with diverse 
issues that contribute to traffic congestion and pose risks 
to safety, including inadequate pedestrian access and 
safety, absence of NMT infrastructure, lack of parking, 
insufficient shade and street greening, and lack of utilities 
and street furniture, among others. In December 2013, the 
report of the HPEC (set up in response to the HHCB’s 
directive) mandated the provision of adequate and 
contiguous pedestrian infrastructure, including sidewalks 
and crosswalks. It also proposed, for the first time, that 
the BMC adopt a policy to redesign the 1,238 existing 
major intersections in the city and make them pedestrian-
friendly. The report focused on road safety through 
design features. Intersections, despite occupying relatively 
less road space compared to corridors, represented a 
disproportionately high share of crash risk according to 
MTP data from 2014–17, accounting for 30 percent of all 
blackspots (sites where five or more road crash fatalities 
have occurred in the last three years) as well as 30 percent 
of all pedestrian deaths in Mumbai. As open nodes of 
the street network where multiple roads meet and cross, 
intersections intensify conflicts between all road users 
moving in different directions (see Figure 25). Recognizing 
this, Mumbai’s CMP , in 2016, also identified 50 critical 
intersections that required design interventions.
WRI India’s role during BIGRS involved creating a 
street design vocabulary for pedestrian access and safety, 
identifying site-level challenges, and prioritizing the 
specific interventions for maximum impact while working 
within the system with minimal disruptions (see Table 10 
for a summary).
WRI India deployed an integrated approach that 
combined urban design as a catalyst for change alongside 
traditional traffic engineering, enhancing the existing civil 
engineering approach. This data-driven method involved 
the collection and analysis of baseline data, including 
traffic volume counts, signal phase timing, and pedestrian 
movement patterns, to comprehensively understand 
vehicular and pedestrian behaviors. The aim was to 
identify conflict points, preferred routes, and unauthorized 
movements caused by existing geometry. Queue lengths 
and activity mapping were used to contextually analyze 
streets and intersections, while crash data (wherever 
available) were examined to assess safety concerns.
This detailed analysis informed the redesign of 
intersections and street corridors, with a focus on 
improving both traffic flow and pedestrian safety. Key 
interventions were implemented contextually, with 
each element applied wherever it best suited the local 
conditions. These interventions included optimizing 
geometric design to ensure consistent carriageway widths 
and better lane alignment, along with channelizing 
vehicular traffic for smoother movement. Curb and median 
extensions were introduced where appropriate to create 
more compact junctions and corridors and to reduce 
pedestrian crossing distances. Refuge islands were added in 
select areas to provide safer pedestrian waiting spaces and 
improve traffic channelization. Traffic-calming measures, 
such as tighter turning radii and raised crossings, were 
applied where needed to slow vehicles down and prioritize 
pedestrian safety. Universal access was considered through 
the addition of curb ramps where feasible, while enhanced 
road markings, signage, and wayfinding systems were 
installed where navigability improvements were necessary. 
For a comprehensive list of design features implemented, 
refer to Appendix E. The specifications of each of these 
were based on IRC guidelines and global best practices in 
traffic engineering and urban design.
was new, the process of mobilization was not easy. BMC 
officials initially resisted modifying certain terms in 
their standard tender documents and were closed to new 
processes, including accepting the relevant urban design 
scope of work, fees, and the obligations of consultants, 
among others. WRI India had to strike a balance at each 
stage of the process in their discussion with the BMC 
and the urban design consultants to ensure that the 
interests of both were upheld while working with the 
system and still pushing for improvements in routine 
processes from within.

TABLE 10  |    Summary and description of efforts under WRI India’s design interventions approach for street 
improvements from a pedestrian safety lens
APPROACH NATURE OF ENGAGEMENT DESCRIPTION
Design interventions 
for street 
improvements from a 
pedestrian safety lens
Bloomberg Initiative for Global Road Safety 
(BIGRS), 2015–2020
Geometrical design improvements for intersections and corridors from a road 
safety perspective prioritizing pedestrians. A proof-of-concept pilot using 
tactical interventions was evaluated and permanently implemented in most 
cases. Programmatic interventions in partnership with local government and 
traffic police attempted to scale up design interventions for safer streets.
Landscape analysis of BMC’s policies and 
circulars related to streets
WRI India analyzed relevant policies, technical guidance circulars, and 
procedures issued by the BMC to identify gaps that could be addressed in future 
work.
 
Source: WRI India.
Why were intersections prioritized 
through tactical trials?
Street corridors represent a high degree of complexity 
for tactical demonstrations, with multiple property 
entrances and encroachment by vendors, parking, and 
utilities. Intersections, on the other hand, are relatively 
less contested spaces with high visibility and are often 
considered landmarks within a local area. Unlike the 
permanent implementation of designs, which would 
require multiple approvals across the BMC’s internal 
decision-making hierarchy, tactical trials required only an 
NOC from the MTP . Identifying intersections as strategic 
low-hanging fruit that could create maximum impact with 
minimal intervention, WRI India prepared proposals to 
conduct tactical trials for several major intersections in 
suburban Mumbai. This prioritization was also aligned 
with recommendations in the NUTP (Ministry of 
Urban Development 2006, 2014) and CMP , and by the 
HPEC, all of which recommended making intersections 
safer by design.
What pathways of change 
were leveraged through design 
interventions?
Every year, the BMC undertakes street infrastructure 
improvements through annual construction tenders 
for corridors and intersections, together or, in some 
cases, separately, particularly in the case of high-priority 
intersections. Safety audits or detailed studies of existing 
conditions typically do not inform these tenders before 
designing the “improvement.” The scope of most 
tenders was limited to beautification or repaving the 
carriageway and rarely included footpath upgradation, 
crosswalks, geometric alignment, or speed-calming 
measures. Between 2009 and 2016, WRI India (earlier 
EMBARQ India) studied and submitted designs for 
several intersections and corridors, which, apart from 
improving geometry, prioritized the safe movement of 
pedestrians, public transport, and other non-motorized 
road users. However, the teams struggled to obtain the 
buy-in of BMC engineers and MTP officials, for whom 
the idea of reclaiming excessive carriageway space from 
vehicles for pedestrians was counter-intuitive as they 
considered decongestion to mean a widening of streets 
and not vice versa. By themselves, designs on paper proved 
insufficient to change decades of legacy systems, processes, 
and priorities. This challenge, however, is not unique to 
India. Globally, tactical urbanism, or the use of temporary 
interventions—ranging from putting up planters to placing 
rubber sheets to change the surface, barricading, and using 
paint to demarcate areas (see “International Opportunities 
and Commitments”)—gained momentum as a strategy 
to demonstrate the results of new design ideas and pave 
the way for permanent change (see Figure 26). WRI 
India developed a data-driven problem identification and 
analysis process to redesign streets and intersections and 
tested them through tactical trials (see Figure 27). Diverse 
stakeholders and citizens experienced the changes to 
the streets over a few weeks, and various feedback loops

FIGURE 26  |   Tactical trial at HP junction, April 1, 2017, before (top) and after (bottom)
Photo Credit:  Saurabh Jain.
94 I wri-india.org
were developed (discussed in the next section) to inform 
them of the effectiveness of the design. Upon reviewing 
such feedback, the WRI India Team would consult 
with the BMC and revise the designs before permanent 
implementation.
WRI India was able to identify a key gap within the 
process of street improvements executed by the BMC 
every year and provided an alternative pathway as part of 
the overall process of change within the business-as-usual 
model (see Figure 28).

How responsive were design 
interventions to local sites’ 
unique physical conditions and 
problems? 
Even though the layout of every major street intersection 
in Mumbai is unique, each represents a site of maximum 
conflict between different types of road users. All the 
junctions that WRI India redesigned had excessive 
undefined road space—sometimes big enough to 
accommodate three basketball courts, as in the case 
of the HP intersection—allowing for speeding and 
unauthorized movement that made crossing the street 
dangerous and chaotic. Furthermore, pedestrian crossings 
were undefined or worn out and often ran into a dead end 
because of inaccessible traffic islands. Median placements 
were not ideal, and pedestrian refuge areas were lacking, 
forcing pedestrians to wait on the road while crossing. 
Footpaths were often obstructed by signage, utility boxes, 
and trees, forcing pedestrians onto the carriageway. The 
redesigns proposed dedicated pedestrian infrastructure 
by streamlining traffic lanes, expanding sidewalks, and 
providing protected medians and islands. The implemented 
designs could reclaim public space from the intersections 
by reducing their size and using the reclaimed space to 
increase median size, including refuge islands or protected 
waiting areas, thereby reducing pedestrian crossing 
distances (see Figures 29 and 30). Other features, like 
crosswalks and wheelchair ramps, also improved safety 
and accessibility for pedestrians and other users while 
tighter turning radii ensured lower vehicular speeds. For 
motorized traffic, the redesign called for standardizing the 
number and width of traffic lanes entering and exiting the 
intersection. This provided clearer guidance to traffic but 
was a departure from the conventional design idea of larger 
intersections to facilitate faster traffic flow.
One of the largest intersections that WRI India worked on 
was Kalanagar, a five-arm junction with more than 13,500 
vehicles per hour during peak hours—almost three times 
that of the HP junction. According to data shared by the 
MTP , the junction saw over 100 crashes in 2018–2020 
with five fatalities and 36 serious injuries, making it a 
highly critical junction. The junction was unsafe for all 
road users, particularly pedestrians (see Figure 31A). The 
large junction area, measuring 8,690 square meters, led 
to speeding and overtaking, resulting in bottlenecks and 
increased queue lengths along multiple arms. The existing 
geometry allowed for multiple conflicts between vehicles 
as well as vehicles and pedestrians while also allowing for 
wrong/unauthorized movements. The public spaces and 
parks in and around the junction remained underutilized. 
They had low footfalls due to long crossing distances 
and lack of safe access and refuge areas, exposing people 
to moving traffic and contributing to the difficulty of 
walking across to reach them. As a result, they were under-
maintained. The junction also hosts amenities like a public 
toilet and a bus stop on one of the islands, but people have 
to risk their lives to access them due to the lack of direct, 
safe connectivity.
The proposed redesign went beyond just the geometric 
realignment of the carriageway to tap into the potential of 
connecting existing public spaces and increasing the same 
by reclaiming residual areas at the junction. It sought to 
improve overall pedestrian connectivity without negatively 
impacting vehicular movement. The junction was made 
more compact, reducing its area by almost 70 percent—
from 8,690 square meters (about the area of a Manhattan 
city block) to 2,580 square meters (about the area of a 
large mansion)—by reclaiming residual and underutilized 
spaces and converting them into pedestrian refuge areas. 
This resulted in a sevenfold increase in active public 
space from 4,370 square meters to 31,500 square meters 
while reducing crossing distances across all arms (see 
Figure 31B). In addition to this, the slip lane on the arm 
toward BKC was closed, and the geometry was corrected 
to reduce the number of conflict points. The proposed 
design was also tested through traffic simulation software, 
VISSIM, for varying scenarios of future vehicular and 
pedestrian volumes, which estimated a reduction in queue 
length of 34 percent.

100 I wri-india.org

How did the political economy 
of the city challenge design 
implementation?
The geometric correction of intersections claimed public 
spaces in various forms across the projects. However, in 
high-density cities, every inch of public space is valuable 
real estate and is subject to multiple claims spanning 
varied priorities. The contestation of space by different 
interest groups most poignantly played out at a six-arm 
intersection with a skewed geometry and large undefined 
spaces. It is a historically significant public space that 
offered a political platform to freedom fighters during 
India’s fight for independence from British rule. Later, it 
was the venue for other political movements, such as the 
Progressive Writers’ Movement.
Before the transformation, a part of this junction was used 
for unauthorized parking by Mumbai’s private vehicles 
and iconic black-and-yellow taxicabs. Multiple street 
vendors almost completely blocked entry to one of the 
junction’s arms, and a wide turning radius that encouraged 
speeding and the lack of continuous footpaths further 
jeopardized pedestrian safety. The redesign was able to 
reclaim a large amount of public space and continuous 
footpaths that could have been used to accommodate the 
vendors. However, an influential ALM member opposed 
accommodating vendors in the reclaimed public space, and 
it escalated into a local political standoff, causing delays to 
the project. Suggestions included putting up spikes on the 
edges of the reclaimed space and footpaths to discourage 
the homeless, any vending activity, and other forms of 
encroachment. No input was taken from the vendors. 
Eventually, as the politics of the redesign were settled 
between different parties, the trial of the complete redesign 
was conducted in August 2017.
Four of the junction’s six arms belonged to the electoral 
ward whose corporator initiated the project. The remaining 
two arms were part of a different electoral ward, and due to 
political differences, their designs did not get implemented 
even though the trial was conducted for all six arms. The 
island’s construction was completed in February 2019, 
close to two years after the project’s initiation, at a high 
cost of INR 5.25 crores ($624,458). The ample public 
space claimed from the junction was given to a famous 
city architect for “beautification”. A grassy stepped garden 
resulted with the national flag hoisted atop a 25-meter 
pole on the top tier of the garden (see Figure 32). The flag 
was a strong political statement that embraced the patriotic 
history of the locality where leaders have incentives to 
promote projects that will secure them votes.
How culturally relevant were the 
design interventions?
Cultural considerations have shaped and modified the 
designs of a few intersections. The Lalbaug intersection, 
in particular, stands out. Every year during the famous 
Ganesh Festival celebrations in Mumbai, the iconic 
“Lalbaug cha Raja” idol is placed near the intersection for 
11 days, attracting around 1.5 million visitors (Khandelwal 
2019). The culmination of the festival involves immersing 
the idol in the sea, and a procession takes place, passing 
through the Lalbaug intersection. To accommodate the 
large number of devotees during the procession, the area 
around Lalbaug junction is transformed into a pedestrian 
priority zone. The design had to accommodate these 
needs, and the proposed refuge island could not be built 
permanently as that would have put an obstacle in the way 
of crowd movement during the festival (see Figure 33).
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 101

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 103

Process relevance of approach 
#3: Design interventions
The engagement process was designed to initially identify 
levers of change to create awareness of the road safety 
challenges faced by pedestrians on Mumbai’s streets, 
develop a design vocabulary, test ideas on the ground, 
seek consensus on solutions, and scale up by overseeing 
implementation by the system. This process was critical to 
winning support from key stakeholders for implementing 
tactical trials and also to ensure that the projects 
themselves are relevant to solve the city’s mobility issues.
When we try to share studies to change their [BMC 
engineers, traffic police, elected representatives, 
ward officials] minds, it is often hard. The challenge 
starts with lower level officers and continues up to 
top officials. It can be tough to convince them, but 
with persistence and perhaps some trial projects, we 
can show them that our approach works. Once we 
demonstrate this, people are more likely to be convinced.                                                        
—Interview with an Assistant Commissioner, 
Planning, BMC, July 27, 2022.
Key features of the process are discussed below.
A. Building trust in experimental ideas to 
solve Mumbai’s mobility challenge
WRI India built relationships with the police and local 
government stakeholders during the Equal Streets 
program. However, as this movement started moving 
toward a festival mode, the need to intensify the focus 
on road engineering and design to drive permanent 
change became clear. Since multiple departments, both at 
the local and state levels, were involved in the planning 
and implementation of streets and road infrastructure 
in Mumbai, obtaining the buy-in of and approval from 
all stakeholders became a very time-consuming process 
with a low rate of success. Many projects stalled or 
were abandoned, often due to the monetary cost of 
transformation and the time taken for construction.
Cognizant of the global experiments with tactical 
urbanism as a first step toward developing more permanent 
infrastructure, WRI India presented two proposals 
for tactical trials at intersections near Khar Telephone 
Exchange and the Lucky Restaurant in Bandra to the 
then Joint Commissioner of Police ( JCP) for Traffic, 
who was also a key stakeholder during Equal Streets. 
However, since those roads were within the DLP , the 
JCP offered the HP junction as an alternative. With 
the support of BMC engineers and the JCP , a tactical 
trial for the HP junction was undertaken on March 31, 
2017. A month later, leveraging the momentum of the 
successful HP junction trial, the municipal corporator 
for Nagpada invited the WRI India team to discuss 
solutions for the complex Nagpada intersection. WRI 
India provided on-the-ground technical support to 
the BMC during the construction at the HP junction, 
the first project implemented under BIGRS. Soon 
after the Nagpada intersection was implemented, the 
local corporator asked WRI India to suggest design 
improvements for Bellasis Road, an 823-meter-long 
street stretching between Nagpada junction and Mumbai 
Central Station junction. As relationships were built with 
the city, requests for improving intersections and streets 
increased. Following the successful implementation of the 
HP and Nagpada intersections, by the middle of 2019, 
BMC’s Roads & Traffic Department asked WRI India 
to redesign problematic intersections as identified in the 
city’s CMP . Intervening in intersections that were nearing 
the end of the DLP from previous civil works was not a 
problem. Where that was not the case, the city devised 
a workaround by agreeing to execute the improvements 
under ongoing active tenders to remove potholes on 
the roads near the intersection. With this assurance, the 
WRI India road safety team set about redesigning the 
intersections at scale.
B. Data-driven design
Before undertaking any trial, the WRI India team 
studied mobility patterns at the location for all road users, 
including both motorized and non-motorized modes. For 
most intersections, it was found that equal numbers of 
cars and pedestrians crossed the intersection during peak 
hours. This data surprised city authorities, who were mainly 
concerned with improving the vehicular capacity of streets 
while giving limited attention to pedestrian infrastructure.
The proposed redesigns almost always created dedicated 
pedestrian infrastructure and redistributed space to 
benefit all users. The redesigns called for standardizing 
the number and width of traffic lanes entering and exiting 
the intersection for motorized traffic. This provided 
clearer guidance to traffic but was a departure from the 
conventional design idea of larger intersections to facilitate 
faster traffic flow. When the street and intersection work 
scaled, the starting point was survey drawings provided by 
the BMC using a Total Station Survey followed by site 
studies. By April 2019, the team had identified 17 other
104 I wri-india.org

intersections in the city that needed to be improved.
C. Learning from trials
The first trial conducted by WRI India was at the HP 
intersection. It used paints and barricades to give a 
temporary physical shape to the street redesign concept 
drawn with chalk on the road. The trial was active for 45 
days during which the team collected data to measure the 
impact. All movements by all road users were critically 
observed, and a video conflict analysis of before and after 
conditions was performed. Preliminary assessments of 
the design before implementation already suggested that 
high-risk conflicts dropped by almost 70 percent. During 
the trial, the team kept going back to the drawing board, 
made required changes to improve the design, and tested 
those changes as the trial went on. The JCP also monitored 
the trial through traffic CCTV cameras over multiple days 
personally. A junction that had previously required three or 
four constables to manage traffic now channelized traffic 
better, particularly during peak hours, and provided relief 
to stationed traffic constables. In all subsequent trials, 
pre- and post-data were collected, and an opportunity was 
given to diverse stakeholders to experience and review the 
altered space for a few weeks. Then, based on feedback 
received and the analysis of the data collected; the designs 
were revised. For the Gokhale Bridge intersection work in 
2019, the team engaged students from the Rachna Sansad 
Academy of Architecture to conduct pre- and during-trial 
perception surveys with 230 people. Documentation with 
photos and videos of the existing scenario and trial was 
also gathered during this time. A successful trial means the 
MTP will issue a no-objection certificate, which allows 
permanent implementation of the design.
D. Stakeholder consultations
City authorities, politicians, and residents were invited to 
observe the performance of the trials at the HP junction 
and provide approval for the design to go forward to 
permanent implementation. In the case of Nagpada, even 
before the trial, the municipal corporator, who invited 
WRI India to propose design solutions, brought together 
different RWAs around the intersection and the ALM 
Committee to discuss the proposed redesign and give their 
input over two months. In the case of Bellasis Road, which 
had dense built-up edges with very active retail, local 
stakeholders were concerned about how their business 
fronts would be affected by the design and where they and 
their customers would park their vehicles. Responding to 
these concerns, the WRI India team studied the parking 
situation on the street and provided adequate parking 
spots in the street redesign while calming traffic through 
design measures like bulb-outs, speed humps, and several 
pedestrian crossings.
GAPS IN APPROACHES
So far, in all three approaches employed by WRI India, 
the focus has always been on providing technical solutions 
to visible problems in the physical environment of 
streets. Even though the solution framing had benefited 
from navigating complex institutional arrangements for 
provisioning and managing urban roads and navigating 
the political economy of urban governance at the ward and 
city level, the three approaches had not addressed pressing 
equity challenges. In conformity with the municipal 
laws that regulate the use of pavements and the police 
that regulate the use of roads as spaces of flow instead of 
thriving social systems, the approaches had not engaged 
with the issue of street vending and had not taken a 
position on the removal and eviction of hawkers by the 
BMC to implement reclaimed public space designs as part 
of street improvements.
WRI India has prioritized one clear change pathway—the 
annual street improvement by the BMC. However, other 
critical pathways related to big infrastructure projects as 
discussed earlier (see “Pathways for creating pedestrian 
infrastructure as part of big infrastructure projects and 
citywide road concretization”) have not yet been leveraged 
to the same extent. Similarly, new pathways emerging 
from the MCAP and Climate Budget require design 
interventions to look beyond geometric correction and 
build climate-resilient infrastructure in streets.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 105

CONCLUSION
The three approaches were relevant in many ways, often 
working in tandem, reinforcing each other, and creating 
new opportunities through continued engagement at 
different institutional levels to inform policy and practice. 
The relevance of the approaches is summarized below 
using the system transitions framework (Molas-Gallart et 
al. 2021) elaborated in the opening section of this report.
WRI India’s localized street design experiments 
and data-driven tactical trials created niches to test 
and refine innovative street safety solutions. These 
niche-level interventions created the foundation for 
constant engagement with the regime and landscape by 
demonstrating successful models that could be scaled 
up. Together, they created an enabling ecosystem by 
involving a larger community of practice represented 
by organizations like WRI India, GDCI, the World 
Bank, and the National Association of City Transport 
Officials (NACTO).
At the regime level, WRI India’s engagements included 
programmatic interventions like Safe School Zones, 
MSL, and MSTP , and systemic engagements like 
the development of technical guidelines, task-force 
collaborations with the BMC and MTP for blackspot 
identification and improvement, and continuous input 
into Mumbai’s DP from the perspective of addressing the 
needs of pedestrians and non-motorized transport users. 
These efforts aligned with routine street improvement 
processes and crash-data collection and use, and leveraged 
new opportunities such as concretization to provide 
data-driven, context-specific geometric solutions that 
integrated pedestrian infrastructure into larger road 
improvement projects.
At the landscape level, WRI India’s sustained policy 
engagements have also leveraged broader environmental, 
social, and cultural trends to push for systemic changes 
in Mumbai’s street infrastructure. Integrating road safety 
into the MCAP and the involvement in high-powered 
committees like HPEC have created opportunities for 
policy experimentation and the alignment of different 
interests toward safer streets. While pathways to integrate 
safe-street designs as part of large infrastructure projects or 
the MCAP are still underexplored, WRI India’s systemic 
engagements have sought to address the gaps in existing 
processes and champion tested solutions to enable the 
shift in BMC’s institutional priorities. These efforts seek 
to facilitate a more comprehensive approach to urban 
mobility by including pedestrian-oriented street designs 
through the annual street improvement pathway and 
proposing new programs such as MSTP for scaling up 
and making road safety a more integrated part of the city’s 
transportation imagination.
106 I wri-india.org

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 107

108 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 4.
  
Coherence with 
external and internal 
environments
This chapter discusses the coherence of the 
three approaches with global, national, and 
local developments spanning the last two 
decades. By scrutinizing the congruence 
between external contextual factors and internal 
organizational practices, this chapter highlights 
the strategic robustness and adaptability of WRI 
India’s interventions.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 109

EXTERNAL COHERENCE
International opportunities 
and commitments
The global advocacy for road safety as a critical 
public health issue spearheaded by the World Health 
Organization (WHO) impacts policies in India (see 
Figure 34). On World Health Day 2004, the WHO and 
World Bank jointly launched the World Report on Road 
Traffic Injury Prevention (World Bank and WHO 2004) 
to raise awareness about the magnitude, risk factors, 
and impacts of road traffic collisions globally. It also 
drew attention to their preventability, presented known 
intervention strategies, and called for coordinated action 
by governments and other key players at local, national, 
and international levels to address the problem. Taking 
cognizance of the nature and scale of road traffic fatalities 
globally, the UN General Assembly unanimously adopted 
the World Day of Remembrance for Road Traffic Victims 
in October 2005. Since then, the day of remembrance has 
been marked every third Sunday in November.
A. Decade of Action for Road Safety
November 2009 marked a significant milestone with the 
convening of the first Global Ministerial Conference 
on Road Safety, bringing together high-level delegates 
from 150 countries. The conference yielded the Moscow 
Declaration (WHO 2009b), urging urgent and concerted 
action to curb the escalating toll of road traffic crashes 
worldwide. It also reviewed progress in implementing the 
World Report on Road Traffic Injury Prevention (World 
Bank and WHO 2004). It catalyzed the declaration of 
2011–2020 as the “Decade of Action for Road Safety” 
by the UN General Assembly and its introduction in 
January 2011 of the Global Plan for the Decade of Action 
for Road Safety 2011–2020, advocating the Safe System 
approach as a fundamental guiding principle. The Safe 
System approach was defined as a holistic approach 
prioritizing human safety by designing road systems that 
mitigate the consequences of human error, emphasizing 
shared responsibility and that human limitations—what 
a human body can stand in terms of kinetic energy—
are an important basis upon which to design the road 
transport system (WHO, Social Determinants of 
Health Team 2011).
B. Global Status Report on Road Safety
The WHO’s launch in 2009 of the inaugural Global 
Status Report on Road Safety provided a comprehensive 
assessment of road safety measures across 178 countries. 
The report highlighted key areas for improvement, such 
as strengthening institutional frameworks and capacities; 
improving enforcement of road safety laws around 
speeding, drinking and driving, helmets, seatbelts, and 
child restraints; and developing national and international 
cooperation for quality data collection (WHO 2009). In 
2013, the next iteration of the Global Status Report on 
Road Safety included a section on the role of safer roads 
in reducing crash likelihood and severity, along with 
enforcement and legislative measures. It also reiterated 
the safety gains produced when road design, construction, 
and maintenance are underpinned by the Safe System 
approach, where human error is compensated for by 
streets built in a way that their physical characteristics 
minimize potential harmful consequences to all (WHO 
2013). It identified incorporating speed management 
measures such as traffic calming in road infrastructure 
projects as a key strategy to limit the impact of crashes. 
In 2015, the WHO released the 3rd Global Status 
Report on Road Safety (WHO 2015), which was quickly 
followed by the 2nd Global High-Level Conference 
on Road Safety, this time bringing together over 2,200 
delegates from 110 countries. The conference adopted 
the Brasilia Declaration on Road Safety (WHO 2015), 
to which India was also a signatory, committing to a 50 
percent reduction in fatal crashes by 2020. In 2018, the 
WHO released the 4th Global Status Report on Road 
Safety to monitor the progress toward the achievement 
of performance targets. The report ranked India first in 
the world in road crashes and injuries (WHO, Social 
Determinants of Health Team 2018). In 2020, the third 
Global Ministerial Conference on Road Safety convened 
1,700 delegates from across 140 countries and culminated 
in the Stockholm Declaration (WHO, UN Road Safety 
Collaboration 2020), shifting the global target to reduce 
road crash deaths and injuries by 50 percent to 2030.

2018
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 111

C. Learning from other countries
The sustainable planning and development model of 
Colombia’s capital, Bogotá, inspired similar projects and 
movements in Indian cities, with Ahmedabad and Indore 
successfully implementing Bus Rapid Transit System 
(BRTS) projects. WRI India supported the project’s 
realization in Indore. With the examples of Bogotá’s 
Ciclovía and Guadalajara’s Via RecreActiva in Mexico, 
concepts of car-free days and open streets traveled globally 
and gained momentum. The Indian cities of Gurgaon and 
Mumbai launched their own versions with the assistance of 
WRI India in November 2013 (Gill 2013) called “Raahgiri 
Day” and “Equal Streets,” respectively. Through multiple 
such efforts, WRI India, in partnership with other players, 
was able to sensitize engineers and decision-makers to the 
science of saving lives by making streets “safer by design.”
D. Tactical urbanism experiments
Alongside these developments, “tactical urbanism” was 
also gaining popularity across the world as a strategy 
that used temporary interventions to pave the way for 
more permanent infrastructure. New York led the charge 
under their ongoing initiative PlaNYC (2007–present), 
reclaiming entire stretches of road space for cyclists 
overnight. Interventions included actions such as 
putting up planters, placing rubber sheets to change the 
surface, barricading, and using paint to demarcate areas 
(NYCDOT and Gehl Architects 2008). This led to the 
city recording its safest year in a decade in 2009, with 
road fatalities down by 35 percent compared to 2001 
(NYCDOT 2010). To have a similar impact in Mumbai 
and meet the national mandate to reduce fatal road crashes 
by 2020, WRI India proposed tactical trials at several 
intersections across the city. One of these junctions was 
the HP junction in Bandra, where the first tactical trial in 
Mumbai was conducted between March and April 2017.
E. Bloomberg Initiative for Global Road 
Safety (BIGRS)
From 2010 onwards, Bloomberg Philanthropies supported 
WRI India with open funding for five years to improve 
road safety in India, enabling WRI India to undertake 
multiple studies and projects and create awareness around 
the role of safer infrastructure in saving lives in Indian 
cities. Several road safety audits and projects leveraging 
digital technology were undertaken to optimize public 
transport access and operations in Mumbai, Delhi, 
and Bangalore. Learnings from these studies and 
projects were later synthesized in the 2015 WRI India 
publication Safe Access Manual: Safe Access to Mass Transit 
Stations in Indian Cities (Shah et al. 2015) to guide 
planners and decision-makers so that Indian cities could 
become “safer by design.” A renewed commitment from 
BIGRS for 2020–2025 enabled WRI India to continue 
“safer by design” initiatives, redesigning corridors and 
conducting trials across several intersections in the city, 
many of which saw the permanent implementation 
of submitted designs between 2019 and 2025. 
112 I wri-india.org

National and local opportunities
A. National Urban Transport Policy 
(NUTP)
India’s NUTP , introduced initially in 2006 and later 
revised in 2014, consistently underscored the imperative of 
creating streets that prioritized the needs of pedestrians, 
cyclists, and public transport users over streets designed 
solely for vehicular traffic. Both iterations advocated 
for the adoption of “complete streets” principles, which 
included the provision of dedicated spaces for walking and 
cycling alongside the implementation of traffic-calming 
measures and road design standards aimed at ensuring 
the safety of all road users. Both editions of the NUTP 
also identified the lack of pedestrian-friendly crossing 
facilities at intersections as a critical problem adversely 
affecting the safety of all road users. Both iterations also 
promised central government support for constructing 
safe pedestrian crossings at busy intersections, particularly 
on corridors with high traffic density and volume. While 
the NUTP’s vision of sustainable development was not 
reflected in transport sector investment priorities, the 
national-level policy spurred local action from both within 
and outside the government.
B. Public Interest Litigations (PILs)
In Mumbai, two PILs, one in 2010 and another in 2013, 
and the subsequent orders issued by the HHCB helped 
shape public discourse and local government accountability 
regarding the mandate to provide adequate and contiguous 
pedestrian infrastructure, including sidewalks, crosswalks, 
and associated signage and markings, particularly at 
intersections. In response, the BMC issued two significant 
circulars. First, in 2014, they introduced the Pedestrian 
First Footpath guidelines (BMC 2014b), which later 
became BMC’s 2016 Pedestrian First Policy. Derived 
from IRC guidelines, they aimed to standardize street 
construction and footpath maintenance to deliver safe and 
comfortable walking spaces across Mumbai. Then, in 2018, 
another circular focused on enhancing the material quality 
of street infrastructure by using cement concrete, setting up 
grievance redressal protocols, and introducing a dedicated 
app for lodging complaints related to road quality. In 
subsequent years, the BMC released several circulars, such 
as guidelines on planning and materials for footpaths, 
utilities, parking bays, and other related infrastructure (see 
“Policy/circular landscape analysis”).
C. Equal Streets movement
Between 2014 and 2018, citizen-led local demands for 
better streets and public spaces led to experiments like 
car-free days and the Equal Streets movement that 
temporarily closed selected roads to vehicles on Sundays 
and made them available for walking, jogging, cycling, and 
other recreational activities.  WRI India, active in Mumbai 
since 2009 as EMBARQ, was not only invited to be part 
of the HPEC constituted in 2012 in response to the 2010 
PIL, but was also among the driving partners of the Equal 
Streets movement on the ground building relationships 
and trust with civil society and the BMC.
D. Participation in Mumbai’s BIGRS 
program 
Leveraging this trust and the relationships built with 
multiple stakeholders, WRI India became the BMC’s 
technical partner for implementing the BIGRS program 
in Mumbai in 2015. This positioned WRI India to engage 
with the city corporation’s routine street improvement 
work as well as the improvement of roads and intersections 
suggested in the City Development Plan (2014–2034) and 
the 2016 CMP and work with the BSM task force.
To address the growing requests from within the BMC for 
street and intersection designs, WRI India conceptualized 
several programs under BIGRS, as discussed in 
“BIGRS opportunity.”
In 2019, the BMC set up a committee to form a Mumbai 
Parking Authority (MPA) based on the recommendations 
of the Development Control and Promotion Regulations 
(DCPR), 2034. WRI India was again invited to support 
the committee over the next few years in conducting 
detailed studies on the city’s parking supply, reviewing 
global best practices in parking management, conducting 
area parking-management pilots in different wards 
(Upadhyay 2019), and developing a new draft parking 
policy in 2021. While the MPA has yet to be formally 
established, the committee announced a comprehensive 
parking policy in September 2023. Every ward and street 
in Mumbai will have a parking management plan with 
electronic monitoring and ticketing, area management 
plans, and new signage.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 113

INTERNAL COHERENCE
International opportunities
Through cross-contextual learning and adaptation, WRI 
India strategically leveraged insights gleaned from WRI’s 
global network to advance its mission of cultivating safer 
streets in Mumbai. Notable knowledge products that 
informed the work in Mumbai include:
A. Cities Safer by Design
With the release of Cities Safer by Design (Welle et 
al. 2015) in 2015, WRI unveiled a comprehensive 
compendium of cutting-edge knowledge and global best 
practices to catalyze local and country-level actions to 
mitigate traffic fatalities and serious injuries. Through an 
extensive analysis of traffic safety worldwide, the report 
provided robust evidence supporting key design principles 
and urban form elements conducive to enhancing safety. 
Emphasizing walkability, reducing vehicle speeds, 
providing high-quality spaces for pedestrians and cyclists, 
and improving access to mass transit, the recommendations 
offered actionable insights for urban planners and 
policymakers. This seminal publication has served as a 
cornerstone resource informing WRI India’s endeavors 
toward fostering safer streets in Mumbai.
B. Lives Saved Report
In addition, in 2015, WRI presented its inaugural Lives 
Saved Report, highlighting the progress of monitoring 
and evaluation efforts across 16 cities participating in the 
BIGRS, including Mumbai. The report revealed Mumbai’s 
commendable achievement in reducing its fatality rate by 
16 percent between 2015 and 2018, underscoring the city’s 
proactive measures in enhancing the safety of its wide 
streets and intersections.
C. Sustainable and Safe: A Vision and 
Guidance for Zero Road Deaths
In 2018, WRI released Sustainable and Safe: A Vision and 
Guidance for Zero Road Deaths (Welle et al. 2018), a crucial 
update to Cities Safer by Design to advance road safety and 
sustainable urban mobility. It outlines a comprehensive 
framework for achieving zero road fatalities through 
the Safe System approach, informed by the premise 
that while human error is inevitable, traffic fatalities and 
serious injuries are not. The approach advocates shifting 
responsibility from road users to the designers of road 
systems. Key recommendations include enhancing safety 
for vulnerable road users, improving public transportation, 
using robust data collection to inform decision-making 
and track progress, and implementing traffic-calming 
measures to reduce vehicle speeds. It serves as a vital 
resource for policymakers and urban planners, including 
the WRI India team in Mumbai, guiding initiatives aimed 
at creating safer, more sustainable urban environments. 
Analyses of traffic fatalities in 53 countries show that 
those adopting the Safe System approach achieved the 
lowest fatality rates and the largest reduction in fatalities 
over the past 20 years. From the action areas mentioned 
in this guidebook, the work in Mumbai primarily focused 
on street design and engineering, speed management and 
education, and capacity building.
114 I wri-india.org

D. Towards a More Equal City
In 2019, the World Resources Report Towards a More 
Equal City (Beard et al. 2016) series featured a case study 
on Guadalajara’s transformative public space interventions 
through the Via RecreActiva program (Sarmiento et 
al. 2019). The case study delved into how civil society 
activism and broad stakeholder engagement around 
public space catalyzed awareness of street use as a more 
democratic endeavor, leading to increased funding and 
political support for public spaces and non-motorized 
transportation. The initiative also engaged activists who 
advocated for transformative changes to operate within 
the city’s administration. However, challenges related 
to accountability and trust between civil society and 
government stakeholders were also acknowledged, posing 
limitations to the extent of this transformative change. The 
success of Via RecreActiva inspired not only WRI India’s 
initiatives, such as Raahgiri in Delhi and Equal Streets in 
Mumbai, but also informed program strategies, facilitating 
trust-building and accountability with local stakeholders 
for spearheading systemic engagements and implementing 
programs and design interventions in Mumbai.
E. Publications focusing on reshaping 
mobility and public spaces in response 
to the COVID-19 crisis
In 2020, as the world confronted the challenges posed 
by the COVID-19 pandemic, cities worldwide grappled 
with the necessity of lockdowns and the adoption of 
social-distancing measures that fundamentally altered 
urban life. Amid concerns over the potential for public 
transport to facilitate virus transmission, numerous cities 
embarked on street experiments (Verhulst et al. 2023), 
reshaping mobility and public space in response to the 
crisis (de Bruijn and Bertolini 2020). Between March and 
July 2020, a staggering 394 cities, states, and countries 
reported implementing interventions aimed at reallocating 
street space to facilitate safer and more accessible walking 
and cycling. Recognizing the invaluable insights gained 
from the documentation of these street experiments and 
ongoing research, the World Resources Institute took 
proactive steps in 2021 by releasing two guidebooks: 
Safe Bicycle Lane Design Principles (Adriazola-Steil et al. 
2021) and Low Speed Zone Guide (Sharpin et al. 2021). 
In addition, WRI published a synthesis paper as part of 
the World Resources Report titled Seven Transformations 
for More Equitable and Sustainable Cities (Mahendra et al. 
2021). These knowledge products synthesized learnings 
from various street experiments, providing detailed 
reflections on the processes adopted by different cities. By 
evaluating key transformations and offering actionable 
guidance, these guidebooks and synthesis papers empower 
urban planners, policymakers, and stakeholders to create 
more equitable, sustainable, and resilient cities that 
prioritize the safety and well-being of all residents. They 
have also become critical tools informing WRI India’s 
practice, particularly in prioritizing the needs of vulnerable 
populations in infrastructure design and delivery, especially 
in a post-COVID-19 world.
F. Complete streets in Brazil
Particularly relevant to WRI India’s objectives was the 
concurrent work of WRI Brasil on complete streets. In 
June 2023, WRI Brasil released a report systematizing 
the implementation processes, challenges, learnings, 
and results of complete street interventions across eight 
Brazilian cities. Titled Complete Streets in Brazil: Promoting 
a Paradigm Shift (Santos et al. 2023), the report served as 
a foundational resource elucidating the complete streets 
approach, challenging prevailing norms in urban and 
transportation planning that often prioritize vehicular 
movement in favor of providing safe, comfortable, and 
convenient access to destinations for all individuals, 
regardless of their chosen mode of travel, personal 
characteristics, skills, or income.
While WRI India’s strategies in Mumbai draw heavily 
from the principles of complete streets, they also 
reflect nuanced adaptations to suit local contexts and 
developmental aspirations. Despite these modifications 
and slight deviations, the underlying ethos remains 
aligned with the fundamental tenets of complete 
streets, reinforcing WRI India’s commitment to 
fostering safe and accessible urban environments. By 
synthesizing global insights and tailoring them to 
suit Mumbai’s specific needs and realities, WRI India 
exemplifies coherent approach to urban development 
that balances global best practices with local exigencies.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 115

National and local opportunities
Beginning as early as 2009, Bloomberg Philanthropies 
supported EMBARQ India (later WRI India) to work 
on various projects and studies in multiple cities (Pune, 
Indore, Mumbai, and Delhi) focusing on creating safer 
infrastructure as a key component of road safety in India 
to save lives. All these studies eventually led to the syn-
thesis report Safe Access Manual: Safe Access to Mass Transit 
Stations in Indian Cities. It was published by WRI India 
in 2015 to guide planners and decision-makers so that 
Indian cities could become “safer by design.” Other notable 
Mumbai-specific studies that informed safer-streets-by-
design practice include:
A. Street Design Guidelines for the LBS 
Road
In 2016, based on the ongoing study of a 4.5-kilometer 
stretch of Lal Bahadur Shastri (LBS) Road under BIGRS, 
WRI India developed Street Design Guidelines for the LBS 
road improvement project. As an internal report submitted 
to the BMC, it highlighted footpath, carriageway, 
median, and intersection design elements while providing 
specifications and templates for each element along with 
road markings and signage.
B. Internal reports submitted to the BMC
In 2019, the internal report on the BSM Project studied 
9 of 52 identified blackspots in Mumbai, highlighting the 
problems in street design and suggesting recommendations 
to improve safety and save lives. WRI India also 
submitted intersection design templates that year to 
support on-ground implementation. In 2020, during the 
nationwide Covid-19 lockdown, WRI India developed and 
submitted median design templates to the BMC to guide 
improvement work remotely from a distance.
C. Neighborhood Mobility Improvement 
Study
In 2019, WRI India, in collaboration with the Design Cell 
of the Kamla Raheja Vidyanidhi Institute for Architecture 
and Environmental Studies, released a detailed case 
study of the H/West ward in Bandra, Mumbai, as a 
participatory initiative to identify interventions to make 
the entire neighborhood pedestrian-friendly. Titled 
Neighborhood Mobility Improvement Study, it combined 
detailed inventory mapping of pedestrian movements, 
on-street parking, public transport nodes, and routes, with 
qualitative insights from residents and vendors to develop 
recommendations for improving a sample of the streets 
in the ward. Based on a similar study conducted in 2014 
for ward 174 in Bangalore’s Hosur-Sarjapur Road Layout 
(Maiti et al. 2014), the Neighborhood Improvement 
Plan model sought to embody the 74th Amendment 
and engender a ground-up process for envisioning and 
planning cities.
D. Pedestrian Super-pathways for 
Southern Mumbai
In 2020, WRI India developed a draft internal report 
titled Pedestrian Super-pathways for Southern Mumbai as 
a pilot study to enhance East–West connectivity through 
pedestrian connections to mass transit stations and traffic 
generators. With the southern tip of Mumbai being 3.5 
kilometers in width or less, it identified the opportunity 
for the development of dedicated pedestrian walkway 
corridors interlinking the mass transit lines with various 
important commercial and institutional areas that could 
significantly reduce the need for vehicular travel and 
contribute toward reducing traffic congestion. While these 
studies and reports remain internal submissions to guide 
the BMC’s processes and projects, they underpin WRI 
India’s sustained focus on innovative solutions to enhance 
pedestrian connectivity in Mumbai.
116 I wri-india.org

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 117

118 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 5.
  
Effectiveness
This chapter assesses the BMC’s interventions to 
improve road safety and pedestrian access at major 
intersections and arterial roads. Using a framework 
with six dimensions—physical environment, 
institutional structures, financial flows, crash and 
fatality data, behaviors, and perceptions—the analysis 
examines policy changes, budget allocations, 
and practices. The findings suggest that design 
interventions have improved road safety and public 
health in Mumbai, though scaling these efforts 
remains challenging.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 119

To analyze the effectiveness of interventions through 
specific criteria tailored to the nature of each outcome, the 
authors revisited the framework of urban transformation 
developed in “What does urban transformation look like? 
Findings from a global prize competition” (Maassen and 
Galvin 2019). This paper developed the following five 
dimensions of transformation to capture the plurality of 
ways that initiatives can transform in their own deeply 
contextual and relative ways:
 ▪ Physical environment—changes to land use, the 
built and/or natural environment of cities, including 
new infrastructure and/or public spaces, the upgrade 
and maintenance of existing structures, and changes 
in externalities (e.g., reduced GHG emissions, waste 
collected and processed).
 ▪ Institutional structures and routines—changes 
to institutional arrangements, practices, and laws, 
including new and strengthened governance structures 
and enterprises, planning approaches, agenda setting 
on new priorities and standards, data, legal reform, 
and precedents.
 ▪ Financial money flows—changes to the type, origin, 
and destination of money flows, including public and 
private finance, such as municipal finances, market-
rate and concessional funding and finance, land 
values and property prices, investment incentives for 
desirable activities, and household-level impacts on 
financial inclusion and the cost of living (housing, 
commuting, food).
 ▪ Behaviors and daily life—changes in patterns 
of behavior in daily life, such as commuting and 
transportation patterns, consumption practices, access 
to economic, social, and recreational opportunities, 
increases in public safety (traffic, violence), and service 
coverage. In the context of this evaluation, we interpret 
this as behavior changes in pedestrians, motorists, 
and law enforcement officers. We further measure 
the impact on daily lives by the reduction of crashes, 
fatalities, and serious injuries involving pedestrians at 
intersections and corridors.
 ▪ Perceptions and mental models—changes that could 
affect a range of stakeholders, such as local business 
owners, residents, visitors, public officials, community 
members, leaders, and young people, in their attitudes 
toward social issues and self-perceptions, including 
awareness and support for new agenda and policy 
priorities and new social and public narratives.
In this evaluation, these dimensions were adapted and 
further modified to develop the specific criteria for analysis 
of the intended outcomes identified in the Theory of 
Change. They are:
A. Driving high-level and ULB-level actionable policy 
frameworks and informing BMC finance for road safety and 
improved street design
Criteria for Analysis:
 ▪ Changes to policies: Assess the extent to which 
policies related to road safety and street design have 
been revised or newly formulated as a result of WRI 
India’s three approaches.
 ▪ Changes to municipal budgets: Determine 
whether the three approaches have led to allocations 
or reallocations of financial resources within 
the BMC budget to prioritize road safety and 
improved street design.
 ▪ Changes to institutional practices: Evaluate whether 
the approaches have led to tangible changes in the 
practices and procedures of relevant road-owning 
institutions, particularly the BMC.
B. Greater awareness of the role of design in shaping 
NMT infrastructure and safe streets, especially for the most 
vulnerable street users
Criteria for Analysis:
 ▪ Changes in stakeholder perceptions and mental 
models: Evaluate whether systemic engagements and 
programmatic interventions have led to fundamental 
changes in how stakeholders conceptualize the 
relationship between urban design and creating safe 
streets, particularly for the vulnerable.
C. Achieving safe access for pedestrians and greater pedestrian 
safety on corridors and intersections in select local areas, 
progressing from proof-of-concept pilots to scaling through 
implementation by the BMC
Criteria for Analysis:
 ▪ Physical change: Assess the results achieved through 
design interventions, particularly the extent of 
physical modifications made to infrastructure (e.g., to 
sidewalks, crossings, median refuges, etc.) to enhance 
pedestrian safety.
 ▪ Behavior changes and impact on traffic management: 
Evaluate whether there has been a noticeable shift in 
pedestrian and motorist behavior, such as increased 
120 I wri-india.org

adherence to traffic regulations or changes in travel 
patterns, as a direct result of design interventions. 
Also, the relative effectiveness of traffic management 
as a result of implemented design interventions 
will be evaluated. 
 ▪ Improved road safety and public health: Measure 
reductions in crashes, injuries, and fatalities involving 
pedestrians, as well as any associated improvements in 
public health outcomes (for example, improvements 
in local air quality) as a direct outcome of design 
interventions. Also, estimate the potential lives saved in 
the long term due to implemented design interventions.
 ▪ Perceived safety: Assess public perceptions of safety, 
including subjective feelings of security and confidence, 
while walking along designated paths as a result of the 
design interventions.
By applying specific criteria to each outcome and 
conducting a comprehensive analysis across approaches 
based on the above considerations, this evaluation provides 
a nuanced understanding of the effectiveness of the 
interventions across three approaches in achieving the 
impact targets identified in the Theory of Change of both 
WRI India and those outlined in the SDGs. In addition, 
this approach allows for identifying strengths and areas 
for improvement in designing and implementing future 
interventions to enhance road safety and pedestrian access.
ACHIEVING SAFE ACCESS 
FOR PEDESTRIANS AND 
GREATER PEDESTRIAN 
SAFETY
To evaluate the effectiveness of design interventions 
in achieving safer access for pedestrians, WRI India 
measured different changes to the built form of the street. 
These changes included geometric correction for better 
channelization of vehicles and pedestrians, lane alignments, 
calming traffic speeds, compacting junction areas to reduce 
conflicts and crossing distances, and reclaiming excessive 
carriageway space for use as refuges or as public space. 
Particular physical elements were modified or added, such 
as footpaths, curb extensions, medians, median extensions 
and median refuges, traffic refuge islands, zebra crossings at 
intersections and mid-blocks for corridors, pedestrian and 
property ramps, table-top crossings and speed humps for 
traffic calming, road markings, signages, and bollards. Over 
the last 10 years, WRI India has submitted designs for 
several intersections and streets in the city, many of which 
were permanently implemented (see Figure 35).
Intersections
Designs submitted for 82 junctions (18 identified as 
blackspots) spread across 19 wards, out of which 21 
designs (including one blackspot) have been implemented.
 ▪ The city has approx. 3,580 junctions with arms more 
than 10 meters wide (our interventions are also on 
streets with arms more than 10 meters wide). WRI 
India’s safe-street interventions affected roughly 0.6 
percent of those junctions.
Corridors
Designs were submitted for 38 kilometers of street 
corridors spread across 11 wards, out of which 20 
kilometers were made safer, accessible, and more inclusive 
by design interventions.
 ▪ The city has about 2,000 kilometers of roads. WRI 
India’s safe-street interventions affected 1 percent of 
Mumbai’s total road length.
These figures are based on WRI India’s direct role in 
designing and supporting the implementation of safe-
street interventions through data-driven tactical trials 
as pilots before implementation. This approach did not 
allow for greater scale, which directly led to creating the 
MSTP program for scaling safe streets by involving a 
larger community of urban designers with the potential to 
reshape nearly 15 percent of Mumbai’s streets.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 121
