124 I wri-india.org
Physical changes to the built 
environment provide safer access 
for pedestrians
In this evaluation, changes in physical elements were 
mapped at 6 of the 21 improved intersections implemented 
by the BMC (a sample size equivalent to 29 percent). In 
addition, one more intersection (Gokhale junction) was 
considered where physical changes were proposed and 
tested during the trial but are yet to be implemented by 
the BMC. Similarly, 9 kilometers out of 20 kilometers of 
corridors—that is, three corridors—improved by the BMC, 
are considered in this evaluation (a sample size of 45 
percent). The extent of physical change at intersections was 
measured by comparing the percentage of implemented 
design elements to those proposed. Mapping physical 
elements along corridors proved challenging due to their 
numerous and varying characteristics across segments of 
the same street. Nevertheless, the extent of implementation 
was calculated for Bellasis Road.
One of the most significant results achieved by 
the interventions was visible changes to the built 
environment of the streets to provide safer access to 
pedestrians. The most effective strategies to deliver this 
result, as seen across six intersections, are discussed below.
A. Safer pedestrian access can be 
achieved by streamlining traffic, reducing 
excess carriageway space, and reducing 
the size of intersections
By streamlining vehicular movement, excess carriageway 
space was reduced, thereby decreasing the likelihood of 
vehicle-to-vehicle conflicts. This also reduced the overall 
size of the intersections, by 49 percent on average (see 
Figure 36), creating direct, shorter pedestrian paths, 
minimizing crossing distance and time, and thereby 
decreasing chances of vehicle-to-pedestrian conflicts. 
Compact junctions also deter speeding and overtaking 
by motorists, prevent bottlenecks, and curb unauthorized 
vehicular movements.
B. Geometric corrections enable the 
reclamation of public space, providing 
safer pedestrian access
While reducing the junction area alone did not guarantee 
safer access, it allowed for effective utilization of space at 
the junction by reclaiming excessive carriageway space to 
provide safe and continuous pedestrian connectivity and to 
serve as public spaces.
A total of 90,338 square meters of public space was 
reclaimed across all 21 intersections and 20 kilometers 
of corridors where WRI India implemented safe-street 
designs. This reclamation was achieved by creating refuge 
islands, public plazas, and footpath additions and exten-
sions. Of the reclaimed public space, 86,452 square meters 
was from 20 kilometers of corridors (see an example from 
a corridor in Figure 37), while 3,886 square meters was 
reclaimed from 21 intersections.
Across the subsample of three intersections and two 
corridors, a total of 45,267 square meters of carriageway 
space was reclaimed as either refuge or public space—
837 square meters from the three junctions (Nagpada, 
Bharatmata, and Namaha) and 44,430 square meters 
from the two streets (a 6.74-kilometers-stretch of LBS 
and an 850-meter-stretch of Bellasis Road).
Among the intersections, the largest amount of 
carriageway space reclaimed as refuge islands was 
at Kalanagar—1493 square meters—despite being 
only partially implemented (see Figure 38). However, 
this represented only 17 percent of the total junction 
area. Proportionally, the most extensive reclamation 
of carriageway space was implemented at Nagpada, 
measuring only 602 square meters in comparison but 
representing 23 percent of the overall junction area. 
This was followed by HP (20 percent), Bharatmata (8 
percent), Poddar (7 percent), and Namaha (6 percent) 
(see Figure 39).

C. Implementing specific design 
elements supports safe pedestrian 
access
Across the six intersections, 12 specific design elements 
were mapped pre-intervention, at the proposal stage, 
and post-implementation to calculate the extent of 
implementation across each design element. These 
included footpaths or curb extensions, medians, median 
extensions and median refuges, traffic islands, zebra 
crossings at intersections and at mid-blocks for corridors, 
pedestrian and property ramps, table-top crossings and 
speed humps for traffic calming, road markings, signages, 
and bollards (see Appendix E for a summary of the extent 
of implementation of proposed design elements across five 
intersections and three corridors).
Three of the intersections—Namaha, HP , and Poddar—
implemented most of the design changes, including 
specific physical elements proposed by WRI India’s 
designs, the extent of implementation being 90 percent, 
88 percent, and 85 percent, respectively (see Figure 40). 
These high-implementing intersections were followed 
by Bharatmata and Kalanagar intersections where 67 
percent and 65 percent of the proposed changes were 
implemented, respectively, and Namaha, where less than 
50 percent of the proposed changes were retained after 
final implementation. At Gokhale junction, though the 
proposed changes were tested during the trial phase, they 
are yet to be implemented because of the ongoing work for 
the repair of the Gokhale railway bridge.
Among the corridors, mapping of proposed and 
implemented physical design elements was undertaken 
only for Bellasis Road, where overall, 68 percent of the

Behavior changes and impact on 
traffic management
Intercept surveys and user-behavior mapping were 
simultaneously administered at three out of the six 
intersections considered in this evaluation (a sub-sample 
equivalent to 50 percent) and two out of the three 
corridors considered in this evaluation (a sub-sample 
size of 67 percent) to determine the extent to which 
pedestrians and motorists complied with the design 
while navigating the sites. Along with this, pedestrians’ 
perceptions of safety, accessibility, and comfort were 
recorded. Motorists’ compliance with design elements like 
marked stop lines and traffic management instruments like 
signals was also observed and recorded at these five sites. 
The effectiveness of the design interventions is discussed at 
the following four levels:
A. Pedestrian-behavior mapping
Across the subsample of three intersections and two 
corridors, the behavior of 761 pedestrians was mapped 
using behavior mapping techniques to determine 
compliance with the design. Overall, an average of 62 
percent of the mapped pedestrians followed the designated 
pedestrian paths while 38 percent did not comply. 
Similarly, when crossing the street, 40 percent of mapped 
pedestrians adhered to marked crossings across the three 
FIGURE 41  |    (Clockwise) Implementation of curb extensions, mid-block crossings, footpaths near bus stops, and

Source: WRI India.

intersections and two corridors. This indicates that even 
after the implementation of the proposed designs, user 
behavior is not fully in accordance with the suggested 
path/route for various reasons, necessitating continuous 
monitoring of pedestrian movements to adequately inform 
the various stages of implementation.
Intersections where the highest percentage of the 
proposed design was implemented on the ground also 
recorded the highest compliance by pedestrians, as 
demonstrated through behaviors such as walking on 
designated paths and crossings (see Figure 42).

Namaha junction, which saw the highest percentage of 
the proposed design implemented on the ground (90 
percent), also recorded the highest compliance with both 
designated pedestrian paths (73 percent) and designated 
crossings (53 percent) (see Figure 43). In some cases, 
observed pedestrians were already walking on the 
carriageway beyond the redesigned area and continued 
their movement on the carriageway and not along 
designated paths.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 135
At Bharatmata, though 100 percent of proposed medians, 
median refuges, pedestrian crossings, and road markings 
were implemented, only 67 percent of proposed pedestrian 
ramps were implemented. In contrast, footpath extensions 
were not implemented at all. However, the presence 
of street activities like vending and parking obstructed 
both the designated path and access to the designated 
crossings. At Nagpada, which saw the lowest compliance 
of pedestrian behavior with the intended design, 67 
percent of proposed medians and median refuges were 
implemented, and only half the proposed pedestrian 
crossings and road markings. Furthermore, designs were 
often modified over time due to various city beautification 
drives, leading to visibility and accessibility issues. At 
Nagpada, the footpath was barricaded with entry and exit 
at a location not visible from the desired walking route, 
as a result of which people ended up walking on the 
carriageway (see Figure 44).
Amongst the subsample of corridors, 68 percent and 61 
percent of pedestrians mapped at LBS and Bellasis Roads, 
respectively, were compliant with designated walking 
paths. This is because at some locations along the street 
corridors either surfaces were damaged or the presence of 
parked vehicles and street furniture obstructed movement 
and made it uncomfortable to follow designated paths. 
Crossing compliance was low along both corridors, with 37 
percent of mapped pedestrians compliant at Bellasis Road 
and only 30 percent at LBS (see Figure 45). This is because 
the interventions at LBS Road covered a large distance 
(6.74 kilometers) with infrequent median gaps; this meant 
infrequent designed pedestrian crossings, resulting in poor 
compliance. On-going metro construction along LBS may 
also have prohibited crossing at designated locations.
At Bellasis, most of the proposed designs were 
implemented since they covered a relatively small distance 
of 0.85 kilometers. Though frequent crossings were 
provided along the intervention stretch, they were not 
signalized. Despite the variation in the implementation of 
proposed physical design elements, 61 percent of mapped 
pedestrians followed designated paths and crossings, 
and only 39 percent did not, largely due to parking on 
the footpath and spillovers from shops and vending 
along the edges.

Photo Credit:  Arpitha Lakshman.

136 I wri-india.org
B. Motorist behavior mapping
Across the same five sites, a total of 767 motorists were 
observed for their compliance with the stop line, with 
signals, and with both signals and stop lines. Motorists 
exhibited varying levels of compliance with traffic 
measures—20 percent of motorists complied with both 
measures (stop line and signals), 57 percent complied 
with one of the measures (15 percent followed only stop 
lines, 42 percent observed only signals), and 23 percent 
failed to comply with either measure. As a result, motorists 
occupied pedestrian crossing areas at times, forcing the 
pedestrians to cross at undesignated and unmarked 
locations (see Figure 45).

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 137
Motorists’ compliance with stop lines varied across the five 
sites, ranging from 9 percent and 11 percent at Bellasis 
and LBS Roads to 18 percent and 20 percent at Namaha 
and Bharatmata junctions, respectively. Bharatmata 
exhibited higher compliance with stop lines and with 
traffic signals (57 percent) than Namaha (50 percent), 
despite only 67 percent of the design being implemented 
at Bharatmata versus 90 percent at Namaha. However, 
Namaha exhibited marginally higher compliance with 
both stop lines and signals (19 percent) than Bharatmata 
(17 percent). Nagpada, on the other hand, exhibited the 
lowest compliance with stop lines among junctions (16 
percent) and a significantly higher compliance (56 percent) 
with traffic signals, even though only 48 percent of the 
proposed design was implemented at Nagpada. Based on 
these results, motorists may be more likely to comply with 
traffic signals than marked stop lines on the carriageway, 
primarily due to the significant penalties for running a red 
light. While some drivers might have overlooked the stop 
lines, fearing hefty fines and potential legal consequences 
typically ensures adherence to traffic signals. Bellasis and 
LBS exhibited the lowest compliance with traffic signals 
at 28 percent and 20 percent, respectively. This could be 
partially attributed to shorter pedestrian signal timings 
and defective signals along these corridors, resulting in an 
increased risk of conflicts and non-compliance. 
C. Self-organization of vendors and social 
life of streets
Beyond just modifying the built environment of 
intersections and corridors to facilitate conflict-free 
movement of vehicles and people, WRI India’s design 
interventions have also enabled the enhancement of the 
place-ness of streets and their inherent relationship to 
people, their perceptions, and practices ( Jha and Bharat 
2023). Mumbai’s streets exhibit a sometimes graceful 
and sometimes conflicting coexistence of pedestrian 
pathways and vibrant commerce, intertwining to meet 
the diverse needs and rhythms of everyday lives. But with 
the redesigned intersections and corridors, street vendors 
have adapted to the changes, finding new opportunities 
and ways to organize their activities without disrupting 
pedestrian movement. This self-organization underscores 
the dynamic and vibrant nature of urban street spaces and 
the adaptability of street vendors in responding to changes 
in their environment. For instance, at LBS and Bellasis 
Roads, vendors have strategically positioned themselves 
along the widened sidewalks, ensuring they do not hinder 
pedestrian movement. The same was observed on the 
Andheri Ghatkopar Link Road (AGLR), where wider 
footpaths seamlessly improved pedestrian access to metro 
station entry points, also creating opportunities for vendor 
integration while maintaining a clear line of movement for 
pedestrians (see Figure 46). 
Photo Credit:  Ameya Mahale.
FIGURE 46  |   Vendor integration along wider sidewalks on AGLR after implementation

138 I wri-india.org
Enhanced pedestrian infrastructure, along with 
streamlined traffic flow at intervention sites, have 
reduced the likelihood of conflicts, creating safer and 
more inviting environments for vendors to operate. 
Moreover, these changes have likely boosted foot 
traffic, offering vendors the prospect of tapping into a 
broader customer base.
Redesigned streets help to redefine spatial relationships, 
reflecting the community’s negotiation of public spaces for 
other uses that go beyond the stated intent of safe walking 
environments. For instance, the inclusion of benches along 
AGLR’s sidewalks, the establishment of a vachanalaya, 
or reading room, at Nagpada junction, the repurposing 
of reclaimed traffic islands for activities like cycling or 
skateboarding at the HP junction, and the claiming of 
sidewalks for celebrating local festivals in Bellasis Road 
transform streets into performative spaces (see Figure 47). 
These changes extend beyond physical alterations, fostering 
encounters beyond familial ties, facilitating connections 
with “familiar strangers,”and offering vantage points 
for pause and temporary ownership. As a result, they 
imbue the streetscape with enduring, quasi-primary, and 
passive forms of sociability (Thomas and Padmanabhan 
2023), enriching the urban experience and fostering a 
sense of belonging. 
FIGURE 47  |     Local festival celebrations at Bellasis 
Road
However, certain challenges remain, such as the 
installation of railings at certain locations that disrupt the 
intended path for pedestrians and restrict space available 
for vendors to operate. While at AGLR, WRI India built 
the case for and successfully convinced stakeholders to 
remove existing railings while widening the footpath; at 
LBS the railings were installed after the implementation 
of the proposed design, thereby partially undermining 
its intended outcome (see Figure 48). Railings along 
footpaths, ostensibly installed for pedestrian safety against 
speeding vehicles, paradoxically compromise overall safety. 
This dilemma underscores the complexity of mental 
models and stakeholder perceptions concerning road safety, 
a challenge that WRI India continues to grapple with in 
its efforts to enact change.
Photo Credit:  Yash Siroliya.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 139
FIGURE 48A  |    Railings installed after implementation at LBS Marg (top), Pre-existing railing replaced with wider 
footpath (bottom)

140 I wri-india.org
D. Impact on traffic management
The most substantial transformation in the urban 
landscape occurred with the reclamation of pedestrianized 
public spaces. However, any endeavor to repurpose 
carriageway space necessitated approval from BMC 
administrators, engineers, and the MTP . Initially hesitant 
to reallocate space designated for cars to enhance 
pedestrian access, these stakeholders gradually embraced 
change after observing successful trials at key intersections 
such as HP , Nagpada, and Bharatmata. Administrators 
and traffic police reported significant improvements in 
traffic management at these junctions, which improved 
pedestrian accessibility without any additional burden 
on ground personnel. At the HP and Bharatmata 
intersections, which previously required the presence 
of three to four traffic constables for effective traffic 
management, the redesigned junction rendered the need 
for constables obsolete. Such tangible improvement in 
traffic management without burdening MTP helped build 
consensus from the grassroots regarding the pivotal role 
of pedestrian infrastructure in enhancing accessibility and 
efficiently managing traffic at intersections. 
FIGURE 48B  |   Public seating at AGLR
Source: WRI India.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 141
Improved road safety and public 
health
To evaluate the effectiveness of design interventions in 
improving pedestrian safety, mainly against vehicles, WRI 
India obtained crash data for seven intersections and three 
corridors from the MTP for five years, 2018 through 
2022. The dataset covered parameters such as crashes, 
crashes involving pedestrians, fatalities, serious injuries, 
and pedestrians sustaining serious injuries. This dataset was 
then used to calculate results against each parameter before 
and after the implementation of the design to determine 
its effectiveness in improving road safety and public health.
A. Greater safety from crashes due to 
design interventions
ABSOLUTE NUMBER OF CRASHES AT 
INTERSECTIONS AND CORRIDORS
Among the seven intersections assessed for crash data over 
five years, Gokhale junction recorded the highest number 
of crashes, totaling 18. Despite a tactical trial conducted 
in December 2019, the BMC is yet to implement the 
design at this location. Following closely behind were 
Nagpada with 16 crashes, Kalanagar with 13, and HP with 
8 (see Figure 49).
Nagpada, where the new design was introduced in January, 
experienced the highest number of crashes in 2019. Two 
main factors may have contributed to this surge: first, 
only 48 percent of the proposed design was executed. 
Only 50 percent of the proposed footpath extensions, 
pedestrian crossings, road markings, and bollards were 
realized on-site, with crucial elements like raised crossings 
and speed humps left entirely unimplemented. This 
likely contributed to increased vehicle speeds, potentially 
escalating the severity of crashes in 2019. Second, the 
presence of street vendors before the implementation of 
the redesign may have helped calm traffic by partially 
blocking the street and slowing down vehicles, a known 
phenomenon—and their removal from the carriageway 
might have inadvertently led to higher vehicle speeds 
that year. However, we do not have concrete evidence to 
substantiate the impact of these two factors. Over time, 
access to the footpath around the garden space was barely 
visible due to barricading, which forced people to walk on 
the carriageway, exposing themselves to speeding traffic. 
Further detailed data analysis of each crash incident and 
the role of vendors is necessary to draw more definitive 
conclusions regarding the effectiveness of the design 
interventions at Nagpada junction.
Similarly, across the three corridors assessed for crash 
data, Lal Bahadur Shastri (LBS) Road had the highest 
number of reported crashes (86) in 2018–22, followed by

the Andheri-Ghatkopar Link Road (AGLR) with 53, 
and Bellasis Road with 34 (see Figure 50). The reason for 
this is not solely high crash risk along each corridor but 
also because the length of each intervention site varied. 
LBS saw changes proposed for 9.5 km, of which 6.74 
km were implemented in May 2022. AGLR had changes 
implemented in only 2.9 km out of the proposed 4.8 km 
in May 2023, and Bellasis Road had a total of 0.85 km 
implemented in April 2021. When considering crashes 
per km of intervention in 2018–22, the ranking is reversed, 
with Bellasis having 40 crashes per unit kilometer, followed 
by AGLR with 18, and finally LBS with 13 crashes per 
kilometer. Furthermore, since implementation in each case 
was completed after April 2021, crash data were available 
for more years before implementation than after. Hence, 
each site recorded more crashes before implementation in 
absolute terms. As AGLR was implemented in May 2023, 
beyond the timeframe of available data (2018–2022), there 
are no records for the post-implementation period.
ABSOLUTE NUMBER OF CRASHES AT 
INTERSECTIONS AND CORRIDORS
Nagpada witnessed a notable decrease in average annual 
crashes post-implementation, with a significant 45 percent 
reduction compared to before implementation. This 
decrease is primarily due to data availability, with four 
years of post-implementation data compared to only one 
year before. Similarly, the HP junction, implemented in 
January 2019, shows a similar data discrepancy. However, 
while the HP junction recorded a higher cumulative 
number of crashes post-implementation, it is important 
to note that 88 percent of the proposed redesign was 
executed there. Consequently, despite the higher crash 
count, the HP junction shows a more uniform distribution 
of crashes each year, resulting in a marginal 25 percent 
reduction in average annual crashes post-implementation 
(see Figure 51).

At Kalanagar junction, there is a reversal in the data 
scenario: four years of pre-implementation data 
are available compared to less than a year of post-
implementation. Despite a higher number of cumulative 
crashes pre-implementation, average annual crashes 
increased by 78 percent post-implementation. While 
65 percent of the proposed redesign was executed, the 
focus was primarily on extending and constructing two 
traffic islands, which reduced the junction area by 70 
percent. However, implementation of the remaining 
design elements was slated to resume after the 2022 
monsoon season but was halted entirely due to a change 
in government in July 2022. Consequently, many other 
proposed physical elements remained unimplemented. 
However, when considering average annual crashes along 
the three corridors, each site (excluding AGLR) saw a 
significant reduction in crashes post-intervention, with a 
69 percent reduction in crashes post-implementation at 
LBS, followed by 38 percent at Bellasis. Although Bellasis 
was implemented in April 2021, meaning crash data were 
available for a year-and-a-half after implementation, 
LBS was implemented in May 2022, resulting in only

seven months of post-implementation crash data. It is 
important to note that evaluating the effectiveness of 
these design interventions is more reliable when crash 
data for a comparable number of years before and after 
implementation are available. Future studies can include 
control sites with similar intersections to understand 
the effectiveness of the implementation through crash 
data analysis. 
B. Pedestrian lives saved due to design 
interventions
AT INTERSECTIONS 
Pedestrian involvement in crashes, fatalities, and serious 
injuries appear to have been reduced at improved 
intersections, suggesting that changes implemented at 
junctions contributed to saving lives.
Regarding crashes involving pedestrians at the six sample 
intersections, the results were far more positive, suggesting 
that the changes implemented on the ground contributed 
to saving lives (see Figure 52). At Bharatmata and Namaha 
junctions, there were zero crashes involving pedestrians for 
the period for which data were available (i.e., 2018–2022). 
As a result, they recorded no change in crashes involving 
pedestrians before and after implementation. At Nagpada 
junction, while 83 percent of crashes involving pedestrians 
occurred in the year of permanent design implementation, 
subsequent years saw a notable decrease of 50 percent. 
Despite only 48 percent of the proposed design being 
implemented and pedestrian compliance with designated 
routes and crossings assessed at 51 percent and 36 percent 
of the observed sample, respectively, the reduction in 
crashes involving pedestrians underscores the positive 
impact of the implemented changes at Nagpada.
Poddar and Kalanagar junctions, which had very few 
crashes before implementation, recorded zero incidents 
after the changes were made. Before implementation, 
Kalanagar witnessed only two pedestrian-related crashes. 
Both crashes occurred in 2018, four years before changes 
were implemented on the ground. Similarly, Poddar had 
one crash involving pedestrians, in 2018, two years before 
permanent implementation. Meanwhile, the HP junction 
saw one pre-implementation crash involving pedestrians 
in 2018 and another three years post-implementation in 
2022, therefore resulting in a 75 percent reduction in the 
annual average of crashes involving pedestrians. While 
this highly positive reduction can be attributed to the 
successful implementation of 88 percent of the proposed 
design, it is based on just two incidents over a period 
of five years. Further research is therefore required to 
strengthen the claim.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 145
It is important to note that across these intersections, 
the implemented designs reduced the burden on law 
enforcement by the traffic police. They reported that 
traffic channelization improved along with pedestrian 
safety, and as a result, fewer traffic personnel were 
deployed to manage the junctions post-implementation.
Among the seven intersections studied, Gokhale junction 
had the highest fatalities (3), and although a trial was 
conducted in December 2019, permanent implementation 
by the BMC is still pending. Kalanagar followed with two 
fatalities in 2022, the same year the proposed design was 
implemented. Nagpada, Namaha, and HP each recorded 
one fatality in 2021, while Bharatmata and Poddar had 
no fatalities during the data collection period from 
2018 to 2022 (see Figure 53). Since Nagpada, HP , and 
Kalanagar had no fatalities before implementation but 
recorded fatalities during or after implementation, the 
percentage change in average annual fatalities at these 
sites before and after implementation is mathematically 
undefined (the way we computed percentage change 
before and after implementation is by the formula (new-
old/old) x 100. While the increase is not mathematically 
undefined, the percentage change formula puts 0 in 
the denominator, which can be read as mathematically 
undefined or infinite based on preference). Only Namaha, 
implemented in March 2021, reported a 100 percent 
reduction in average annual fatalities, which can be 
attributed to 90 percent implementation of the proposed 
design at Namaha. This is also reflected in the survey 
results, where 86 percent of surveyed pedestrians reported 
feeling safe while accessing Namaha junction. Further 
data on individual crashes leading to these fatalities 
are necessary to conclusively assess the effectiveness 
of the implemented junction redesigns in saving lives.

Across the seven intersections, Kalanagar recorded 
the highest number of crashes resulting in serious 
injuries (10), followed by Gokhale and Nagpada, each 
recording nine such crashes between 2018 and 2022. 
Even though Kalanagar recorded more crashes resulting 
in serious injuries before implementation compared to 
after implementation cumulatively, there was no change 
in average annual crashes resulting in injuries since 
post-implementation data were available for only nine 
months of 2022. Nagpada, implemented in January 2019, 
recorded a significant drop of 69 percent in average 
annual crashes resulting in injuries. HP , implemented 
in the same month, also recorded a reduction of 25 
percent. The proposed design for both Bharatmata and 
Poddar junctions was implemented in June 2020, with 
data available for an equivalent number of years before 
and after implementation. Since both junctions recorded 
twice as many crashes resulting in serious injury after 
implementation compared to before, average annual 
crashes resulting in serious injury recorded an increase 
of 35 percent and 100 percent, respectively. Namaha, 
implemented in March 2021, saw a significant increase 
of 350 percent in average annual crashes resulting in 
serious injuries, despite 90 percent implementation of the 
proposed design. 
Despite a 350 percent increase in average annual crashes 
resulting in serious injuries, Namaha saw no serious 
injuries involving pedestrians post-implementation, 
suggesting that the physical changes implemented at 
Namaha may have benefited pedestrians navigating the 
junction. Similarly, Bharatmata, which also experienced 
a 33 percent increase, had no serious injuries involving 
pedestrians. In comparison, Poddar had a 100 percent 
increase in serious injuries overall but saw a 100 percent 
reduction in average annual crashes resulting in serious 
injuries to pedestrians. Similarly, Kalanagar experienced 
a 100 percent reduction in average annual serious injuries

involving pedestrians, followed by HP (75 percent) and 
Nagpada (50 percent) (see Figure 54). These findings 
indicate that despite increases in crashes, fatalities, and 
serious injuries at intersections after implementation, 
those involving pedestrians decreased across all sites. 
This evidence suggests that physical changes like 
aligning lanes, geometric corrections, median refuges, 
refuge islands, and marked crossings could potentially 
enhance pedestrian safety in navigating intersections. 
ALONG CORRIDORS
Design changes implemented along improved corridors 
decreased pedestrian involvement in crashes resulting in 
fatalities and serious injuries.
Regarding the annual average number of crashes 
involving pedestrians, both LBS and Bellasis recorded a 
high proportion of crashes involving pedestrians before 
implementation, comprising 67 percent and 77 percent 
of total crashes, respectively. LBS experienced a notable 
increase in crashes involving pedestrians from 57 percent 
in 2018 to 79 percent in 2019, which partially could be 
linked to the onset of Mumbai Metro Line 4 construction 
in mid-2018. The pandemic years of 2020 and 2021 
saw reductions in overall crashes and crashes involving 
pedestrians due to lockdowns. After implementing LBS in 
May 2022, only one crash involving pedestrians occurred 
in the seven months of available post-implementation 
data, representing a 91 percent reduction in the annual 
average number of such crashes. Similarly, Bellasis 
recorded significantly fewer crashes involving pedestrians 
post-implementation in April 2021, representing a 60 
percent reduction in cumulative and average annual terms 
(see Figure 55).
Across the three main corridors, LBS stood out with 
the highest number of fatal crashes (12) between 2018 
and 2022, followed by AGLR (6) and Bellasis (2). One 
of the two fatal crashes at Bellasis occurred in 2019, 
before the proposed design was implemented, while the 
other took place in 2021, coinciding with the year of 
implementation. Although Bellasis saw higher fatalities 
post-implementation, with a 50 percent increase in 
average annual fatalities, none of these crashes involved 
pedestrians, resulting in a zero percent change in 
fatal crashes involving pedestrians before and after 
implementation. LBS, however, saw a spike in fatal crashes 
in 2019, with nine occurring amidst the construction of 
Mumbai Metro Line 4, a majority (78 percent) involving 
pedestrians. Subsequently, LBS recorded one fatal crash in

C. Estimated lives saved
The design improvements implemented at the sample 
intersections and corridors are estimated to have saved 
hundreds of lives.
The WRI presents the Lives Saved Report annually to 
showcase the progress of WRI’s work on safer streets in 16 
cities, as part of BIGRS. This internal report estimates the 
number of lives saved by specific road safety interventions, 
including (i) physical changes to street infrastructure, (ii) 
guidelines and publications, (iii) training, workshops and 
capacity building, and (iv) road safety plans, policies, and 
laws. Lives saved estimations for site-specific infrastructure 
interventions are based on several variables, including 
site-level historical crash data, citywide collision trends, 
baseline traffic counts in each city, and the type of new 
road safety infrastructure built due to WRI’s support. 
While challenges of availability, quality, and completeness 
of collected data persist, methods using variables of

completed projects were applied to estimate the number 
of lives saved by 2023, 2025, 2030, and 2040. For a note 
on methodology, formulae used, and the full list of crash 
reduction factors, see Appendix F. 
The highest estimated number of lives potentially saved 
due to design implementation was along LBS Road.
According to WRI India’s calculations, the highest 
estimated number for lives potentially saved due to the 
on-the-ground implementation of proposed designs 
is observed at the 6.74 kilometers implemented along 
LBS Road. Although the proposed design at LBS was 
implemented in May 2022, various proposed elements 
were only gradually implemented between 2017 and 2023 
because of the ongoing metro construction. Based on 
evaluations conducted in 2020, this resulted in potentially 
saving 23 lives by 2023, 33 by 2025, 56 by 2030, and 105 
by 2040 as a direct outcome of WRI India’s proposed 
design (see Figure 58). 
Among intersections, the highest estimated number for 
lives potentially saved due to design implementation was 
at the HP junction.
The next highest estimate for lives potentially saved was 
at the HP junction, where 88 percent of the proposed 
design was implemented on the ground by January 2019, 
potentially saving 74 lives by 2040. In contrast, Nagpada,

Five junctions in sample Two arterial roads in sample
implemented in the same month as HP , saw only 48 
percent of the proposed design implemented, resulting 
in the lowest estimate of lives potentially saved—18 by 
2040. At Namaha, despite 90 percent of the proposed 
design being implemented in March 2021, the estimate 
was only marginally better than at Nagpada, with 20 
lives potentially saved by 2040. Both Bharatmata and 
Poddar were implemented in June 2020, but designs 
at Poddar have a higher potential to save lives (36 by 
2040) than at Bharatmata (24 by 2040). This discrepancy 
could be attributed to 85 percent of the proposed design 
being implemented at Poddar, while only 67 percent was 
implemented at Bharatmata. 
If these design improvements can be sustained on 
the ground, their potential to save lives can increase 
dramatically. Using 2022 as the baseline, WRI India’s 
interventions across five intersections and two corridors 
could increase the number of lives potentially saved by up 
to 600 percent by 2040 (see Figure 59). 
Evaluations based on crash, fatality, and injury data, along 
with lives saved estimates, have a significant limitation 
since they rely primarily on baselines for traffic crash data. 
Such approaches are reactive as a substantial number of 
crashes must be recorded before action can occur, requiring 
a minimum of three to five years of crash data before and 
after implementation for a robust evaluation. This creates 
a paradox wherein road safety practitioners must wait for 
crashes to occur to prevent them, hindering safety analysis 
on a larger scale, particularly in countries like India that 
have poor-quality and often unreliable crash data. In recent 
years, advancements in sensing technologies and statistical 
modeling have made the widespread adoption of traffic 
conflict analysis as a surrogate for crashes both feasible 
and cost-effective. The underlying assumption is that the 
process leading to a serious conflict is almost identical 
to that of a serious crash, thus providing insight into the 
mechanisms behind road collisions. However, cities like 
Mumbai and institutions like WRI are yet to implement 
traffic conflict analysis to evaluate the effectiveness of 
safer-street interventions.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 151
Perceived safety at select local 
sites
Across the same five sites where pedestrians were 
mapped and motorists’ behaviors observed, intercept 
surveys were conducted with a sample of 768 pedestrians, 
approximately 150 at each location. The survey used six 
specific categories: Extremely safe, Safe, Partially safe and 
partially unsafe, Neither safe nor unsafe, Unsafe, Extremely 
unsafe. For analytical simplification, the responses were 
collapsed as follows: 1. Safe (combining Extremely safe 
and Safe), 2. Partially safe and partially unsafe, 3. Neither 
safe nor unsafe, and 4. Unsafe (combining Extremely 
unsafe and Unsafe).
According to the findings of this survey, a significant 74 
percent, regardless of their location, felt safe navigating 
the junctions and corridors in the sample. 
A. Perceptions of safety
Overall, 75 percent of surveyed pedestrians (n=455) felt 
“extremely safe” or “safe at junctions, while 73 percent felt 
“extremely safe” or “safe” at corridors (n=313).
Key contributing reasons cited for feeling safe were 
mostly social factors, such as the presence of people 
(44 percent), familiarity with surroundings (9 percent), 
physical street design interventions (9 percent), 
surveillance (8 percent), and street activities (5 percent) 
(see Figure 60). Notably, only 9 percent of respondents 
specifically attributed their feelings of safety to the 
presence of physical street design interventions, 
indicating that at these sites in Mumbai, social factors 
rather than physical design features determined their 
perception of safety. However, the physical features of a 
setting, when supportive of behaviors people associate 
with the social life of public spaces, often become 
imperceptible. That, too, is a measure of the effectiveness 
of physical change.
B. Perceptions of feeling unsafe
The survey also revealed that a significant proportion, 20 
percent of pedestrians surveyed, irrespective of their age 
group, felt “extremely unsafe” to “partially unsafe,” while 
6 percent did not have any specific perception of safety. 
The reasons for feeling “unsafe“ included over-speeding 
vehicles (24 percent), lack of security (15 percent), 
non-compliance with traffic rules (14 percent), difficulty 
in crossing (10 percent), high vehicular volume (10 
percent), insufficient lighting at night (10 percent), and a 
lack of traffic management (10 percent).
The reasons for feeling “partially unsafe” included people 
not following traffic rules (19 percent), over-speeding 
of vehicles (14 percent), and high vehicular volume (9.5 
percent). Only 3.7 percent of respondents identified the 
absence of physical street design elements as a reason 
for feeling “partially unsafe”. Thirty-one percent of 
female respondents, as compared to 21 percent of male 
respondents, either feel “extremely safe” to “partially 
unsafe” or do not have a definite perception when it 
comes to safety. 
While some of these are behavioral issues, they can 
also be addressed through design and enforcement 
interventions. For instance, speeding, which accounts for 
almost 50 percent of all road crashes in Mumbai (National 
Crime Records Bureau 2020), can be addressed through 
narrowing carriageway spaces and installing traffic-calming 
measures such as speed humps and rumble strips—both 
elements suggested during the proposal stage at each of 
the sample sites. 
DRIVING HIGH-LEVEL AND 
ULB-LEVEL ACTIONABLE 
POLICY FRAMEWORKS
In 2006, the NUTP spotlighted the need for NMT 
infrastructure in Indian cities, but few organizations 
at that time had the expertise to provide relevant 
technical solutions.
In 2011, EMBARQ India, supported by Bloomberg 
Philanthropies, began studying mobility challenges in 
various Indian cities, including Mumbai. Through a series 
of systemic engagements, they drove several actionable 
policy frameworks that, over time, led to changes in 
policies, municipal budgetary allocations, and institutional 
practices within Mumbai’s government agencies.

152 I wri-india.org
Changes to policies
A. High-Powered Committee (HPEC) on 
Mumbai’s traffic
The public discourse on safe mobility in Mumbai was 
significantly influenced by PILs in 2010 and 2013, leading 
to the formation of the high-powered committee (HPEC) 
on Mumbai’s traffic. This committee aimed to find 
long-term, comprehensive solutions to enhance vehicular 
and pedestrian movement in the city. Having built its 
credibility as a strong technical solution provider to urban 
mobility challenges, EMBARQ India was invited to be 
part of HPEC. In its report, HPEC acknowledged for the 
first time, among other things, how “the lack of good street 
design features including proper sidewalks and crosswalks 
forces pedestrians to use the carriageway meant for 
vehicles,” (Rajan et al. 2013, 13) thereby endangering their

safety and the safety of other road users across Mumbai 
city. Among the proposed actions was the implementation 
of pedestrian accessibility plans crafted by EMBARQ 
India for the MIDC area in Marol. This initiative aimed 
to convert the industrial zone into a transit-friendly and 
pedestrian-friendly business hub, leveraging new metro 
connectivity. The HPEC recommendations prompted the 
BMC to create and adopt the Pedestrian First Footpath 
Policy (2014). While the introduction of new policies 
by itself does not guarantee success, it does indicate the 
mainstream acceptance of emerging ideas.
B. Mumbai’s Development Plan (2014–
2034)
WRI India provided technical inputs on TOD, parking 
regulations, and safe access around station areas for the 
Mumbai Development Plan (2014–2034), the only

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 153
statutory plan shaping and regulating urban development 
in the city. After the earlier 2012 draft development 
plan was scrapped due to public objections, the revised 
draft development plan (RDDP) scrutinized the 
pedestrianization potential in Mumbai across 24 wards, 
particularly outside transit stations. For the RDDP , 
AIILSG appointed planners for each ward, whose area-
specific pedestrianization suggestions were adopted into 
the statutory document and routinely considered in annual 
street improvement work undertaken through the BMC 
budget. Through persistent systemic engagement by WRI 
India and various other agencies, safe-street principles 
became part of statutory documents such as the RDDP .
C. Mumbai Climate Action Plan (MCAP) 
2022
In 2020, Mumbai joined the C40 Cities global network 
to undertake city-scale climate action planning. In 
2021, WRI India partnered with C40 and the BMC to 
develop the Mumbai Climate Action Plan (MCAP) as a 
comprehensive strategy to tackle the challenges of climate 
change by adopting inclusive and robust mitigation and 
adaptation action. Amongst the key action areas under 
MCAP was achieving sustainable mobility through 
strategies to enhance public transport accessibility and 
inclusive planning for non-motorized transport, while 
enabling a shift from private to public modes. The MCAP 
emphasized parking management and safer intersections 
with pedestrian and cycling infrastructure to alleviate 
congestion, enhance commuter safety, and improve air 
quality. WRI India’s extensive work on safer streets over a 
decade in Mumbai informed each of these action areas. 
Changes to municipal budgets 
and institutional practices
Analysis of BMC budgets from 2010 to 2024 revealed a 
total allocation of approximately INR 3600 crores or $4.3 
billion to the Roads & Traffic Department for street works, 
primarily focused on routine construction and maintenance 
like resurfacing, concretization and widening of roads, 
filling potholes, and building flyovers and foot overbridges, 
among others (BMC 2010, 2011, 2012, 2013, 2014a, 
2015 2016, 2017, 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021a, 2022b, 2023a). 
Specific allocations for improving pedestrian safety and 
accessibility were absent until 2015, when the Pedestrian 
First Footpath Policy was enacted. Between 2015 and 
2018, budget documents mentioned improvement of 
footpaths and junctions more often, but the emphasis 
remained on aesthetics and encroachment-free footpaths. 
It took nearly four years post-policy implementation for 
dedicated footpath allocations to materialize. Between 
2018 and 2024, INR 820 crores ($97.5 million), a mere 
2.3 percent of the entire Roads & Traffic Department 
budget between 2010 and 2024, was allocated for footpath 
improvement and walking infrastructure. See Figure 61 for 
a summary of BMC budget allocations in 2010–2024 for 
improving citywide pedestrian infrastructure.
Notably, shaped by the efforts such as the BIGRS initiative 
in 2018, INR 160 crores ($19.3 million) were allocated 
for LBS Road’s redesign, including four-meter-wide 
footpaths, a project driven by WRI India, Bloomberg, and 
BMC collaboration.
In 2019, a major shift occurred when BMC decided to 
concretize footpaths instead of using paver blocks, and 
even though the focus was mostly on material change, 
it provided an opportunity for WRI India to suggest 
geometric corrections in footpath and junction alignments. 
In 2021, the successful implementation of the SATS pilot 
allowed WRI India to inform the city’s approach to street 
improvements by prioritizing the needs of vulnerable 
users, particularly school-going children. Consequently, 
the 2022–23 BMC budget allocated INR 50 crores ($6 
million) for the Safe Access to Schools (SATS) program. 
Most recently, the BMC’s empanelment of urban design 
consultants led to a 2023–24 BMC budget announcing an 
allocation of INR 200 crores ($23.8 million) for pedestrian 
facilities across the city with a comprehensive mapping of 
the existing conditions along all major roads.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 155
...This year BMC will map the Footpath facilities 
for all major roads of more than 9-meter width and 
wherever footpath is found to be absent or not in good 
shape, new CC [cement concrete] footpath with smooth 
walking quality and international surface designs will be 
constructed in fast-track mode. BMC Roads department 
has also empaneled urban road designers who will design 
these footpaths. Once constructed, these footpaths will be a 
pleasure to walk for citizens of Mumbai. A token provision 
of 200 crore has been kept for this. (BMC 2023).
Beyond nudges impacting institutional practices 
and leveraging municipal budgetary allocations for 
programmatic engagements such as the SATS program, 
WRI India’s approach to design interventions leveraged 
additional municipal finance of more than INR 220 crores 
($26.2 billion) for permanent design implementation at 
local intervention sites.
The first-ever Climate Budget for Mumbai (2024–25), 
prepared with technical assistance from WRI India, aligns 
INR 10,224.24 crores ($1.2 billion)—that is, 32.18 percent 
of the BMC’s capital budget expenditure—with the 
MCAP’s recommendations in six priority sectors and 24 
action tracks (BMC 2024). Improving NMT infrastructure 
while ensuring increased pedestrian safety and inclusive 
access to mass transit through better last-mile connectivity 
is a key action track identified in the MCAP to reduce 
GHG emissions in the city. In alignment with this action 
track, the Roads & Traffic Department of the BMC 
pledged to improve footpaths to ensure safe and inclusive 
pedestrian infrastructure.
GREATER AWARENESS OF 
THE ROLE OF DESIGN IN 
SHAPING SAFE STREETS, 
ESPECIALLY FOR THE 
MOST VULNERABLE 
STREET USERS (I.E., 
PEDESTRIANS)
The continuous work on improving road safety and 
pedestrian infrastructure in Mumbai’s streets through 
urban design interventions was supported by Bloomberg 
Philanthropies, initially through a more open-ended grant 
to EMBARQ India between 2009 and 2014 and from 
2015 onwards through the BIGRS program. This decade-
long effort shifted citywide safe pedestrian infrastructure 
from a nascent issue in 2011–13 to an emerging one 
between 2014 and 2020, eventually gaining mainstream 
recognition in 2021.
Mumbai’s 2014 City Mobility Plan demonstrated that 
poorer people are the primary users of non-motorized 
transport and pay the price of walking on unsafe streets. 
An analysis of the location of the design interventions for 
safe streets showed that the proposed interventions were 
within one kilometer (a distance that the poor regularly 
cover on foot) of 21.8 percent of Greater Mumbai’s 
slums and 500 meters of 8.2 percent of the city’s slums. 
Similarly, the safe-street design interventions implemented 
on the ground were located within a one-kilometer 
walking distance of 11.8 percent of Greater Mumbai’s 
slums and 500 meters of 4.2 percent of the city’s slums 
(see Figure 62).

Changes in stakeholder 
perceptions and mental models
WRI India got involved in street design work with LBS 
Marg under BIGRS in 2015 as a direct result of the 
relationships built during the Equal Streets program in the 
previous years. Impressed by the program’s demonstration 
of streets as vital public spaces for people, not just 
for moving vehicles, the then Additional Municipal 
Commissioner suggested that WRI India work with 
municipal engineers to redesign LBS Marg along safe-
street principles. He said, as recalled by the WRI India 
staff leading the road safety work in Mumbai, “Even if 
engineers don’t like you, they have to tolerate you—it is 
up to you to make the best of the opportunity.” Seizing 
this opportunity of greater access to the municipal system, 
WRI India developed an understanding over the next two 
to three years of how the system works and the process 
to be followed for sensitizing municipal engineers to the 
importance of good street design in saving lives. Around 
the same time, the BMC was going to undertake the 
improvement of the HP junction in Bandra, hearing 
which WRI India submitted a design proposal for a 
trial run. Leveraging relationships built with the MTP 
during Equal Streets(2014–17), particularly with the 
then Additional Commissioner of Police, Traffic for 
Western Suburbs, who by 2017 had been promoted to 
JCP , Traffic, for Mumbai city, WRI India also obtained 
permission for 100 barricades to conduct the first-ever 
tactical trial of an intersection’s redesign in India. Using 
paint and barricades, the HP junction was transformed 
overnight on April 1, 2017. The JCP monitored the trial 
through CCTV feeds in his office and provided feedback 
that the junction was functioning better than before. This 
high-level endorsement was a critical turning point for 
key stakeholders such as the BMC, the police, and the 
ward to recognize the value of design in improving both 
pedestrian and vehicular mobility. The trial established 
WRI India as a trusted technical advisor and led to 
increased demand for design-led improvements of streets 
and intersections in Mumbai.
A. Blackspot mitigation (BSM)
In 2018, when the state PWD, Municipal Corporation 
of Greater Mumbai (MCGM), and MTP formed 
a Blackspot Mitigation task force under BIGRS, 
WRI India once again gave technical inputs and 
corrective recommendations for 9 out of 52 blackspots 
identified by the task force (see Figure 63). The 
recommendations included:
 ▪ compact intersections that are safe for 
vulnerable road users;
 ▪ speed-calming measures near intersections; and
 ▪ good-quality pedestrian infrastructure, including 
pedestrian crossings at grade, with signalization 
based on the time required for pedestrians to cross 
multiple lanes.
The MTP routinely identifies blackspots in Mumbai 
based on the methodology set up by the Ministry of Road 
Transport and Highways. While implementation of the 
recommendations for most blackspots made by WRI 
India is still pending, the continuous emergence of “Top 
20 Blackspot” locations every year necessitates prioritizing 
high-risk intersections through BSM.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 161
B. Safe Access to Schools (SATS)
Between 2017 and 2019, a 500-meter area around 
Christ Church school recorded 23 crashes and three 
fatalities, including two seriously injured children and a 
third child that succumbed to their injuries. During the 
same period, 28 percent of Mumbai’s 2,610 municipal 
school campuses experienced over three fatalities within 
a 500-meter walking radius. In October 2021, WRI 
India, under BIGRS and in partnership with the BMC, 
MTP , and Christ Church School, implemented a safer-
school zone in the Byculla neighborhood, using tactical 
urbanism tools. Students participated in the design process, 
resulting in clearly designated walking and waiting areas, 
pick-up and drop-off zones demarcated through signage 
and road markings, child-friendly spaces with playful 
elements, and a vibrant pedestrian crossing (see Figure 
64). Intercept surveys conducted before and during the 
trial showed a positive perception shift, with 98 percent of 
school children feeling safer, 93 percent finding the street 
more accessible, and 68 percent reporting comfortable 
waiting spaces.
Although the pilot faced community pushback and was 
not fully implemented, its visual appeal, through vibrant 
colored patterns on the street, and its low cost and great 
speed sparked substantial interest, leading to requests for 
SATS interventions in different parts of the city. 
Following this successful pilot by WRI India, a Mumbai 
State Minister attempted to expand SATS citywide 
by launching the Safe Access to Schools Program for 
200 municipal schools in May 2022 and allocated INR 
50 crore ($6 million) for the program in the municipal 
budget. The BMC engaged five urban design consultants 
to redesign the access streets for 25 schools, with WRI 
India providing technical oversight. Discussions with 
the Minister of Urban Development, Government of 
Maharashtra were also initiated to extend the program 
statewide. Municipal commissioners from other cities were 
invited to a meeting where WRI India’s presentation on 
SATS generated interest in replicating the program in 
various cities in Maharashtra. Though initially imagined 
as a capacity-building program, WRI India made 
extensive efforts toward a statewide proposal for tactical 
interventions across different cities to be completed before 
schools reopened after the summer break. Even though 
SATS was a BMC project, with the change in state 
government, high-level municipal corporation officials 
are typically transferred, and priorities also change, which 
leads to the slowing down of non-routine projects. This 
highlights the challenges of introducing design-led 
initiatives in public administration without firmly aligning 
with established city-making agendas such as decongestion 
or city beautification that have earmarked budgets.
FIGURE 64  |   Safe Access to Schools (SATS) tactical trial at Christ Church School, Byculla, in October 2021
Photo Credit:  Ameya Mahale.

162 I wri-india.org
C. Mumbai Street Lab
WRI India collaborated with the BMC to launch the 
Mumbai Street Lab (MSL) in 2019 to promote a design-
led approach to street improvements. MSL was a first-of-
its-kind design competition that sought to introduce more 
urban designers into the system. Hosted by the BMC 
with technical support from WRI India, the competition 
selected five urban design consultants to collaborate with 
BMC on five important street redesign projects. Trials 
were conducted for three out of five winning proposals 
for street design, namely Vikhroli Park Site Road No. 
17, Rajaram Mohan Roy Road, and Nepean Sea Road. 
Eventually, only the proposals for the Nepean Sea Road 
were partly implemented by the BMC (see Figure 65). 
While the competition officially acknowledged only five 
winners, it opened the doors for further engagement with 
other consultants. The top 20 proposals were presented 
in an exhibition attended by different departments, 
ward officials, and the public. The exhibition successfully 
showcased the transformative potential of urban design, 
leading the Municipal Commissioner to announce 20 
additional streets for improvement in Phase 2. A second 
exhibition of the entries was staged, and the design 
proposals were presented directly to the Chief Minister. 
However, Phase 2 faced challenges and stalled due to 
the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic and a change 
in administration. Nonetheless, MSL winners received 
recognition for their work and continued to collaborate 
with the city on street transformation projects.
BMC engineers critiqued the MSL proposals, noting 
their conceptual brilliance but questioning their 
practicality for implementation given Mumbai’s traffic 
conditions. Concerns centered on the challenges of 
altering recently laid concrete roads from the perspective of 
accountability of public funds and on reducing carriageway 
capacity by widening footpaths, reflecting a prevailing 
mindset of prioritizing cars in street-space distribution. 
There were some good concepts from urban designers, but 
they were not practical under the situation at the ground 
level. For example, in some proposals, an increase in the 
footpath width was proposed, resulting in a decrease in 
the width of the carriageway. Mumbai has lots of traffic, 
and there is much congestion due to traffic. Since there is 
more traffic congestion, we require more carriageway for 
it. Moreover, the traffic congestion situation is all over the 
city, hence none of the proposals could be implemented on 
the ground. Therefore, we need design proposals that are 
practically feasible in the context of the city’s streets. There 
is heavy traffic, and proposals must help ease the traffic.   
—BMC Official, September 13, 2022.  
FIGURE 65  |  Footpath improvement at Nepean Sea Road, captured February 2024
Photo Credit:  Akanksha Aggarwal.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 163
The consultants hesitated to alter their design proposals, 
fearing that their unique concepts would be compromised. 
In contrast, WRI India’s strategy involved conducting trials 
and collaborating with the system to minimize disruptions 
during implementation. As a result of this practical and 
flexible approach, more of WRI India’s proposals were 
readily implemented by the BMC than were those of the 
consultants and peer organizations.
D. Mumbai Streets Transformation 
Program (MSTP)
To sustain the momentum and scale ongoing efforts, 
WRI India proposed the Mumbai Streets Transformation 
Program (MSTP) to empanel urban designers with the 
BMC for citywide street design rather than individual 
pilot projects. MSTP is a landmark INR 450 crore ($53.5 
million) project by the BMC, targeting the retrofitting of 
300 kilometers of municipal streets in Mumbai. In 2022, 
WRI India’s street-safety work achieved a significant 
milestone when the BMC issued its first tender for the 
empanelment of urban design consultants for street 
improvement projects in the city. Despite resistance from 
local architects, financial hurdles, and administrative 
delays, the BMC successfully empaneled six urban design 
consultants in November 2022. The 2023–24 BMC budget 
allocated INR 200 crores ($23.8 million) for citywide 
footpath improvements to be executed with the support of 
these empaneled consultants. However, the 2022 political 
transition shifted priorities again; slowing down street 
improvement works. This reflects a common challenge 
where long-term programs can lose momentum during 
transitions. The MSTP , though stalled, had shown strong 
potential to provide a structured pathway for scaling up 
safe and inclusive street design.

164 I wri-india.org
UNINTENDED 
CONSEQUENCES
WRI India’s approach to creating safe streets in 
Mumbai had adopted principles of complete streets to 
democratize the use of streets from just being designed 
for cars to privileging pedestrians in a city where most 
trips are undertaken by walking. However, critics of the 
complete streets movement asked, “Complete for whom?” 
questioning the extent to which age, ability, income, 
ethnicity, or mode of travel had been considered in the 
complete street design (Zavestoski and Agyeman 2015). 
Even though WRI India’s approach had been to work 
with existing street improvement processes by pushing 
and testing street-design ideas and securing stakeholder 
buy-ins before shepherding the implementation through 
the system, in our research, we also encountered similar 
sentiments among leading urban thinkers in Mumbai. 
Indian streets are never about clarity; they are this mixed 
bag of things. What is the relevant form of street to hold 
complex and diverse forms of street life? We try to clarify 
[through design] rather than hold things together. So, our 
argument has been that the more we clarify, the more 
it will exclude.... You know it will look good, but for 
whom?... The thing is to understand that cities are not 
resolved or settled places. They will be contested. There 
will be a constant dynamic of the contest, which goes on.”        
—Noted Urbanist, Mumbai, March 17, 2023.
The most significant achievement of the physical design 
was reclaiming public space from vehicular movement 
spaces. However, such spaces are not without contestation 
by different interest groups, including those who have been 
directly evicted from them, such as vendors and parked 
vehicles, or those who believe that these public spaces 
need protection from evictees who will eventually come 
back to claim them.
In one of the intersections, fear of homeless people 
claiming the large, reclaimed plaza led to bizarre 
deliberations by important local stakeholders on designing 
for exclusion, such as the installation of sharp spikes to 
deter homeless individuals from seeking shelter or sleeping 
there. Equally, private and public agendas for public 
spaces, most notably through city beautification agendas, 
have claimed the reclaimed public spaces, making them 
less accessible to pedestrians and creating visual barriers, 
as seen in the case of the HP and Bharatmata junctions 
(discussed further in the section “Lessons learned”).
The idea of clarifying spaces for primary uses, such 
as walking on sidewalks without providing for the 
spillover activities from the uses at the edges, has 
led to the claiming of sidewalk space by shopfronts 
and vendors as unintended consequences but are 
equally attempts to reclaim the right to the city 
by people who were designed out (see Figure 66). 
FIGURE 66  |  Claiming of sidewalk space by shopfronts and vendors on LBS road
Photo Credit:  Saurabh Jain.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 165
Development of social 
infrastructure as an unintended 
consequence
Surprisingly, unintended consequences have also 
expanded public space use in an intersection deemed 
incomplete from a design implementation perspective. The 
incomplete redevelopment of the Nagpada intersection 
notwithstanding, the flagpole installation in the beautified 
plaza infused the space with new currency for events and 
gatherings. Despite the project implementation steering 
away from the original intent, it helped generate media 
coverage (Pinto 2017; Bhatt and Ashar 2017; The Hindu 
2018; The Guardian 2018; Deshpande 2019) and triggered 
multiple other improvements near the junction, such as 
the public reading space, or vachanalaya, on the sidewalk 
(see Figure 67), improvement of the garden area close to 
the junction, and the redesign of Bellasis Road, one of 
the arms of Nagpada junction. The project also helped 
the team gain and maintain the trust of the corporator 
and the ward, leading to other, longer-term engagements 
such as support for the SATS project in the same 
ward later in 2022.
Unintended consequence of 
physical design
Over the last decade, Mumbai’s road network 
expansion and street widening efforts, following the 
recommendations of the 2016 CMP and 2034 RDDP , 
have resulted in several unintended consequences, both 
positive and negative. For instance, the street edges were 
extended while widening the Linking Road, but existing 
trees could not be moved. As a result, footpaths were 
positioned behind the tree line, causing the trees to divide 
the space between the footpath and the carriageway. This 
increased the crossing distance for pedestrians, turning 
the simple task of crossing the road into a safety hazard. 
These gaps have also become spaces for parking and 
dumping garbage.
Similarly, in 2017 and 2018, parts of the Andheri 
Ghatkopar Link Road (AGLR) were widened to increase 
its capacity with the hope that this would ease congestion, 
smooth traffic flow, and address the flooding issue near the 
entrances of metro stations along the corridor. However, 
the support pillars beneath the metro stations
FIGURE 67  |  Reading room (vachanalaya) at Nagpada intersection
Photo Credit:  Saurabh Jain.

166 I wri-india.org
restricted uniformity in road widening across the entire 
corridor. This resulted in variations in the right of way, 
resulting in carriageway widths ranging from 30 meters 
wide at some sections to 45.75 meters wide at others 
(see Figure 68).
Under these metro stations, the pillars limited the road 
to three traffic lanes on each side, while between metro 
stations they increased to four or five traffic lanes on 
each side (see Figure 69 and Figure 70 top). Though this 
expanded the capacity of the road in certain sections, 
the inconsistency or lack of uniformity in the width of 
the carriageway led to frequent bottlenecks and traffic 
jams, particularly under the metro stations. This has 
likely exacerbated the anxiety of metro users navigating 
the area under these stations on foot or searching 
for last-mile transport options. In 2021, when the 
street was revisited for improvements, the issue of the 
inconsistent carriageway provided an opportunity to 
reclaim the extra lanes causing bottlenecks and create 
pedestrian infrastructure in the form of wider footpaths. 
This was finally implemented in May 2023.
Given the vibrant nature of the AGLR, characterized by 
bustling street activities and high pedestrian footfall, the 
additional footpath space has brought about significant 
improvements, providing enhanced access to metro 
stations and making the daily commute more convenient 
and safer for pedestrians and metro users. The extended 
footpaths offer safer and more comfortable waiting areas 
at bus stops, reducing the risk of crashes and improving 
overall safety (see Figure 69 and Figure 70 bottom). 
Moreover, the extra space has been utilized for planting 
trees and increasing green cover, contributing to a more 
pleasant urban environment that beautifies the area, 
helps mitigate the effects of extreme heat, and improves 
air quality. The widened footpaths have also facilitated 
place-making efforts, allowing for the creation of small 
public spaces where people can gather, relax, and interact, 
fostering a sense of community.
These changes have greatly enhanced the walking 
experience along the AGLR corridor. Pedestrians now 
enjoy a more accessible, safer, and aesthetically pleasing 
environment, encouraging walking and supporting the 
vibrant street life that defines the area. The thoughtful 
reclamation of footpaths demonstrates how human-centric 
street design can positively impact daily life, making the 
city more livable and enjoyable for its residents.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 167

IMPLICATIONS FOR IMPACT 
TARGETS AND LIMITATIONS
The Theory of Change identified three impact targets, 
two of which were aligned with the SDGs, and one with 
WRI’s internal impact target. They are:
 ▪ SDG Target 3.6: Halve the number of global deaths 
and injuries from road traffic crashes by 2030;
 ▪ SDG Target 11.2: Provide safe, affordable, accessible, 
and sustainable transport systems for all, improving 
road safety, notably by expanding public transport, with 
special attention to the needs of those in vulnerable 
situations, women, children, persons with disabilities, 
and older persons;  
 ▪ WRI Intervention Target: By 2027, >300 km of safer 
roads will be implemented globally through direct 
WRI influence, including >200 intersections redesigned 
globally through direct WRI influence.
While these impact targets are global, each of WRI India’s 
approaches and outcomes, though local to Mumbai, were 
framed against these impact targets through the identified 
dimensions of urban transformations.
Through systemic engagement activities, WRI India 
and partners steered high-level and urban-local-body-
level policy frameworks, first through participation in 
the HPEC on Mumbai’s traffic, followed by inputs to 
Mumbai’s DP (2014–2034), and since 2020, through the 
framing of the MCAP in conjunction with C40. Alongside 
programmatic interventions like MSL, SATS, MSTP , and 
on-the-ground proof-of-concept pilots, key stakeholders 
within the BMC and MTP were able to see firsthand how 
simple changes like geometric correction and refuge islands 
can not only increase safety but also streamline and help 
manage vehicular traffic. This eventually helped shape their 
perceptions and mental models and enhanced the capacity 
of local government engineers and traffic police to address 
the global SDG impact target 3.6.
Through constant engagement with the government and 
non-government stakeholders in the city, WRI India was 
able to provide design recommendations for safer streets 
at 82 intersections and along 38 km of corridors and 
implement these recommendations at 21 intersections and 
along 20 km of corridors. Even though the interventions 
are localized, at a larger scale they significantly contribute 
to WRI’s internal intervention target of implementing 
more than 300 km of safer roads globally through direct 
WRI influence, including redesigning more than 200 
intersections globally through direct WRI influence 
by 2027. Given the proximity of safe-street design 
interventions to mobile underserved populations in the 
city (implemented designs located within a one-kilometer 
walking distance of 11.8 percent of Greater Mumbai’s 
slums and 500 meters of 4.2 percent of the city’s slums), 
one can also argue that the interventions benefited these 
underserved populations.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 171

172 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 6 
 
Sustainability 
This chapter examines the factors influencing the 
long-term sustainability of projects aimed at creating 
safer streets. It highlights uncertainties arising from 
rapidly evolving transport infrastructure, changing 
political and private sector priorities, and poor inter-
agency coordination. To sustain these efforts and 
fully realize their potential, a deeper understanding of 
system transitions and political change management 
is essential for effectively embedding innovative ideas 
within existing governance structures.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 173

Cities are dynamic entities undergoing constant change 
due to the pressures of urbanization, population growth, 
and diverse city-making agendas. Over the past decade, 
incessant changes to Mumbai’s built environment driven 
by various agendas have challenged the sustainability 
of initiatives aimed at creating safer streets. WRI India, 
along with other partners and stakeholders, has struggled 
to respond to these challenges, which often jeopardize 
the long-term effectiveness of efforts to create safer 
urban environments. Some of these challenges are 
discussed below.
RAPIDLY EVOLVING 
TRANSPORT 
INFRASTRUCTURE
Mumbai has prioritized adding new mass transit 
infrastructure for over two decades, starting with the 
MUIP . Big infrastructure projects typically have long 
construction timelines spanning several years, and 
their plans are susceptible to modifications and delays. 
The redevelopment of streets may face challenges in 
accommodating these mass transit projects as they often 
end up being driven by the vision and priorities of the 
larger infrastructure projects.
For instance, the planning and design of the LBS route 
in 2015 did not originally account for the introduction of 
a metro system. In fact, the construction of the Mumbai 
Metro Line 4 connecting Wadala to Kasarvadavali was 
planned after the safer design for the street was submitted. 
As a result, the metro construction coincided with the 
street’s redevelopment and compromised the execution of 
the proposed design. The metro work is still in progress, 
and the metro station exits will have to be integrated into 
the footpaths. Fortunately, the width of the footpaths 
is large enough to accommodate these metro exits 
without major disruptions. However, this was not the 
case everywhere.
At the HP junction, the ongoing Metro Line 2B work 
led to modifications of one of the traffic islands to 
accommodate additional carriageway space for vehicles, 
compromising waiting space for pedestrians at the 
junction. Also, existing medians along the metro line 
have been removed to add metro columns, resulting in 
unregulated vehicular movements that lead to pedestrian 
safety issues (see Figures 71 and 72).
FIGURE 71  |    Traffic island toward Bandra Station 
near the HP junction demolished in 
anticipation of columns for Metro Line 2 
(Yellow line) in October 2023
174 I wri-india.org
Photo Credit:  Yash Siroliya.

Other examples of disruptions due to large-scale transport 
infrastructure include planning new bus routes. In the case 
of Namaha junction, new bus routes were proposed in 
2022, crossing the junction from S.V. Road to the Rajguru 
flyover. Previously, only minibuses, including school buses, 
operated on this route. The R-South Ward reached out 
to WRI India in December 2022 seeking suggestions for 
modifications to the refuge island at Namaha junction. 
The team visited the site in January 2023 and submitted 
a modified proposal to accommodate the new bus routes. 
The geometry of the island next to Hotel Delicacy was 
changed as per WRI India’s recommendations. However, 
this allowed the engineers to make other modifications to 
the island as part of the beautification efforts. The islands 
were modified to add two enclosed spaces for statues and 
vegetation around them (see Figure 73).
FIGURE 72  |   Disruption to safe-street features at the HP junction due to metro line insertion
Currently, the Mumbai metro rail network is the sixth 
largest in the country, with an operational length of 46 km. 
However, the metro network is set to expand sevenfold, 
according to a masterplan prepared by MMRDA. In 2019, 
this master plan revealed that the expansion will cover 337 
km with 10 different metro lines. Future work on safer 
streets has to align with and account for the inclusion of 
metro infrastructure in streets and develop strong and 
practical guidelines for planning safe pedestrian access on 
such streets, ensuring seamless last-mile connectivity to 
and from the metro stations.
