178 I wri-india.org
PRIVATE AGENDAS
One of the most visible changes in the improved streets 
was the reclamation of carriageway space for large 
pedestrian plazas by rationalizing traffic lanes through 
geometric correction and ensuring safe pedestrian access. 
However, such public spaces are often sites of community 
conflict as some influential residents fear such spaces 
will be claimed by vendors, homeless people, or religious 
minorities. This was the case at the HP intersection, where 
there was a pushback from the community for shutting 
down the slip lane, leading to the BMC tweaking the 
design after implementation. This was done without WRI 
India’s knowledge.
Through their CSR initiatives, private players play a key 
role in street interventions. In 2021, during COVID-19 
lockdowns, a COVID warrior monument was set up 
using CSR funds at the public plaza at the HP junction. 
This further reduced vital public space at the center of 
the junction (see Figures 74 and 75). There was a similar 
interest expressed in the case of another junction in the 
city, where the ward officer was keen on improving the 
safety and accessibility of the junction as per WRI India’s 
recommendations and wanted to use CSR funds for the 
same. However, the CSR entity was more interested in 
using the money to install a COVID warrior statue at the 
junction and not in improving it holistically.     
Photo Credit:  Sudeshna Chatterjee.

FIGURE 75  |    Hindustan Petroleum (HP) junction before implemetation (top-left), during trial (bottom-left), after 
implementation (top-right) and after installation of the Covid warrior statue (bottom-right)
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 179
Source: WRI India.

Businesses often play a proactive role in maintaining the 
sidewalks outside their premises. They engage in micro-
beautification efforts such as placing planters to protect 
sidewalk spaces from encroachment by two-wheeler 
parking, thereby ensuring unrestricted pedestrian access 
in front of their shops. There have been efforts in the past 
to institutionalize these kinds of practices through local 
policies. The Saaf Aangan Scheme, initiated in 2002, is 
a case in point. The idea was promoted by 70-year-old 
lieutenant Madhu Sawant after he realized that he could 
not safely walk on the footpath near his house (The Times 
of India 2005). The scheme was simple and encouraged 
citizens to lease the footpath near their homes and 
office buildings from the BMC for the small sum of two 
rupees and maintain it. Once the ward office cleared their 
application, people were responsible for maintaining 
the footpath (Chopra 2013) and keeping it clear of any 
obstructions or encroachments. The corporation initially 
did not accept Sawant’s scheme, with some ward offices 
even demanding INR 25,000 ($297) as a bank guarantee 
before agreeing to the lease (The Times of India 2005). 
However, such schemes have never been evaluated to 
understand how inclusive they are for all classes of people 
and communities. It took a circular issued by the then 
municipal commissioner asking ward offices to clear all 
applications received under this scheme within 15 days 
for the idea to take off. While keeping footpaths outside 
private premises clean was taken up by a few residents in 
some parts of the city, it struggled to maintain momentum 
and was eventually withdrawn (Somani 2012). In Figure 
76, can be seen local efforts to ensure unrestricted 
pedestrian movement on Bellasis Road after its redesign.
FIGURE 76  |    Planters placed by local businesses to restrict parking on sidewalks and ensure unrestricted 
pedestrian movement on Bellasis Road
180 I wri-india.org
Photo Credit:  Rohit Tak.

CHANGING GOVERNMENT 
PRIORITIES
Improving the city’s aesthetics and visual appeal has always 
motivated political and administrative leaders to drive 
changes in the city’s public infrastructure. Before 2022, 
such initiatives were taken up in a fragmented manner 
across Mumbai through various tactics such as drives 
for plant cover on medians and refuge islands, lighting, 
painting of flyover columns and walls, and installation of 
sculptures, plaques, and statues, among others. However, 
in September 2022, with the launch of the massive INR 
FIGURE 77  |    The evolution of the pedestrian plaza at Bharatmata junction and th erosion of public space due to 
city beautification drives
1700 crore ($202.2 million) “Mumbai Beautification 
Project” by the Chief Minister of Maharashtra, many of 
the public open spaces on the streets, including junctions 
improved by the BMC to enhance safety and accessibility, 
became easy targets for beautification. The Bharatmata 
junction is one such example, where in 2023 the refuge 
island underwent modifications as part of the city 
beautification drive to include the addition of walls with 
railings, planters, and a statue, leaving only limited space 
for pedestrians to cross safely (see Figure 77). Moreover, 
the visibility of the slip lane was obstructed due to the 
height of the walls and planters, posing a safety risk at 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 181
Source: WRI India.

the location. There used to be a weekend market at the 
junction and the reclaimed island was meant to function 
as a plaza accommodating such activities. Modifications 
due to the beautification drive left limited space for social 
gatherings, eliminating the possibility of the weekend 
market continuing at the plaza.
ENCROACHMENT BY 
PARKING
Some footpath-widening exercises were carried out at 
the expense of reducing parking spaces. Following the 
implementation of safe-streets projects, illegal parking 
sometimes crops up on reclaimed pedestrian spaces for 
various reasons. Two-wheeler parking is most common, 
often seen outside shopfronts along LBS Road or beneath 
FIGURE 78  |    Parked two-wheelers encroaching on footpaths outside a metro station in Mumbai
metro stations on the AGLR Road (see Figure 78). 
Moreover, specific livelihoods depend on automobile-
related services, such as taxi services, including Ola and 
Uber, auto-rickshaws, and delivery persons. Open car-
servicing garages operated by street-based mechanics also 
contribute to encroachment on footpaths. During our 
documentation of activities, we observed that a sizeable 
portion of on-street parking along AGLR Road was 
occupied by tourist vehicles, especially those associated 
with Ola and Uber.
182 I wri-india.org
Photo Credit:  Saurabh Jain.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 183
FREQUENT EXCAVATIONS 
FOR REPAIRS AND 
MAINTENANCE
The absence of a ducting system for underground utilities 
poses a significant challenge for Mumbai’s streets, 
leading to frequent excavations for maintenance and 
repairs. The BMC’s trenching policy outlines guidelines 
for meticulously restoring excavated patches to their 
original condition after repairs. However, as these 
restoration efforts often fall short, they leave behind 
visibly uneven patches of sidewalks that compromise 
safety for pedestrians and road users (see Figure 79). 
To mitigate disruptions, utility agencies are required 
to submit yearly utility plans to their respective wards, 
coordinating closely with central agencies. The trenching 
work should ideally be scheduled just before a road is 
slated for improvement, minimizing the need for double 
work. The policy also stipulates procedures for necessary 
utility work when a road is not scheduled for improvement. 
The trenching work is meant to be conducted in phases, 
limited to no more than 300 meters at a time, to minimize 
inconvenience for street users. Despite these guidelines, 
adherence to the policy remains a significant challenge and 
instances have occurred where roads were dug up shortly 
after improvement.
FIGURE 79  |    The One Green Mile Project dug up for maintenance soon after completion
Source: WRI India.

184 I wri-india.org
Photo Credit: Saurabh Jain.
LACK OF DURABILITY OF 
MATERIALS USED FOR 
FOOTPATHS AND ROADS
Starting in 2002, BMC used paver blocks for footpaths 
and sometimes for roads. As a material, paver blocks 
present a more accessible and time-efficient alternative, 
and their interlocking design facilitates easy removal, 
which simplifies the process of reaching underground 
utilities for maintenance and renovations. However, the 
adoption of paver blocks is beset with many concerns. 
First, their uneven surface prompted numerous complaints, 
particularly from pedestrians, including the elderly and 
disabled, who face an increased risk of tripping and 
falling. Second, the lack of durability has resulted in 
frequent dislodgment or breakage of paver blocks, creating 
hazardous walking conditions (see Figure 80).
By 2014, opposition to paver blocks had gained 
momentum, prompting the BMC to cease using them 
on roads in 2016 in favor of more durable materials such 
as asphalt. Despite this shift, paver blocks continue to be 
used on footpaths.
INFLEXIBILITY OF 
CONCRETE ROADS 
AND UNINTENDED 
CONSEQUENCES
Based on a 2013 High Court suo moto PIL (S. Kumar 
et al. 2013) filed against the BMC regarding poor road 
conditions in Mumbai, the bench, in its 2016 order, 
observed that the BMC is under statutory obligation 
under the Mumbai Municipal Corporation Act, 1888, to 
maintain and improve public roads (The Asian Age 2016). 
In response, the BMC proposed the concretization of 
roads to tackle the challenge of frequent potholes on city 
roads. In 2018, the BMC floated a circular to concretize 
the carriageway and side strips of roads to a width of 13.4 
meters and 18.30 meters (Roads & Traffic, BMC 2018), 
but in 2022 decided to concretize all streets irrespective 
of width (Gokhale and Acharya 2022). Previously, 
intersections and side strips were constructed with 
flexible materials like asphalt. However, with the updated 
approach, all elements were made of concrete. This posed 
a serious problem, as, once implemented, it was difficult 
to make any changes to concretized roads due to a longer 
defect liability period (as discussed under “Context”). 
Therefore, implementing safe-street designs became a big 
challenge as the city needed to justify excavating a smooth-
surfaced concrete road during the DLP .
An example highlighting this concern was the site in 
Vikhroli under the Mumbai Street Lab, where authorities 
expressed reluctance to adopt the proposal, which would 
have required excavating a concrete road during the DLP . 
Another example was AGLR, where the whole 200-meter 
stretch under consideration had already been concretized, 
posing a challenge to making any safe-street design 
alterations on the ground. Changes from the edge up to 
the runner beam for footpath extensions were allowed, 
but beyond that, the higher thickness of the concrete led 
the ward to decide against breaking or damaging the road. 
This led to uneven footpath widths along the stretch. In 
2019, a new circular (Roads & Traffic, BMC 2019) was 
introduced to improve the quality and lifespan of footpaths 
by replacing paver blocks with improved concrete 
specifications. These material changes, however, still did 
not address the underlying issue of frequent excavations 
for utility maintenance.
FIGURE 80  |    Paver blocks on footpath and side strip 
at LBS Road, 2015

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 185
LACK OF CONSIDERATION 
OF CLIMATE CHANGE 
IMPACTS ON STREETS AND 
ROAD SAFETY
Currently, there is no discussion of the impact of climate 
change on the sustainability of pavements or footpath 
infrastructure or the urgency of adapting to intense 
weather events. This is despite Mumbai becoming in 2021 
the first South Asian city to develop a climate action 
plan. Climate change adds another layer of complexity 
to the ongoing challenges faced by the BMC in finding 
sustainable and practical solutions to road safety and the 
maintenance and longevity of footpaths in the city.
WRI India’s study on AGLR showed a 3°C rise in the 
highest and lowest recorded temperatures across the 
corridor between the periods 2005–2010 and 2015–2020, 
a direct result of increasing concretization and decreasing 
tree cover (see Figure 81). A critical issue is how climate 
change and adverse climate events impact street use 
across different user groups. A likely gradual change is 
in transport mode shares, with fewer people choosing to 
walk (to their destinations or public transport stations) 
or cycle, given increasingly unsafe and uncomfortable 
conditions. Moreover, the extensive concretization of 
roads and sidewalks adversely affects the ecology of streets, 
impacting tree mortality. Despite the National Green 
Tribunal order advising a one-meter radius around trees be 
kept free of concrete during construction (S. Kumar et al. 
2013), the bases of many trees are encased in thick layers of 
impenetrable concrete. A 2013 investigation by Vanashakti 
(Virani 2022) revealed that 1,359 of 2,000 trees in 
Mumbai were infested with mealybugs and fungi due to 
concretization, compromising their immunity. In 2020–21, 
as part of a tree conservation process (BMC 2022a), the 
Garden Department acted to deconcretize 3,112 trees. 
However, 584 trees had to be removed as they were already 
dead or were endangering other plants.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 187

188 I  wri-india.org

CHAPTER 7 
 
Lessons learned
This chapter examines whether citywide initiatives 
for safer streets have contributed to broader urban 
transformation. This assessment considers multiple 
dimensions of urban transformation, including 
changes to the physical environment, shifts in 
financial flows and investment priorities, evolution 
in institutional structures and decision-making 
routines, tangible shifts in people’s behaviors and 
daily mobility patterns, and transformations in 
public perceptions and mental models regarding 
urban mobility. 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 189

The purpose of this report is to enhance organization and 
ecosystem-wide learning from a formative evaluation of 
the pedestrian-focused road-safety initiatives undertaken 
in the last decade by the city of Mumbai, in partnership 
with WRI India and other partners supported by the 
Bloomberg Initiative, to create safer, inclusive, accessible, 
and more resilient streets at scale across the city. This 
chapter discusses the lessons learned based on two 
research questions:
 ▪ What lessons can be learned from citywide action 
on improving street design for safer streets for urban 
transformation across large cities in India?
 ▪ What are the gaps and challenges that remain?
We will discuss the first question in two parts. The first 
part will share some broad lessons that surfaced from 
evaluating the multi-dimensional efforts over a decade 
to improve road safety with a focus on pedestrian access 
to streets in Mumbai. The second part will comment 
on whether citywide action on safer streets led to urban 
transformation.
BROAD LESSONS FROM A 
DECADE OF PROCESSES 
AND SOLUTIONS ON SAFER 
STREETS IN MUMBAI
Citywide action promoting safer streets by design has 
come a long way from the vision first articulated in the 
NUTP 2006. In 2012, when WRI India started working 
in Mumbai, road safety was a nascent issue. WRI India, 
in adopting three different approaches aligned with the 
Safe System approach to road safety work—systemic 
engagement, programmatic interventions, and design 
interventions—managed to move the needle toward 
making safer streets by design an emerging issue by 2018 
and a more mainstream issue by 2022. The evaluation 
highlights that change, as discussed in the section 
“Effectiveness,” (and referring to changes in policies, 
municipal budgets, institutional practices, stakeholder 
perceptions, physical changes, behavior, and perception 
changes) must be understood as both the process and 
outcome of intervening in complex systems while acting on 
interdependent leverage points (discussed in detail later).
The first lesson is the necessity for long-term investment 
in this work to play the long game. Results only became 
visible after five to six years of engagement with the city on 
municipal policy, shepherding the implementation of data-
driven design interventions on the ground, mainly using 
municipal budgets. This engagement continues to support 
the city in scaling, first through a linear model, which had 
limited impact, and then adopting a partnership-based 
model through the empanelment of urban designers to 
significantly scale up the coverage of streets across the city 
(from 1 percent to 15 percent).
However, we find that despite having municipal budgets 
to support this work and in spite of the mainstreaming 
of programs like SATS and MSTP , safer streets for 
pedestrians in Mumbai remain a promise and have not 
achieved population scale, nor are they self-sustaining, 
despite significant tangible on-the-ground influence 
on citizens’ lives. Many safe-street designs were only 
partially implemented due to many issues, among 
which jurisdictional issues of wards and agencies feature 
prominently. Moreover, two hard-won mainstreamed 
programs—SATS, which sought to scale safe, child-
190 I  wri-india.org

friendly access streets to 200 municipal schools from a 
single pilot, and MSTP , which envisioned scaling from 20 
km of safe-street interventions achieved by 2022 to 300 
km of safer streets by urban design—are currently on hold 
due to government change at the state and local level.
There is often a mismatch in conceptualizing pathways of 
change within global policy frameworks for sustainable 
and livable cities (such as citizen- and data-driven 
approaches with strong feedback loops communicating 
the real impact that enable regimes to embrace, scale up, 
and mainstream innovative ideas) and urban governance 
experiences on the ground. Multiple agencies are working 
on the same street with different principles, budgets, and 
mandates, multiple actors are advocating for different 
solutions to the same problem, and there are multiple 
pathways available to influence safer-street design work 
at scale, as discussed in the “Context” section, as well as 
multiple ways of managing information flows and shaping 
perceptions and mental models.
The second lesson from this experience is that technical 
engagements alone are insufficient without the 
development of appropriate methods to understand the 
complexities, interdependencies, and trade-offs involved 
in urban change. Without these approaches, many 
important pathways of change may remain overlooked.
The third lesson speaks to the need for a deep 
understanding of governance and institutional 
arrangements, the political economy of the city, and clear 
strategies for change management within complex urban 
governance systems. Such approaches can help identify 
and question the dominant assumptions about how change 
occurs (Angheloiu and Tennant 2020), and better help in 
dealing with the uncertainty about the future of scaled-up 
initiatives and project sustainability.
HAS CITYWIDE ACTION 
ON SAFER STREETS 
LED TO URBAN 
TRANSFORMATION?
To draw lessons from the citywide action in Mumbai for 
safer streets for pedestrians as part of a systems transition 
in urban mobility, it is important to ask whether such 
actions have led to an urban transformation of a megacity 
like Mumbai. Here we define urban transformation 
as the process and the outcome of changing the 
systemic configuration of urban areas with a view to 
sustainability, performance, and achievements (Wolfram 
and Frantzeskaki 2016). To understand how WRI India 
and other stakeholder interventions used leverage points 
to frame and inform interventions for citywide street 
improvements and urban transformations through the 
lens of road safety and prioritizing pedestrian access to 
streets, we will use Donella Meadows’ (1999) framework of 
leverage points in Table 11.
From the analysis presented in Table 11, it is clear that the 
safer-streets-by-design approach had been predominantly 
concerned with shallow leverage points, with most 
interventions in parameters to leverage the city municipal 
corporation’s annual street improvement program 
using a linear scaling model. It leveraged the footpath 
concretization policy to implement geometric corrections 
of streets and intersections but has not adequately 
leveraged the opportunities presented by the large citywide 
infrastructure projects to effectively build a strong pitch for 
safer streets for pedestrians as part of those projects.
The demonstrated improvement of key spatial metrics 
reinforced the methodology’s success in improving 
pedestrian access and shaped demands from diverse 
stakeholders in the city. However, the failure to sustain 
the programs that sought to mainstream citywide safer 
streets can be attributed to a disproportionate focus on 
parameters to leverage low-hanging fruit—improving a 
few street designs of selected streets every year through 
the BMC’s annual street improvement pathway to achieve 
outcomes of improved road safety and greater pedestrian 
access to public spaces. These interventions through 
short-term projects are obviously quick wins and reinforce 
the importance of scaling the work. However, long-term 
engagement to improve streets at scale and sustain the 
initiatives requires the building of transformational 
capacities within the system structure and mental models 
(deep leverage points) to consider the impact on people’s 
lives and realize a sustainable transport system beyond 
external funding timelines and political cycles. We revisit 
these ideas and present a deeper analysis of gaps and 
challenges later in the chapter.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 191

192 I  wri-india.org
TABLE 11  |   Analysis of safer streets work using Donella Meadows’ leverage points framework
NATURE OF LEVERAGE PLACES TO INTERVENE IN A SYSTEM LEVERAGE POINTS FOR SAFER STREETS WORK IN MUMBAI
Shallow leverage points Parameters 
(Frameworks, targets, physical form—what is 
visible and tangible)
Principles
WRI India used the principles of design, test, learn, build and scale for pilot 
projects based on a linear view of scaling until 2021. After that, the view of 
scaling adopted a partnership-based approach and introduced the idea 
of building a community of practice through urban design street labs and 
scaling up through the empanelment of urban designers to undertake 
citywide street design work. 
Policy
WRI India has provided technical inputs to and informed IRC codes, BMC 
guidelines and circulars, and RFPs for empaneling urban designers. 
Investments
The focus is on leveraging annual street improvement budgets, footpath 
concretization budgets, CSR funds, and MP/MLA Local Area Development 
funds to make streets safer by design.
Feedback loops  
(Information flows, positive and negative 
feedback loops)
Principles
The key principles are data-led design of pilots, data collection using 
thorough perception surveys to gather user feedback, and observational 
studies. Press coverage of pilot projects described the design interventions 
as supportive of pedestrian access. Policy: PILs in Mumbai High Court led to 
the HPEC on Mumbai’s traffic whose recommendations drove the framing of 
the Pedestrian First Footpath. 
Policy
Equal Streets as a citizen-driven initiative supported by the police 
reinforced the importance of streets as public spaces. At the same time, WRI 
India became a knowledge partner to BMC in the BIGRS partnership. These 
reinforcing feedback loops brought the value of data-driven safer streets 
by design to the surface and led to WRI India being invited to redesign 
LBS Road using the Safe- System approach. This was followed by redesign 
requests for many more junctions and streets. 
Investments
Improvements in metrics such as improved throughput of vehicles, 
reduced crossing distance, and utilization of median refuges, and therefore 
increased safety for pedestrians, have been demonstrated, leading to 
more demand from the BMC and MTP for WRI India to work on street 
improvements from an urban design approach.
As sustainability, performance, and achievements (defining 
features of urban transformation) are closely linked to the 
maturity of an ecosystem, we use the framework developed 
by Sattva Consulting in their system change training 
for WRI India to understand the level of maturity of an 
ecosystem for urban transformation in Mumbai through 
policy innovation, scaling, and sustaining change (see Table 
12). The case of Mumbai also highlights that leverage 
points are interdependent, with shallower leverage points 
(the design interventions at the parameter level) and 
deeper leverage points (systemic engagements that bring 
about policy changes, allocated budgets, and launched 
programs for mainstreaming safer-street work) influencing 
each other. From the analysis presented in Table 12, it is 
clear that even though there is consensus in the ecosystem 
(mainstream) on “what to do” and how to scale, lack of 
non-negotiable principles for safer streets by design for 
municipal actors, gaps in change management, and lack of 
inclusion of the last mile and the most vulnerable prevent 
the hard-won gains from becoming transformative.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 193
NATURE OF LEVERAGE PLACES TO INTERVENE IN A SYSTEM LEVERAGE POINTS FOR SAFER STREETS WORK IN MUMBAI
Deep leverage points System structures  
(Self-regulating structures and rules such as 
governance and decision-making structures)
Principles
Principles: Mumbai Street Lab (MSL) showed the potential of urban design 
through a design competition for five streets working at a principle level. 
Fifty junctions were identified as high-risk in the CMP for phased upgrades 
over 18 years. Annually, a certain number of these are assigned by the BMC 
to BIGRS for upgrading through the annual budgeting process. Many of 
these were redesigned by WRI India. However, this linear scaling by the 
system fails to reach the population scale while large programs like MSTP 
can scale more effectively by involving a larger community of practice.
Policy
SATS was launched in 2021 to make access to schools safe, covering 
200 municipal schools. MSTP , launched in 2022, saw the empanelment 
of urban designers to redesign 300 km of streets as part of a large urban 
transformation program in the city. Both programs are currently on hold.
Investments
In 2022, two programs for scaling up safer streets by design were framed 
with support from municipal budgets, but they have yet to take off.
Mental models  
(Leadership Mindset Shift/Commitments that 
have the power to transcend paradigms)
Principles
After 2018, there was increased demand from different stakeholders across 
the city to improve intersections as a matter of civic pride, develop new 
public spaces at intersections, that are local landmarks, and reduce conflict 
between vehicles and pedestrians at intersections.
Policy
Safer-street work continues leveraging other well-funded agendas, such as 
city beautification, decongestion, and road concretization, as pedestrian 
safety is not a policy priority by itself.
Investments
Analysis of municipal budgets between 2010 and 2024 revealed that 
approximately INR 36,000 crores, or $4.3 billion, was allocated to the Roads 
& Traffic Department, of which, only INR 820 crores (i.e., $97.5 million), or 
a mere 2.3 percent of the entire Roads & Traffic Department budget in 
2010–24 was allocated for footpath improvement or resurfacing and walking 
infrastructure. WRI India’s approach to design interventions leveraged 
additional municipal finance of more than INR 220 crores ($26.2 million) for 
permanent design implementation at local intervention sites.

GAPS AND CHALLENGES 
BASED ON DIMENSIONS OF 
URBAN TRANSFORMATION
To understand the gaps, we revisit the effectiveness of the 
three approaches and analyze them from the lens of the 
five-point framework on urban transformation (Maassen 
and Galvin 2019) as introduced in the “Effectiveness” 
section. In Table 13, we summarize how the outcomes of 
each approach relate to the different dimensions of urban 
transformation.
The dimensions related to the changes in the physical 
environment, perceptions of safety, and impact on 
the daily lives of users, as measured by the reduction 
in crashes, fatalities, and serious injuries involving 
pedestrians, recorded the most tangible benefits through 
design interventions.
TABLE 13  |   Success of approaches in terms of dimensions of urban transformation
SYSTEMIC ENGAGEMENTS PROGRAMMATIC INTERVENTIONS DESIGN INTERVENTIONS
Physical environment Yes Yes Yes
Institutional structures and 
routines
Yes Yes No
Financial money flows Yes Yes Yes
Behavior changes and daily lives No No Yes
Perceptions and mental models Yes Yes Yes
Mumbai also presents the curious case that in spite of 
sufficient availability of finances to undertake large-scale 
work on road safety, and despite diverse efforts over a 
decade by the BMC, WRI India, and other partners to 
improve streets by design, several gaps remain stemming 
from the complex political economy of the city and 
the city’s institutional arrangements for transport 
infrastructure that compromise the mainstreaming of 
the work and the development of an integrated approach 
to transport planning. In this section, we will discuss 
the gaps and challenges linked to each dimension of 
urban transformation that prevented the ecosystem from 
transforming (as discussed in Table 13) to undertake safer-
streets-by-design work at the population scale and become 
self-sustainable through the city’s established systems.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 195
Source: WRI India.

Changes to the physical 
environment
Even though the biggest change is visible in the physical 
environment, with a significant increase in pedestrianized 
public spaces claimed from streets, several implementation 
challenges often compromise the quality of the redesign 
of safer streets.
A. Different ward jurisdictions of multi-
arm intersections
Different arms of an intersection can fall under different 
ward jurisdictions, requiring buy-in of the whole design by 
all concerned wards. The lack of ownership of the shared 
vision and related physical changes could compromise the 
intent of safe design. This is best exemplified at Nagpada 
where less than 50 percent of the overall design was 
implemented due to jurisdictional issues; implementation 
was eventually limited to one ward, thus excluding two 
arms of the junction from implementation even though the 
trial was conducted for the entire junction.
B. Multiple infrastructural jurisdictions 
across agencies compromise design 
solutions
Different agencies are responsible for different 
infrastructures on the road. This often necessitates revising 
designs led by the BMC and its partners to accommodate 
all agencies’ concerns. In the case of AGLR, the proposed 
green corridor under the metro was under MMRDA’s 
jurisdiction. Given the jurisdictional and procedural 
challenges of shutting down the street to implement the 
design, it was decided by the stakeholders to do away 
with the central green public space and instead reclaim 
an equivalent amount of residual space that was available 
along the edges that the BMC controlled.
196 I  wri-india.org

C. Gaps in communication between 
multiple executing contractors
When multiple contractors are involved in executing 
different parts of a design, errors in coordination occur 
primarily due to gaps in communication between different 
parties. A case in point is the HP intersection where 
certain design features, such as curb ramps at footpaths, 
were not implemented due to gaps in communication 
resulting from the involvement of multiple contractors; 
a private CSR contractor executed the islands at the HP 
junction while the various arms were implemented by 
different BMC contractors.
D. Disruptions due to a gap between trial 
and design implementation
If there is a considerable gap between the trial phase 
and full implementation, compromises occur with some 
design elements moving to implementation in a piecemeal 
manner without an integrated approach to implementing 
the whole design. In many cases, despite successful trials, 
there have been delays in the implementation of the design 
for different reasons. These include, but are not limited 
to, delays in lining up finance from different sources, land 
acquisition challenges, delays due to monsoon, transfer of 
ward officers, and other ongoing infrastructure works.
E. Challenges to the sustainability 
of safe-street physical design 
improvements
The sustainability of the physical changes over time is 
discussed in the preceding chapter. However, some of 
the challenges to the sustainability of safe-street physical 
design improvements are as follows:
LACK OF AN INTEGRATED APPROACH TO 
INFRASTRUCTURE PLANNING
The biggest challenge to sustainability stems from the lack 
of an integrated approach to planning the city’s extensive 
big-ticket transport infrastructure, such as the metro rail, 
in conjunction with promoting road safety on all access 
streets. An integrated approach is also lacking in the 
regular maintenance works by different departments of 
the BMC and other agencies responsible for providing 
different services on streets.
UNCOORDINATED REPAIR AND MAINTENANCE 
WORK BY DIFFERENT AGENCIES
Such work makes even the improved streets pedestrian 
un-friendly. Our observation studies showed that the 
surface condition of most sidewalks was unsatisfactory 
with some unevenness or spot damage through 
uncoordinated repair and maintenance works (see Figure 
82). This translates into pedestrian dissatisfaction, as 
captured in the perception survey, which shows that 22 
percent of the survey respondents want improvements to 
footpaths in the streets with safe-design interventions. 
When probed further on the kind of improvements people 
would like to see, 36 percent of the respondents asked 
for wider footpaths while 18 percent of the respondents 
wanted better footpath maintenance.
FIGURE 82  |    Uneven, broken footpath surfaces from 
uncoordinated repair work
Photo Credit:  Akanksha Aggarwal.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 197

UNIVERSAL CONCRETIZATION OF FOOTPATHS 
WITHOUT ACCOUNTING FOR SUSTAINABILITY 
CHALLENGES
Implementing safe-street designs on concretized roads 
is a big challenge if that involves breaking and damaging 
the concrete surface, as the city needs to justify such 
decisions, especially during the DLP . Besides safer street 
work, the concretization policy also fails to effectively 
address the issue of excavation for utility maintenance, 
leading to damaged and potentially unsafe footpaths. 
Additional challenges include imposing a blanket policy 
without understanding the environmental costs of 
concretization in the face of climate change, which causes 
the city to experience more heat stress and unprecedented 
flooding events.
LACK OF INCENTIVES FOR COMMUNITIES TO 
MAINTAIN SIDEWALKS
There is evidence that private actors, particularly 
businesses, maintain the sidewalks in front of their 
premises. However, these examples are few and are not 
incentivized through any policy. See a further discussion 
on this under “Sustainability.”
Institutional structures, routines, 
and gaps
Systemic engagements with the city by diverse 
stakeholders on the issue of pedestrian safety prompted 
the BMC to create and adopt the 2014 Pedestrian 
First Footpath Policy. This policy was a major turning 
point in making streets safer for pedestrians by design. 
Because of this, various circulars related to footpaths, 
medians, and islands were revised to include design 
considerations for improving user safety. However, these 
continued to prioritize a civil engineering perspective 
focusing on materials and utilities, with limited scope and 
consideration for urban design. Other documents that 
guide the planning, design, and implementation of safer 
streets, such as IRC guidelines for pedestrian facilities 
(IRC 2012), planning and design guidelines for street 
furniture for Mumbai and its suburbs, planning and design 
guidelines for signage systems for Mumbai and its suburbs, 
and street design guidelines for Greater Mumbai, are not 
legally binding. There is a lack of mandatory guidelines 
that would establish non-negotiable principles of safer 
street design for different types of streets to support efforts 
at the local level.
To sustain the decade of processes that experimented 
with urban-design-led approaches and introduced five 
more steps in the business-as-usual model of the BMC’s 
typical annual road improvements (see “Relevance of the 
Approaches”), the civic body is dependent on external 
partners to shepherd the implementation of niche 
ideas through the system and is unable to sustain the 
mainstreaming of projects stemming from these ideas 
through political transitions. In 2022, the city witnessed a 
significant milestone as the BMC issued the first tender of 
its kind for the empanelment of urban design consultants 
for street improvement projects along 300 km of streets 
across the city. This moment was possible after a decade 
of systemic engagements, programs for urban-design-led 
street improvements by WRI India and other partners, and 
the embrace of new experimental processes of data-driven 
design that are first tested through tactical interventions 
before scaling. However, MSTP did not survive the 
political transitions at the state and BMC levels soon 
after its launch. This points to entrenched institutional 
challenges to this work and gaps in change management 
when such transitions happen, some of which are listed 
below. As a senior urban planner who has worked on 
several projects with the city remarked:
While we as professionals may be convinced of the benefits 
of safer-street design, the gap in the understanding, 
imagination, and assumptions of common people and 
political actors is still significant... also I often find myself 
focused on the institutional setup and governance. My 
perspective has evolved to recognize that these areas, 
along with the distribution of powers, are crucial and 
require reform. The designs we create, whether they are 
architectural or otherwise, are all constrained by these 
factors.—Interview with Senior Urban Planner, 
Mumbai Parking Authority (MPA) June 24, 2022.
198 I  wri-india.org

A. Lack of institutionalization of the idea 
of safe streets at a principle level
Non-negotiable principles of safer streets have yet to be 
developed to inform all city-making agendas and transport 
infrastructure projects that target streets so that large new 
infrastructure projects like the metro, or other programs 
such as city beautification, do not undermine gains made 
through safe-street designs.
B. Lack of regulation of CSR and private 
activities in street improvements
Many activities under CSR and those of private players 
are focused on street beautification to make a statement. 
There is no requirement for an assessment of work done 
through CSR to understand the impact on pedestrian life 
and the use of public open spaces claimed through safe-
streets initiatives.
C. Reluctance to adopt new concepts 
and processes
Officials from the BMC and other road-owning agencies 
are reluctant to adopt new processes and concepts without 
established principles and mandatory regulations for 
safe-street design. They are eager to maintain the status 
quo with established power dynamics and systems of 
accountability. There is also a fear of failure in trying 
something new and of possible negative press and public 
backlash. Also, any new process requires officials and 
engineers to invest time and effort in developing new 
skills and capacities that may not be easy to accommodate 
within their current workloads. Any project that deviates 
from the established processes requires constant advocacy, 
follow-ups, and capacity building.
D. Dependence on champions for scaling 
and issues of change management
Because of the reluctance to adopt new processes, the 
gains made by programmatic interventions for scaling 
the safe-streets work were typically championed by key 
individuals within the government who played a crucial 
role in driving street transformation initiatives. These 
individuals often held key leadership positions within 
agencies like the BMC, MTP , MMRDA, and the Urban 
Development Department, among others. They can 
support and advance innovative design solutions and 
navigate bureaucratic hurdles. Their commitment to 
enhancing street infrastructure and their understanding of 
the local context and stakeholder dynamics enabled them 
to champion projects from conception to implementation. 
By leveraging their position, expertise, connections, and 
understanding of budgets, these government champions 
mobilized resources and garnered support from other 
agencies and stakeholders. They helped technical and 
knowledge partners like WRI India support implementing 
agencies to navigate regulatory challenges and ultimately 
facilitate the successful execution of street transformation 
initiatives while navigating regulatory challenges. 
Elected representatives and other political change agents 
also played a pivotal role in driving positive change in 
design-led street transformations and allocating budgets 
for scaling up niche ideas through programs such as 
SATS and MSTP . 
With changes in local government and state government 
administration, the city’s priorities become realigned 
with a new development, which may often result in 
cities discontinuing ongoing projects and programs. In 
Mumbai, while addressing challenges of mobility remained 
important, newer priorities placed greater emphasis on 
city beautification and road concretization. With recent 
political and administrative changes, the existing safer-
streets projects lost momentum. Frequent transfers of 
mid- and junior-level engineers also undermine capacity-
building efforts by non-state actors such as WRI India.
E. Challenges of working with external 
urban planning and design consultants
Due to the extensive nature of the projects undertaken by 
the BMC, it heavily relies on external agencies and vendors 
to facilitate the implementation of safer-street projects. 
However, onboarding and collaborating with new design 
consultants for street design presents challenges for both 
the BMC and the consultants. This is mainly due to the 
lack of a common understanding and alignment regard-
ing the scope of work, fees, timelines, and other factors. 
Each party involved is concerned about safeguarding their 
interests, which can hinder the smooth progression of the 
project. Moreover, building trust between the parties is a 
gradual process, so consultants working with the system for 
a longer period may not face these initial challenges. Even 
if they have limited expertise in designing safer streets, 
they are often preferred as trusted partners by the BMC.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 199

WRI India leveraged routine municipal budgetary 
allocations through design interventions of more than 
INR 220 crores ($26.2 billion) for permanent design 
implementation at the selected local intervention sites 
(see Figure 63 in “Effectiveness”). Of these, 73 percent, or 
INR 160 crores ($19.3 million), were allocated for LBS 
Marg. In addition, in 2021, the successful implementation 
of the SATS pilot enabled WRI India to inform the 
city’s approach to street improvements by prioritizing 
the needs of vulnerable users, particularly school-going 
children. This resulted in INR 50 crores ($6 million) 
being allocated for the SATS program in the 2022–23 
budget. Similarly, in 2023–24, the BMC’s empanelment 
of urban design consultants led to an allocation of INR 
200 crores ($23.8 million) for pedestrian facilities across 
the city. In early 2023, the BMC initially floated tenders 
for INR 5800 crores ($689.9 million) to concretize 400 
kilometers of roads. However, due to a lack of adequate 
response, the tenders were canceled. Fresh tenders were 
invited this year (2024) for a larger amount of INR 6080 
crores ($723.2 million) for 397 kilometers of roads across 
the city (PNT Bureau 2024). A hidden challenge with 
such municipal financial flows is the lack of accounting 
for the environmental costs of purely material replacement 
approaches to street improvements through large-scale 
concretization of roads. A study by Moody’s RMS, an 
international risk management company, highlights that 
the urban heat-island effect increases risk mitigation 
costs and requires targeted investments in modifying the 
built environment and infrastructure. This means that 
Mumbai will spend more than double what New York 
spends to manage urban heat-island effects due to massive 
concretization (Tembhekar 2024). Mumbai shows a more 
than 100 percent increase in risk mitigation costs year on 
year due to heightened urban heat stress, and considering 
the higher emissions scenario in the year 2050, Mumbai 
is looking at nearly double the risk mitigation costs as 
compared to the base year 2024 (Tembhekar 2024).
F. Gaps in technical capacity within the 
BMC to mainstream urban-design-led 
approaches to safer streets
Street improvement projects in Mumbai are typically 
considered to be in the domain of civil engineering, 
resulting in a stronger emphasis on structural integrity and 
construction materials than on street design. The BMC 
Roads Department has separate departments for planning 
and implementing projects on street improvements. 
However, they are also responsible for multiple other 
municipal tasks required to run the city and deal with day-
to-day issues in their routine work. There is currently very 
limited capacity to embed urban-design-led approaches in 
street improvements, which often results in surface-level 
enhancements rather than addressing underlying issues 
of pedestrian safety, accessibility, and climate resilience. 
At present, the absence of a dedicated resource, such as a 
design cell with the necessary technical expertise, makes it 
challenging to consistently adopt a holistic urban design 
approach at scale.
Financial flows
A. Leveraging routine finance/municipal 
budgetary allocations
There is a considerable difference in the budget 
allocation and investments made by the BMC in big 
infrastructure projects compared to specific footpath 
improvement projects (see “Foundational factors” and 
“Behavior changes and impact on traffic management”). 
However, with the adoption of a major policy to 
concretize the city’s streets in 2019, substantial municipal 
budgets were allocated for footpath improvement by 
replacing paver blocks with concrete to lengthen the 
design life of streets. This offered another entry point 
for WRI India to suggest geometric improvements in 
street designs. While no direct municipal budget was 
explicitly allocated for enhancing street safety through 
design improvements, organizations like WRI India 
could navigate municipal corporation budgets and 
policies, leveraging annual maintenance or material 
replacement funds to implement innovative street and 
footpath improvements.
The safer-streets-by-design initiative, a niche idea 
promoted by BIGRS partners, became more widespread 
due to the linear scaling of its implementation by WRI 
India. WRI India effectively leveraged the BMC’s 
routine annual street improvement budgets through 
three approaches. 
200 I  wri-india.org

COVID-19 lockdown, the idea of the statue reappeared, 
this time in the form of a COVID warrior statue and was 
eventually financed and implemented by another corporate 
group. Similarly, the R-South ward had also identified 
CSR funds to implement WRI India’s proposed redesign 
for the Namaha junction. However, due to the transfer 
of the engineer and sub-engineer in charge, the funds 
did not materialize, and implementation was undertaken 
leveraging the routine budgetary allocations to the BMC’s 
Roads & Traffic Department.
D.  Gaps in municipal financial flows
LACK OF DIRECT FUNDING FOR STREET 
IMPROVEMENTS
Beyond the disproportionate budgetary allocations for 
large infrastructure projects as compared to safe-street 
and walkability projects, there also seems to be no direct 
municipal budget explicitly allocated for enhancing street 
safety through design improvements. Organizations like 
WRI India have had to leverage maintenance or material 
replacement funds to implement safety improvements, 
indicating a gap in targeted funding for safety.
NO ACCOUNTING FOR ENVIRONMENTAL COSTS OF 
CONCRETIZATION
There is a lack of accounting for flood- and heat-risk 
mitigation costs related to the massive concretization 
of streets across the city. Even though WRI India has 
leveraged concretization budgets to drive safer-street 
design work, these budgets do not take into account that 
open spaces contribute to flooding due to the near-zero 
infiltration capacity of concrete surfaces, with the runoff 
coefficient being as high as 1 (WRI India 2022). This 
indicates that a larger portion of the precipitation turns 
into runoff, causing waterlogging (Behal 2023). A study 
conducted by the United States Trade and Development 
Agency (USTDA) and KPMG has revealed that Mumbai 
incurred a loss of INR 14,000 crore ($1.7 billion) due to 
floods between 2005 and 2015 (Money Control 2019), 
and another survey of 4,000 Mumbai residents in 2023 
revealed that 63 percent of residents experience a decline 
in working hours and productivity due to waterlogging 
during monsoons (Pinto 2023). One of Mumbai’s pressing 
issues is persistent waterlogging during high flood 
situations. Approximately 30 percent of the city’s streets 
are identified as waterlogging hotspots. This problem 
disrupts daily activities and poses significant risks to user 
safety and infrastructure (see Figure 83).
B. Leveraging funds available to elected 
representatives
In at least two of the five intersections we studied in detail, 
elected representatives were involved in developing public 
spaces with different outcomes linked to their specific 
demands and agendas. During the trial of the Gokhale 
junction, the local MLA was keen to fund the public space 
claimed under a flyover only if the design protected it as a 
garden with controlled access—a deviation from the open-
access public space designed by WRI India. In the case 
of the Nagpada intersection, the implementation of the 
redesigned spaces was spearheaded by a municipal corpo-
rator who was keen to change the image of the area and 
found the resources to hire a famous architect to beautify 
the sizeable public space reclaimed by WRI India’s street 
redesign. The project was funded at a higher cost than 
other similar intersections in the city.
Similarly, the involvement of the Guardian Minister of 
Mumbai in 2021, during the study and design submis-
sion for AGLR, enabled access to District Planning and 
Development Committee (DPDC) funds. The District 
Collector allocated INR 200 crores ($23.8 million) for 
implementation after removing a proposed green corridor 
from the design. Despite the political change in the state, 
as the pilot stretch was completed, the authorities could 
demonstrate the project’s potential to new officehold-
ers and execute the remaining stretch from Marol to the 
Western Express Highway before the funds secured under 
the DPDC lapsed.
C. Leveraging Corporate Social 
Responsibility (CSR) funds
After the demonstrated success of the tactical trial at the 
HP junction in April–May 2017, the ward officer of the 
H-West ward was able to procure funds of approximately 
INR 50–60 lakh ($59,472–71,366) through CSR for partly 
funding the permanent implementation of the redesign. 
The traffic islands were constructed using these CSR funds, 
while other components of the design were completed with 
the BMC’s Roads & Traffic Department budget. While 
coordinating with these multiple agencies was challenging, 
coordinating with the CSR-funded contractor who wanted 
to install a statue at the reclaimed plaza on the left turn 
from Linking Road to Swami Vivekananda Road was 
particularly difficult. For example, installation would 
hamper the design and free movement of pedestrians. At 
first, the team managed to convince stakeholders against 
installing the statue in 2019. However, in 2021, during the 
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 201
