202 I  wri-india.org

CHALLENGES OF LEVERAGING CSR FUNDS
While CSR funds have been used for some projects, the 
task of coordinating with multiple agencies and aligning 
different stakeholders’ visions has been challenging 
and often leads to delays and inconsistencies in project 
implementation. In addition, political crises and the 
transfer of key personnel can result in the loss of 
committed CSR funds, delaying or complicating project 
implementation.
POLITICAL AGENDAS AND COMPETING INTERESTS
Political changes can result in the reallocation or 
rollback of previously allocated funds, disrupting 
ongoing projects and causing incomplete or suboptimal 
outcomes.Addressing these gaps would require a 
more integrated and strategic approach to budgeting, 
planning, and implementing street improvement 
projects, with a stronger focus on environmental 
sustainability, stakeholder coordination, and political 
change management.
Behavior changes and impact on 
daily life
A significant finding of this evaluation is that physical 
changes implemented at intersections and along corridors 
contributed to saving lives and mitigated the risks for 
pedestrians related to unsafe roads. However, as most 
intersection designs using safe-street principles are carried 
out in isolation, the majority of the streets beyond the 
intersection continue to be unsafe and remain difficult for 
pedestrians to access, resulting in pedestrians walking on 
streets and not on the sidewalks (see discussion below).
The scaling of the safe-streets work followed a linear 
model, building on pilots across different geographies of 
the city, impacting about 6 percent of the intersections and 
1 percent of streets. Our survey of pedestrians in three 
improved intersections and two corridors revealed that 
about 75 percent of pedestrians using these streets felt 
“safe” to “extremely safe”—the main intent behind the 
interventions. However, as the anticipated scaling of safe 
streets through MSTP that would have impacted about 
15 percent of Mumbai’s streets was stalled due to political 
change in the state and local governments, the city’s streets 
remain largely unsafe, directly impacting daily life.
We are only able to comment on pedestrian and motorist 
behaviors as observed pre- and post-implementation at 
three intersections; this omits other aspects of behavior 
such as changes in commuting and transportation patterns, 
consumption practices, access to economic, social, and 
recreational opportunities, increases in public safety (traffic, 
violence), and service coverage. We mapped the behavior 
of 761 pedestrians at three intersections and two corridors 
and observed 767 motorists for their compliance with 
three specific issues: the stop line, signals, and both signals 
and stop lines.
The evidence from this evaluation suggests that there 
is greater compliance among pedestrians in following 
designed routes in streets where safe-street design features 
have been implemented to a greater extent. One example is 
Namaha junction, where 90 percent of the proposed design 
was implemented on the ground and where the highest 
level of compliance with both designated pedestrian 
paths (73 percent) and designated crossings (53 percent) 
by pedestrians was observed. Significant deviations from 
designed routes by pedestrians are often inevitable for 
several reasons.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 203

A. Reasons for pedestrian non-
compliance with intended design and 
sections of streets continuing to be 
unsafe
NARROW SCOPE OF DESIGN
The scope of design is typically limited to geometric 
corrections of the intersection and up to 50 meters on each 
arm. Since streets emanating from the intersections had 
not been subjected to urban design interventions for safer 
streets along their entire lengths, pedestrians often walk on 
streets with limited sidewalk access and continue to follow 
their line of movement without stepping onto the sidewalk 
at the intersection. The scope also does not include traffic 
signals, which can compromise the crossing experience. 
Further, the fact that landscaping is not included in the 
scope contributes to the lack of shade, making walking 
uncomfortable on hot days.
NOT ALL SAFE-STREET DESIGN MEASURES ARE 
FULLY IMPLEMENTED
Pedestrians are forced to cross at undesignated and 
unmarked locations where only partial implementation 
has happened, as well as where surfaces are damaged 
due to poor repair work or encroachments. Partial 
implementation can be due to many reasons, as discussed 
in relation to physical changes under “Effectiveness.” 
When different arms of multi-arm junctions fall under 
different ward jurisdictions, the lack of shared vision 
and ownership of the physical changes by different local 
politicians can prevent the work from being implemented 
in their ward. Full implementation of a design can also be 
undermined when there is a considerable gap between the 
trial and implementation phases.
PEDESTRIAN ACCESS IS OFTEN COMPROMISED 
BY DESIGN FEATURES INSTALLED BY OTHER CITY-
MAKING AGENDAS
Reclaimed pedestrian spaces are often made inaccessible by 
adding railings, walls, statues, and planters as part of city 
beautification drives.
204 I  wri-india.org

B. Reasons for motorists’ non-
compliance with intended design 
improvements
The challenge of non-compliance with traffic regulations 
at the improved sites is starkly evident in the data gleaned 
from the survey of 767 motorists across three intersections 
and two corridors. The findings reveal concerning trends: 
20 percent of motorists complied with both the measures 
(stop line and signals), 57 percent complied with one of 
the measures (15 percent followed only stop lines, 42 
percent observed only signals), and 23 percent failed to 
comply with either measure.
Such disregard for traffic rules results in motorists 
encroaching upon pedestrian crossing areas, forcing them 
to take risks by crossing at undesignated and unmarked 
locations. A 2020 survey of 1,561 car drivers with valid 
licenses in India found that nearly 95 percent lacked 
knowledge and awareness of traffic rules (Dash 2021). 
Moreover, apart from the official system of rules and 
regulations, the road safety culture of a community has 
a strong bearing on road user behavior and the resulting 
road safety outcomes (PIARC 2019). A 2021 study 
published in the Indian Public Policy Review (Tagat et al. 
2021) uncovered the alarming normalization of traffic rule 
violations in India, where breaking the law has become 
both the prevailing attitude among motorists and a social 
norm. Nevertheless, factors such as short pedestrian 
signal timings, lack of clear signage and markings, and 
malfunctioning signals persistently contribute to increased 
risk of conflicts and non-compliance. To address these 
challenges and achieve better compliance, PIARC’s Road 
Safety Manual underscores the critical importance of 
fostering safe behavior among road users through measures 
such as road safety education, driver training, campaigns 
to encourage safe driving practices, and improved 
enforcement mechanisms.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 205

Change in perceptions and 
mental models
A big limitation of the decade-long work on road safety 
in Mumbai pertains to the lack of data on behaviors 
and perceptions before interventions; this compromises 
an understanding of change in perceptions and mental 
models. The pre-implementation studies focused on 
problems in the physical environment that contribute 
to the lack of access for pedestrians and create conflicts 
between pedestrians and vehicles in sharing the street. 
Urban-design-led street improvements were considered a 
vital tool for improving public health through place-based 
urban transformations. No studies were conducted after 
implementation to show the impact on public health and 
the lives saved.
A proxy indicator for change in perceptions and mental 
models is “awareness and support for new agenda and 
policy priorities, new social and public narratives” (Maassen 
and Galvin 2019, 12). After 2018, increased demand from 
different stakeholders across the city revealed awareness of 
and support for safer streets through design work. These 
demands came not just from the BMC and its wards but 
also from elected representatives who had access to their 
own funds to undertake various development works within 
their constituencies, including but not limited to streets. 
However, as one senior ward-level official points out, much 
work must still be done to change mindsets:
The mindset regarding street improvements has not 
fully changed yet, but it is gradually evolving, and we 
are seeing some progress. People are becoming more 
accepting of the need for street improvements and street 
furniture. Additionally, there is a growing consensus 
that, if necessary, we can remove encroachments to ensure 
safer and smoother streets, ultimately reducing the risk 
of accidents. A good example is Bellasis Road, where 
we held several meetings with stakeholders, including 
street vendors. Through these discussions, they eventually 
agreed to organize their space aligned with the design. 
—Interview with an Assistant Ward Commissioner, 
Brihanmumbai Municipal Corporation, 
February 16, 2023.
As part of this evaluation, we tracked news stories and 
media coverage, in both English and the local state 
language, Marathi, of safe-street work in Mumbai. We 
found that most media coverage focused only on the 
design of intersections and discussed them as something 
new being tried in the city for pedestrian safety. Very few 
media reports discussed the improvements from the 
perspective of saving lives or improving public health 
or as safety measures that are effective in both normal 
and extreme weather conditions. Fewer still published 
follow-up stories on the impact of these interventions.
Instruments such as Mumbai’s CMP , Comprehensive 
Traffic and Transportation Studies (CTS/CTTS), and 
the Development Plan (DPs), though crucial for urban 
planning in Mumbai, focus primarily on macro-level data 
collection geared toward vehicular traffic. In Mumbai, 
they also study public transport demand and ridership for 
buses, metro, and suburban rail. However, the usefulness of 
such instruments is significantly limited for addressing the 
safety needs of non-motorized transport users, including 
pedestrians and cyclists.
These macro-level, vehicular-focused data collection 
practices limit the effectiveness of planning instruments 
in addressing the needs and safety of non-motorized 
transport users. To create a safer and more inclusive urban 
environment, it is essential to incorporate detailed micro-
level data for pedestrians and cyclists into the planning 
processes. Key data points such as pedestrian counts, 
walking paths, and crossing behaviors at the interfaces 
of big infrastructure projects are essential to produce a 
shift that will enable the development of comprehensive 
mobility solutions that enhance the safety and livability 
of Mumbai’s streets for all users. At a minimum, the 
planning of large infrastructures for urban mobility should 
do the following:
 ▪ Count pedestrians and cyclists at key locations
 ▪ Map detailed pedestrian and cycling paths and 
crossing behaviors
 ▪ Analyze conflict points between vehicles and non-
motorized transport users
 ▪ Conduct surveys to gather experiences and perceptions 
of safety from pedestrians and cyclists
A. Gaps and challenges
LACK OF DATA ON ALL USERS AND DIFFERENT 
USES OF STREETS CONTRIBUTES TO A UNITARY 
VISION OF STREETS
A major gap in all the studies and planning instruments 
is the lack of accounting of the street as a public space 
with stationary users and uses other than serving as a flow 
space for traffic and non-motorized users. The shift toward 
micro-level data collection for all users and uses requires 
206 I  wri-india.org

a fundamental change in the mental models of decision-
makers, urban planners, and policymakers. For this shift 
to occur, the city and relevant institutions, including 
the BMC, need to fundamentally change their priorities 
and recognize the importance of pedestrian data and 
data on stationary users of streets in order to prioritize 
them alongside vehicular data. This change is essential 
to scaling safer streets by design and creating a more 
inclusive urban environment.
LACK OF EVIDENCE OF LIVES SAVED THROUGH 
INTERVENTIONS IN THE FEEDBACK LOOP
There is an implicit assumption that safer-streets-by-
design projects generate positive health outcomes and save 
lives because they are based on evidence-based strategies, 
and this informs the methodology for calculating lives 
saved in every road safety design intervention. However, 
the lives-saved calculations carried out by WRI remain 
internal to the organization and may represent a lost 
opportunity to make visible the health impacts of the 
interventions. It takes time to show the impact of this 
work on saving lives on the ground, particularly as one 
needs to wait a few years to obtain crash data after an 
intervention to compare lives saved at any site before and 
after implementation. This is a significant gap and requires 
consideration of other, more proactive, traffic conflict 
analysis methods (see “Recommendations for making 
streets safer in Indian cities”). Such methods would help 
decision-makers receive feedback more quickly, enabling 
more targeted public investments in safer street design as 
part of routine municipal work. The feedback loop needs to 
include different stakeholders, such as
 ▪  mid-level municipal engineers who continue to be 
skeptical of the work;
 ▪ decision-makers within the BMC, who need to 
understand the significance of data-driven designs for 
safer streets to save lives and budget for it in routine 
municipal work; and
 ▪ state transport and other related departments, 
who need to allocate budgets for mainstreaming 
and scaling up safe-streets work following the key 
message of NUTP #MovePeopleNotCars and going 
beyond to #SafeStreetsSaveLives as part of multiple 
international commitments.
LACK OF A COMMUNICATION STRATEGY FOR 
DIFFERENT STAKEHOLDERS FOR SHIFTING 
PERCEPTIONS AND CHANGING MENTAL MODELS
The decade-long processes in Mumbai were primarily 
focused on designing safe-street solutions, scaling the 
implementation through the system, and creating a larger 
community of practice. Much less attention has been 
paid to communicating the impact of this work through 
outreach to policymakers, civil society, and the media. 
Efforts should be made to show the extent to which lives 
have been saved on the ground to build the case for the 
effectiveness of a design-led approach to safer streets 
for saving lives.
EQUITY CHALLENGES
Equitable street improvement requires accommodating 
the needs of all users through solutions that are arrived 
at by consulting all stakeholders. Several gaps were noted 
concerning the poor and marginalized urban groups, who 
are the most prominent pedestrians in the city and, hence, 
most impacted by unsafe streets.
These gaps include the following:
 ▪ The poor, who walk more, are not part of the 
decision-making processes for safer streets: Empirical 
evidence suggests that the current pathways of 
street improvements do not embed equity and are 
not producing inclusive and empowering decision-
making processes for the urban poor, who are 
disproportionately affected by crashes as they rely 
on walking much more than others. This requires 
an urgent rethinking of public participation by 
adopting a framework that accounts for different 
degrees of public participation in street improvement 
projects (ADB 2024).
 ▪ Designs are often negotiated in favor of powerful 
groups: Conflicts arising out of claiming public spaces 
that are generated through safer-street interventions 
are almost always settled in favor of more influential 
groups who are fearful of vendors, homeless people, or 
minority religious groups claiming urban spaces. Such 
groups have sometimes negotiated directly with the 
BMC and the wards to design out the disempowered 
marginalized groups, such as by modifying pedestrian 
refuge plazas to effectively limit their use as public 
spaces, without the involvement of WRI India (see the 
discussion under “private agendas” in “Sustainability”).
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 207

▪ Parking is tolerated on pedestrian footpaths, but 
vending is seen as an encroachment Hawkers and 
vendors who occupy space on footpaths are seen 
as encroachers, and decongestion policies strongly 
recommend their removal. In most cases, there is no 
plan to relocate these informal businesses that thrive 
on the streets. Moreover, even if relocation plans exist, 
the chosen location may not be the most conducive 
for business. However, as included in many proposals 
for improving safety, certain design elements, like curb 
extensions, become difficult to implement due to strong 
opposition from private individuals who are reluctant 
to give up their parking spaces. Although these parking 
spaces are encroachments on public spaces at great 
public cost, they are fiercely defended. This situation 
highlights a deep-rooted inequity where vending is 
perceived as encroachment, but parking is not.
 ▪ Insufficient integration of vulnerable road-user 
needs in routine street improvement Despite some 
targeted initiatives like the SATS program, the needs 
of vulnerable users, such as children, the elderly, and 
people with disabilities, are generally not sufficiently 
integrated into routine street improvement plans. 
BMC’s current capacity to mainstream urban-design-
led approaches in street improvements remains 
limited. Often participatory design efforts led by 
partners get lost in translation  during implementation 
resulting in only surface-level improvements, with 
limited attention to functional aspects such as 
pedestrian safety, accessibility, and climate resilience. 
208 I  wri-india.org

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 209

210 I wri-india.org

CHAPTER 8 
 
Recommendations     
This chapter lays out two sets of recommendations 
for decision-makers within municipal governments, 
urban practitioners, and policymakers, particularly in 
Mumbai. These include creating enabling conditions 
to mainstream safer streets by design and improving 
processes for planning, implementation, and 
sustainability of safer street projects.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 211

CREATE ENABLING 
CONDITIONS FOR 
A TRANSFORMED 
ECOSYSTEM TO 
MAINSTREAM SAFER 
STREETS BY DESIGN 
AS INTEGRAL TO 
SUSTAINABLE AND 
LIVABLE CITIES
1. Institutionalize the idea of  “safe streets save lives” 
as a principle within the sustainable development of 
cities in India, and make road safety and its regular 
monitoring and evaluation a vital policy measure for 
urban transformation
Building on NUTP and various international commit-
ments, local, state, and national governments should 
fundamentally reframe road safety from a public health 
and equity lens and define pathways for implementation as 
part of planned urban development while clarifying diverse 
funding sources. Institutionalization of the idea that streets 
can be made safer through an integrated design approach 
is needed as a fundamental principle to be followed in 
policies, regulations, programs, and projects that impact 
streets in all urban planning and development work.
 ▪ Enable shifts in mindsets at the top level, both 
bureaucratic and political, and change how the road 
safety problem is addressed at a fundamental level 
by shifting the approach from assigning blame and 
penalizing offenders to the safe-system approach, where 
it is acknowledged that people will make mistakes, so 
that roads are designed to forgive human errors and 
minimize their consequences.
 ▪ Engage with the national and state governments 
in pushing the boundaries of the NUTP , which 
promoted the key message #MovePeopleNotCars, to 
include framing the road safety problem as a major 
public health threat and support a campaign on 
#SafeStreetsSaveLives in line with India’s multiple 
international commitments.
 ▪ Build a platform of proactive advocates comprising 
local leaders, other influential individuals, and different 
organizations working on road safety across India, to 
collaborate in changing mindsets at the top level. A lot 
of intent-related work is backed by the research and 
missions of different organizations.
 ▪ Develop non-negotiable principles of safer streets 
within the ecosystem at the national level to be adopted 
by state and local governments to align all city-making 
agendas that target streets so that gains made through 
safe-street designs are not undermined through other 
programs. These must be considered by all actors 
intervening in the streets, whether through routine 
improvement work or planning for new streets.
 ▪ All safe-street design projects must align their vision, 
objectives, and performance indicators with the non-
negotiable principles developed for the ecosystem at 
different levels.
2. Build design capacity within municipal corporations 
to scale up safer-street work
To address the challenges and limitations of current street 
improvement projects in Mumbai, it is recommended that 
a dedicated integrated design cell (IDC) be established 
within the BMC Roads Department. This specialized unit 
should have technical expertise and coordination capacity 
to ensure a holistic approach to street design that leverages 
the co-benefits of climate-action planning, road safety, 
equity and economic development, and urban design 
and planning. Doing so would move beyond superficial 
beautification exercises and address the deeper, more 
critical issues of sustainable and inclusive urban develop-
ment, ultimately enhancing the livability and resilience 
of Mumbai’s streets. The success of the IDC will depend 
on the following:
 ▪ Building cross-departmental coordination and 
support: The IDC must establish an infrastructure 
development unit for fostering collaboration among 
key departments and agencies involved in urban 
governance. This unit should serve as a central hub for 
coordination, issuing NOCs for projects that impact 
street design. Transparent processes must be established 
for engaging stakeholders from the BMC, MTP , 
MMRDA, and PWD, with regular inter-departmental 
meetings to promote communication.
 ▪ Incentivizing stakeholder engagement and 
ownership: The IDC must encourage collaboration 
from local communities, developers, and city 
departments. Incentives should prioritize long-term 
urban resilience over short-term benefits. Engaging 
212 I wri-india.org

communities early in the design process and providing 
transparent feedback mechanisms will lead to 
technically sound and socially inclusive solutions.
 ▪ Embedding flexibility and adaptation mechanisms: 
The IDC must adapt to emerging urban challenges to 
remain effective. Implementing periodic reviews and 
continuous capacity building for the IDC’s team is 
essential for staying current with best practices in urban 
design and social equity.
 ▪ Accountability and performance monitoring: Clear 
accountability structures are crucial for the IDC’s 
functionality. Robust mechanisms should track progress 
and performance in street improvement projects, 
with indicators that include both technical metrics 
(safety and mobility) and social metrics (equity and 
community satisfaction).
 ▪ Leadership and political will: Strong leadership and 
political support are vital for the IDC’s success. Senior 
leaders within the BMC and other agencies should 
advocate for the IDC and ensure adequate resources are 
allocated, overcoming resistance to prioritizing cross-
departmental collaboration in urban governance.
3. Build evidence of the impact of safer streets by 
design on people’s lives and invest in impact and 
evaluation studies
To institutionalize safer streets by design, evidence of 
their impact on people’s lives must be gathered. As it is 
challenging to conduct impact studies and evaluations 
without baseline data, invest in improving road safety data 
quality, its collection, analysis, and access, and identify 
unsafe streets across cities using GIS integrated analysis.
 ▪ Collaborate with national, state, and city-level GIS 
agencies, traffic police, academic institutions, and 
other knowledge pools to develop robust city-level 
baseline data that are annually updated to inform local 
government decisions when they develop inventories of 
streets for improvements.
 ▪ Develop well-maintained and accessible digital 
infrastructure for data to systematize data analysis 
and enable robust impact assessment through the 
availability of comparable data before and after 
implementation.
 ▪ Collaborate with academic institutions to develop 
comparative case studies across India showing the 
relationship between city livability, productivity, 
and safer streets.
4. Build stronger feedback loops and design a strategy 
to better communicate the impact of designing safer 
streets on saving lives
 ▪ Closely monitor and evaluate pilots, projects, and 
programs, and improve feedback loops. Sharing 
well-documented program insights with new 
administrations is essential for projects and programs to 
survive political change.
 ▪ Insights from evaluation studies should be used to 
frame the issue in a directly relevant way to each 
stakeholder. To have a greater impact and shift mental 
models of road safety from a focus on decongestion 
and civil engineering, the evidence of lives saved across 
cities using different urban design approaches must be 
built and communicated more effectively to various 
actors in the ecosystem.
 ▪ The feedback loop needs to include different actors 
with messaging tailored to each actor, such as
 ▪ mid-level municipal engineers, who tend 
to be skeptical of the relevance of new 
design-led processes;
 ▪ decision-makers within municipal corporations 
and regional development agencies, who need 
to understand the effectiveness of data-driven 
designs for safer streets to save lives and are 
motivated to budget for them when planning to 
intervene in streets;
 ▪ state transport and other related departments, 
to persuade them to allocate budgets 
for mainstreaming and scale up safer 
street-by-design work;
 ▪ national government, to whom a compelling case 
must be made for creating a national mission on 
street transformation at scale that will embed 
the right capacity, create enabling policies, and 
sensitize the imagination of people at large 
toward safer streets;
 ▪ media, whose awareness of the different ways of 
thinking about street design and their impact on 
saving lives must be built, to shape public opinion 
on pedestrian safety on streets designed using Safe 
System principles; and
 ▪ civil society, whose support is critical to secure 
ground-level buy-in and demand generation for 
prioritizing safer streets by design in all large 
transport infrastructure and urban development.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 213

5. Develop an integrated research and programming 
agenda for climate change and road safety focusing 
on safe and resilient infrastructure for active travel
Climate change threatens human health, compromises 
road safety, and prevents engagement in healthy and safer 
transport choices such as cycling, walking, and public 
transport during the increasingly more frequent episodes 
of extreme weather. Given the persistent flood risk and 
heat stress threatening Mumbai’s people and infrastructure, 
it is important to develop a comprehensive strategy for 
climate-resilient streets:
 ▪ Address flood risks in streets by identifying flood 
hotspots, improving stormwater infrastructure, 
implementing sustainable urban drainage systems, and 
incorporating green infrastructure solutions to enhance 
water absorption and management.
 ▪ Mitigate urban heat-island effects and worsening 
air quality through strategic tree planting and 
integrating green infrastructure elements like rain 
gardens and bioswales.
 ▪ Further research is needed to explore what makes a 
street (and street network) in Indian cities climate 
resilient using nature-based and context-specific urban 
design solutions for shading.
The journey toward resilient streets in Mumbai can be 
envisioned as a gradual progression, starting with regular 
streets optimized for safety. Building upon this foundation, 
the next step involves greening the streets through strategic 
tree planting and integrating nature-based elements 
to provide shading and mitigate the urban heat-island 
effect. Ultimately, achieving climate resilience requires 
implementing robust stormwater infrastructure capable of 
withstanding heavy rainfall events and minimizing the risk 
of flooding (see Figure 84). Further research is required on 
the nature of the hybrid infrastructure that could combine 
nature-based solutions with the business-as-usual at-scale 
concretization of the city’s streets.

Greening of streets
216 I wri-india.org

IMPROVE PROCESSES 
FOR ADDRESSING 
THE PLANNING, 
IMPLEMENTATION, AND 
SUSTAINABILITY OF SAFER-
STREET PROJECTS
1. Adopt an integrated planning approach to 
infrastructural design
A collective platform or forum for safe streets needs to be 
created at the city level. It will persistently engage with 
the city through workshops, talks, and other means of 
engagement to share new knowledge and ways of planning 
and design to achieve safer streets.
 ▪ Adopt an integrated approach to planning the city’s 
extensive big-ticket transport infrastructure, such as the 
metro rail slated for a large expansion across MMR. 
Leverage the opportunity to promote the idea of 
designing every street that the metro impacts, including 
all metro access streets, using safer street principles.
 ▪ When a large new infrastructure project is planned, 
demand a convening of all organizations working 
on safer streets in the city to assess its impact on 
pedestrian safety and public spaces.
 ▪ Create working groups involving relevant stakeholders 
whose interests and jurisdictions impact the project, 
and co-create solutions based on their understanding of 
context and local challenges.
 ▪ Engage with the CSR cells in municipal corporations 
to define standard operating procedures for utilizing 
CSR funds in street improvement projects. Projects 
carried out using CSR funds must conduct an impact 
assessment of how the proposals will affect pedestrian 
safety and have built-in performance indicators for 
monitoring. These indicators must be developed and 
include equity dimensions for accessing streets and 
public open spaces.
2. Encourage cities to adopt a coordinated approach 
to street improvements that considers the whole 
ecosystem of the street
 ▪ Street improvement must be taken up holistically across 
the entire corridor. Intersection improvements are to 
be undertaken along with the design of the related 
corridors. Intersection design projects implemented in 
isolation are not going to transform streets since most 
of the street beyond the intersection continues to be 
unsafe and inaccessible. An urban-design-led approach 
must include signals, landscaping, and enforcement 
of traffic rules.
 ▪ To ensure seamless coordination and optimize street 
improvement efforts, it is imperative that every 
department within the municipal corporation that 
undertakes work affecting city streets logs both planned 
and completed work in a centralized online system. 
This common platform would serve as a comprehensive 
database accessible to all relevant departments 
and agencies, helping coordinate across different 
departments and agencies and optimize and prioritize 
work through annual improvements. The work has 
to meet standards based on measurable parameters 
to achieve a defined level of service and maintenance 
based on sound asset management principles. Here is a 
more detailed development of this point:
 ▪ Implement a common online system for street 
work coordination: Develop a robust, user-friendly 
online platform where all departments can log their 
planned and completed street-related work. Ensure 
that the system allows for real-time updates and 
notifications, providing up-to-date information on 
ongoing and upcoming projects.
 ▪ Develop data-logging requirements: Each 
department must log detailed information about 
their projects, including start and end dates, 
locations, scope of work, and responsible personnel. 
Ensure project status tracking for each project 
(planned, in progress, completed) to provide a clear 
timeline and progress report.
 ▪ Ensure the platforms’  visibility, transparency, 
coordination, and optimization: Ensure all 
departments and relevant agencies have access to 
the platform, fostering transparency and facilitating 
better coordination. Consider allowing public access 
to certain non-sensitive data to enhance community 
engagement and trust. Use the system to identify 
potential conflicts or overlaps between projects 
and resolve them proactively. Optimize project 
scheduling and prioritization based on the logged 
data to minimize disruptions and ensure efficient 
use of resources.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 217

▪ Establish standards and measurable parameters: 
Measurable parameters and standards for street 
work must ensure each project identifies and 
tracks a defined level of service and maintenance. 
Implement quality assurance protocols, ensuring 
that all work meets the established standards and 
promotes sound asset management principles.
 ▪ Conduct annual improvements and reviews: Use the 
data to conduct annual reviews and improvements, 
analyzing past projects to identify best practices and 
areas for enhancement. Develop key performance 
indicators to measure the effectiveness and 
efficiency of street improvement efforts.
 ▪ Seek political buy-in for a centralized online system 
and build the capacity of municipal actors to develop 
the required discipline to pursue a maintenance 
approach that prioritizes the protection of existing road 
assets and does not duplicate or undermine the work 
done on safe streets due to the uncoordinated work of 
different agencies.
3. Plan and design safer streets to integrate climate 
resilience strategies for mitigation and adaptation by 
adopting cleaner and greener technologies, nature-
based solutions, and risk-informed planning to 
manage and recover from growing climate risks and 
extreme weather events
Embed climate resilience as a core principle in all street 
improvement projects, ensuring that every aspect of street 
design and construction considers long-term climate 
impacts from mitigation and adaptation perspectives. 
Develop and implement policies that mandate climate 
resilience in street design and construction by using green 
and clean technologies and building materials. Ensure 
these policies are backed by scientific research and local 
climate data to reduce GHG emissions, improve flood and 
heat resilience, and encourage a mode shift to walking and 
NMT . Advocate for legislation that requires infrastructure 
projects to incorporate nature-based solutions as important 
resilience measures, protecting urban areas from known 
and anticipated climate risks.
 ▪ Use of Geographic Information Systems (GIS) 
for understanding differential exposure and 
vulnerability: Use GIS to identify the areas at risk of 
flooding and extreme heat stress, overlay the flood- 
and heat-sensitive areas with the road network area, 
and conduct an exposure analysis and vulnerability 
assessment for identified streets. Utilize satellite 
imagery and remote sensing technologies to monitor 
environmental changes and assess the impact on urban 
infrastructure.
 ▪ Climate impact assessments of materials used in 
pedestrian infrastructure: Conduct detailed climate 
impact assessments of all footpath materials used in city 
streets. Evaluate the environmental costs and benefits 
of different materials, avoiding those that contribute 
to urban heat-islands or impede water infiltration. 
Avoid solutions that lock into single pathways, such as 
extensive concretization. Instead, adopt design thinking 
to explore flexible, adaptive approaches that can evolve 
with changing climate conditions.
 ▪ Development of climate-sensitive street design 
solutions: Integrate green infrastructure, such as 
permeable pavements, green roofs, and rain gardens, 
into street designs to manage stormwater, reduce heat 
stress, and enhance urban biodiversity. Promote nature-
based solutions, like urban forests and green corridors, 
to mitigate the impacts of extreme weather events and 
improve overall urban resilience.
 ▪ Prioritize community engagement and monitoring 
and evaluation: Engage local communities in the 
planning and implementation of climate-resilient 
street projects by ensuring public input is considered 
in decision-making processes. Conduct awareness 
campaigns to educate residents about the benefits 
of resilient street designs and encourage community 
participation in maintaining and protecting these 
infrastructures. Establish systems for continuous 
monitoring of street performance under various climate 
conditions. Develop clear metrics for evaluating 
the success of climate resilience measures in street 
design, ensuring that projects meet established goals 
for sustainability and safety. Use the data to inform 
ongoing improvements and adjustments.
4. Conduct crash investigations, improve crash 
data collection processes, and use data in an 
integrated manner
 ▪ Crash documentation: This is currently based on crime 
reporting, which limits documentation from a road 
safety perspective. Facilitate the collection of data not 
from a fault-assignment perspective but to understand 
road safety circumstances. For this, the collection of 
data on non-fatal injuries is crucial. The Integrated 
Road Accident Database (iRAD)/e-Detailed Accident 
Report (eDAR) Project, a MoRTH initiative funded 
218 I wri-india.org

by the World Bank, has tried to look at crash reporting 
more broadly. However, the law-and-order police, who 
are entrusted with data collection from crash sites, have 
been hesitant to adopt it.
 ▪ Crash investigation: Differentiate crash investigations 
from crime investigations and have a trained crash 
investigation unit to facilitate more systematic 
investigation and documentation of crashes.
 ▪ Integration with hospital data: Additional 
information from hospitals is important to close 
the loop of crash documentation and investigation. 
An attempt is underway with eDAR, and full 
implementation of reporting through eDAR could 
assist in this integration.
 ▪ Mandating use of data by road safety lead agencies: 
Road safety lead agencies, at least at the state level, 
could be places that have the mandate and capacity to 
access and use crash data to inform policy and suggest 
specific interventions. No institution currently has this 
mandate. The MTP do, to some extent, but purely from 
a policing perspective. Data quality does not improve 
until data are being used. Only when the government 
tries to use these data will feedback in data collection 
processes be taken seriously.
5.  Use surrogate safety events as a preventive analytic 
and alternative to crash, fatality, and injury data
Given the significant limitations of traditional evaluation 
methods based on crash, fatality, and injury data, which 
require extensive historical data and reactive measures, 
a shift toward proactive traffic conflict analysis is 
recommended. Traditional methods necessitate waiting for 
a substantial number of crashes to occur and be recorded 
before implementing safety measures. This process needs 
three to five years of data collection before and after 
implementation. This paradoxically delays the prevention 
of crashes, particularly in countries like India, where crash-
data quality is poor and often unreliable. Studies have 
shown that traffic conflict analysis can serve as an effective 
surrogate for crashes, enabling earlier and more proactive 
interventions (Autey et al. 2012; Sayed et al. 2013; Wang 
and Stamatiadis 2013; Tarko 2019). This proactive strategy 
mitigates reliance on long-term crash data and improves 
the overall safety and efficiency of road systems, ultimately 
saving lives and reducing injuries more effectively 
(El-Basyouny and Sayed 2013).
 ▪ Encourage road safety and traffic engineering 
practitioners to adopt advancements in sensing 
technologies and statistical modeling for traffic conflict 
analysis for all public projects.
 ▪ Leverage modern sensing technologies, such as high-
resolution cameras and sensors, and advanced data 
analytics, to identify and address potential safety issues 
before they result in crashes and identify “greyspots” 
that could become blackspots if unaddressed.
 ▪ Implement surrogate measures to predict and prevent 
crashes, provide timely interventions, and enhance 
the ability to perform safety analysis on a larger scale 
cost-effectively.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 219

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 221

APPENDIX B
Formal and informal rules of the game 
Formal rules
1. CITY MOBILITY PLAN (CMP) 
The BMC conducted the Comprehensive Mobility Plan 
Study (LEA Associates South Asia 2016) to coincide with the 
horizon year of the new Development Plan (DP) 2014–2034. 
It suggested phase-wise implementation of safety and traffic 
management measures while taking cognizance of crash data 
in proposing a priority list of streets and junctions to be taken 
up for improvement. Even though the CMP is a non-statutory 
transport plan, it informed the DP. CMPs are designed to 
create a long-term vision for a city’s transportation network, 
integrating various modes of transport to improve mobility. 
While CMPs aim to reduce traffic congestion and promote 
sustainable transportation, their data collection efforts are 
heavily skewed toward vehicular traffic patterns. Data collected 
for the CMP primarily focus on vehicular volume and flow, 
speeds and travel times, road network capacity and efficiency, 
and public transport ridership. Data related to pedestrian and 
cyclist movements, including in the context of public transport 
use, are often underrepresented or neglected. This macro focus 
leads to a lack of detailed understanding of the conditions of 
the intersection of infrastructure and people, particularly the 
way it impacts non-motorized transport users.
2. COMPREHENSIVE TRANSPORTATION STUDY (CTS)
The MMRDA conducted the CTS in 2021 as an update on the 
CMP, including detailed investigations into the traffic dynamics 
of the metropolitan region. It proposes long-term visions 
and plans for improving Mumbai’s mobility infrastructure. 
Although it deploys critical studies essential for understanding 
current traffic congestion and planning for future needs, the 
result of this focus on congestion emphasizes vehicular data, 
particularly vehicle counts and flows, origin and destination of 
vehicles, travel-time delays, and parking utilization. Insufficient 
information on pedestrian and cyclist movements, behaviors, 
and needs underscores the failure of the CTS to adequately 
address safety and infrastructure requirements for non-
motorized transport users.
3. CITY DEVELOPMENT PLAN (DP)
Development Plans (DPs) in Mumbai are statutory plans 
under the Maharashtra Regional and Town Planning (MR&TP) 
Act, 1966 (Government of Maharashtra, Law and Judiciary 
Department 1967) with 20-year horizons. The DP reserves 
land for road widening and proposes new roads, missing 
links, non-motorized transport streets, and public parking 
lots, among other things. In addition, the DP develops legally 
binding land use and development control regulations for the 
city that shape the spatial structure and urban form, including 
street edges. Thus, the DP provides vital openings for creating 
pedestrian-friendly infrastructure in the city and helps achieve 
several branding goals for the city’s image. DPs outline land 
use and infrastructure development strategies, and while they 
consider various aspects of urban development, their focus 
is often on land allocation for vehicular infrastructure and 
broader urban growth patterns. The data collected include 
land use patterns, zoning regulations, vehicular infrastructure 
capacity, and projected population growth. This means 
that the specific needs of pedestrians and cyclists, though 
they represent half of all trips in Mumbai, are not sufficiently 
integrated into the planning process. Even though the DP 
proposes the pedestrianization of eight streets in the city from 
the CMP, which are largely market streets, and the creation of 
cycling tracks on one of the arterial streets, it does not mandate 
them. The DP also proposes methods of acquiring land for 
creating safe-street infrastructure but does not lay the roadmap 
or identify design regulations specific to streets. Unless 
addressed, this can reproduce an urban environment that is 
unfriendly and unsafe for non-motorized transport users.
4. BOTTLENECK POLICY (2017)
Land acquisition for development work in Mumbai presents 
considerable challenges due to the traditional land acquisition 
approaches under the MR&TP Act of 1966. Land acquisition 
is time-consuming and legally complicated, involving multiple 
authorities outside the BMC. To address these challenges, 
the BMC resorted to innovations such as the Kurar pattern, 
initially an experiment at ward level that allowed additional 
vertical reconstruction space when more than 50 percent 
of any structure is affected by development work. This 
minimizes the need for costly alternative space and reduces 
the displacement of street vendors. It later became integrated 
into the Bottleneck Policy developed in 2017 and incorporated 
in the draft Development Control and Promotion Regulations 
(DCPR) 2034. This allowed vertical or horizontal expansion 
at the same location. In case of removal of structures due to 
road infrastructure work, the projects affected, in line with the 
Kurar pattern, are accommodated within the same ward, along 
with an additional Floor Space Index (FSI) of up to 50 percent, 
as an incentive.
5. FLOOR SPACE INDEX (FSI), TRANSFERABLE 
DEVELOPMENT RIGHTS (TDR), AND DEVELOPMENT 
CONTROL AND PROMOTION REGULATIONS (DCPR)
Mumbai has faced significant challenges in implementing 
its DPs, with only 33 percent of the 1991 DP and 18 percent 
of the 1967 DP being realized. To overcome the barriers to 
acquiring land for urban development, Mumbai used various 
strategies such as the introduction of the Floor Space Index 
(FSI) in 1964 and Transferable Development Rights (TDR) in 
1991 as transactional tools, enabling land acquisition without 
extensive financial outlays. Due to the limited quantity of vacant 
land in Greater Mumbai, most development is expected to 
222 I wri-india.org

occur through the redevelopment of private plots. DCPR 2034 
mandates that redevelopment projects contribute land for 
public purposes, including streets and public spaces.
6. MUMBAI PARKING MANAGEMENT PLAN
Indiscriminately parked cars majorly encroach on the streets 
of Mumbai and lead to further congestion. DCPR 2034 made 
provisions to establish the Mumbai Parking Authority to look 
after the parking needs of the city and come up with the 
Mumbai Parking Management Plan. Until then, the Central 
Roads Department of the BMC was looking after the city’s 
parking needs. The Mumbai Parking Authority has identified 
parking and no-parking zones in all 24 wards in Mumbai 
through surveys and GIS mapping. It has also prepared parking 
policies and a pricing mechanism for parking. It is currently 
in the process of developing an application that will have a 
database of all the parking facilities in Mumbai through which 
citizens can reserve and pay for parking, allowing the BMC to 
regulate parking in the city.
7. CITY CORPORATION’S ROUTINE STREET 
IMPROVEMENT WORK
Every year, the BMC’s Roads Department identifies new 
roads, missing links, and roads for widening as suggested 
in the DP, which in turn is informed by the CMP or the 
Comprehensive Traffic and Transportation Study (CTTS 2008)   
(LEA International Ltd. 2008). The MTP, members of Advanced 
Locality Managements (ALMs), residents’ associations, and 
elected representatives also suggest roads for improvements. 
The scope for such improvements is defined by technical 
documents such as tender documents, General Conditions of 
Contract, and Standard Bidding Documents, and managing 
these projects (from planning to implementation) comes under 
the direct purview of engineers in the BMC’s Roads Department 
with funding earmarked from the BMC’s annual budget.
8. BMC CIRCULARS
The BMC issues circulars or policy documents from time to 
time to provide technical or procedural guidance related to 
the construction of different elements of the street such as 
footpath, central medians, curbs, mix and selection of materials, 
procurement of services, safety practices in construction sites, 
and contractual issues, among others.
9. MAHATMA GANDHI PATHKRANTI YOJANA (2006)
This was a program to rid pavements of pavement dwellers and 
their makeshift hutments. People with proof of having resided 
on the pavement before 1995 were eligible for alternative 
accommodation. An estimated 25,000 pavement dwellers 
were identified under the project in 2006 to make 125 roads 
encroachment-free. However, only 20 roads in the city could be 
made encroachment-free using this program by rehabilitating 
2,643 pavement dwellers. Relocation to state-owned housing 
often takes a long time, robbing several pavement dwellers 
of housing rights and leading to legal challenges that have 
delayed the implementation of the program.
10. SETTING UP NO-HAWKING ZONES OUTSIDE 
RAILWAY STATIONS
In September 2017, the Elphinstone railway station’s foot 
overbridge in Mumbai collapsed due to a stampede as people 
sought shelter on the covered bridge from the rains (H. Kumar 
2017). Many lost their lives, and many more were injured. 
Following the incident, the railway authorities formed 13 multi-
disciplinary teams to inspect and assess the infrastructure of 
the entire suburban Mumbai network and give suggestions for 
making them safer. One of the prominent recommendations 
was to decongest a 50-meter area outside railway stations 
by declaring them no-hawking zones. In November 2017, the 
Bombay High Court also directed the municipal corporation to 
create specific vending zones for hawkers and not allow them 
on footbridges and overbridges The order further stipulated 
that the city should ban hawking within 100 meters of any place 
of worship, holy shrine, educational institution, and hospital and 
within 150 meters of any municipal or other market or railway 
station—all areas where footfalls for business were significant 
and that are natural sites for informal commerce. While vendors 
can still be seen in many areas outside railway stations in 
Mumbai, the continuous eviction drives by the corporation 
have also resulted in vendors moving out and illegal parking 
taking over the vacant spaces. Even though street hawking is 
generally seen as a “menace” or an “eyesore” by the corporation 
and law enforcement, many urbanists and planners have 
argued in their favor for offering crucial services to pedestrians 
and providing natural surveillance by having “eyes on the 
street” (Jacobs 1992).
11. CORPORATORS’ FUNDS AND MP AND MLA LOCAL AREA 
DEVELOPMENT FUNDS
Corporators, or elected representatives, get funds for 
undertaking various kinds of development work within their 
constituency, including street development work. These funds 
are routed through the BMC and allocated based on proposals 
put forth by the corporators. Several proposals initiated by 
the elected representatives relate to beautification projects 
and installations of various kinds on traffic islands and plazas 
on streets. Some projects such as the Irla Nullah walking and 
cycling track were funded using these local funds.
12. MUMBAI BEAUTIFICATION PROJECT (MBP) (2022)
Ahead of the G-20 Summit, Prime Minister Narendra Modi, 
in his address to the mayors of urban bodies across India, 
highlighted the need for the “beautification of cities.” The 
BMC launched the MBP project on the directive from the 
Chief Minister of Maharashtra to change the aesthetic quality 
and visual appeal of the city’s public infrastructure (HT 
Correspondent 2022). The BMC allocated INR 1700 crores 
($202.2 million) for the project in the BMC budget, of which INR 
Safer Streets in Mumbai
 I 223

300 crores ($35.7 million) were allocated from the corporation’s 
contingency fund, and INR 670 crores ($79.7 million) from the 
corporators’ fund. This was the first time that additional funds 
were allocated directly to the ward apart from the annual 2 
crores for regular maintenance and repair works. Vigilance 
committees have been set up at the ward level for quality 
control. Initiatives under this project aim at enhancing the city’s 
overall appeal but also include tasks like road resurfacing and 
pothole filling, adding greenery to medians and footpaths, 
installing street furniture and attractive streetlights, illuminating 
skywalks, removing unnecessary overhead wires, beautifying 
traffic islands, and artistic enhancements of public walls. MBP 
has the potential to drive a coherent citywide beautification 
vision focusing on streets and public spaces off the streets that 
are not just aesthetically pleasing but also functional, inclusive, 
resilient to climate change, and welcoming of all citizens with 
different abilities and backgrounds.
13. CSR (CORPORATE SOCIAL RESPONSIBILITY) 
INITIATIVES (SINCE 2014)
Indian law requires large companies whose worth, turnover, 
and profits exceed a certain level to spend a minimum of 2 
percent of their average net profits on social initiatives. In 2013, 
the BMC established a CSR Cell under the planning department 
to match the needs of the city with CSR initiatives (Patwa 
2013). Even though several small projects of beautifying traffic 
islands and street medians have been funded through CSR, in 
recent years, this funding has been extended to improvements 
of entire streets and upgrading access to residential or office 
campuses. The “One Green Mile Project” sponsored by Nucleus 
Office Parks is a case in point. Such projects are often designed 
by leading architects who create aesthetic value through 
high-quality design and by specifying higher-value materials, 
which are typically much higher than the BMC-mandated 
Schedule of Rates.
14. POLICY CIRCULAR FOR BEAUTIFICATION OF TRAFFIC 
ISLANDS AND ROAD CENTRAL DIVIDERS (2010)
The traffic islands and central dividers on roads are seen as 
legitimate sites for targeting beautification efforts, and given the 
importance of landscaping in beautification, this BMC circular 
made its garden department responsible for the development 
and maintenance of greenery and any vegetation on footpaths, 
medians, and traffic islands. Expressions of interest are 
sought from various sponsors, but commercial advertising 
is banned in these spaces, with only courtesy boards of a 
specified size allowed.
15. HERITAGE-LED CITY AND STREET REVITALIZATION
South Mumbai is known for its heritage structures, cultural 
centers, and iconic promenades, and has witnessed a range 
of initiatives led by city authorities and associations like the 
Horniman Circle Association and the Kala Ghoda Association to 
preserve, maintain, and revitalize specific precincts of historic 
and cultural significance. The initiatives include streetscape 
revitalization, facelifts for iconic promenades, and hosting street 
art festivals that seek to democratize art. Key  projects that have 
contributed to these goals include the Kala Ghoda Art Festival 
(1998) that brought together art enthusiasts, galleries, and 
citizens to celebrate culture and restore the area’s vibrancy; 
the development of Urban Signage Guidelines for Horniman 
Circle and DN Road (1998) through a participatory approach, 
catalyzing the creation of citizen’s associations and leading to 
municipal commitment to adopt urban design guidelines; the 
refurbishment of Marine Drive Promenade (2002) that adopted 
a comprehensive approach to urban planning by understanding 
environmental impact, socio-cultural behavior, circulation and 
movement patterns; and stakeholder interests in the proposals 
developed through competitive process. A new initiative by the 
BMC is to makeover footpaths in heritage precincts by making 
them pedestrian-friendly.
16. PUBLIC INTEREST LITIGATIONS (PIL s)
A PIL (PIL-18 of 2010) filed by the Bombay Bar Association 
on January 29, 2010, to address various issues of traffic 
management in the city of Mumbai prompted discussions 
regarding safe pedestrian infrastructure at a citywide scale 
for the first time. Responding to this PIL, the HHCB bench 
at Mumbai directed the Home Department, Government of 
Maharashtra, on May 7, 2012, to constitute a HPEC to suggest 
long-term, comprehensive, legal, corrective, and remedial 
measures to ensure smooth and disciplined vehicular and 
pedestrian movement in the city. In 2013, a suo moto PIL was 
filed in Bombay High Court against the State Government, 
based on which the Court ordered concerned institutions 
(BMC/MMRDA) in 2015 to improve the bad conditions of 
roads. The BMC acted on the court order, issuing a circular 
in 2018 for improving the quality of roads by using cement 
concrete, setting up grievance redressal mechanisms with 
toll-free numbers, and in 2019 it developed an app for lodging 
complaints on the quality of roads. That same year the footpath 
improvement policy was finalized to improve the quality of 
materials to prolong the life of the constructed footpaths. The 
results of the 2013 PIL were mostly material improvements of 
pedestrian infrastructure instead of improving the planning and 
design of the city’s walking environment.
17. PEDESTRIAN FIRST POLICY (2016)
This was the first comprehensive policy that considered the 
design, construction, and maintenance of footpaths to provide 
safe and comfortable walking spaces in Mumbai. The policy 
was broadly derived from the guidelines set by the IRC in 2012. 
Under this policy, the BMC planned to standardize the process 
of street construction and create footpaths to facilitate easy 
pedestrian movement. The BMC is set to carry out a survey of 
all city roads with a minimum width of nine meters, following 
which footpaths will be constructed. It budgeted funds of 
INR 200 crores ($23.8 million) in the 2023–24 BMC budget 
for this purpose.
224 I wri-india.org

18. GLOBAL PROGRAMS ADVOCATING FOR ROAD SAFETY 
ACROSS THE WORLD
The Bloomberg Philanthropies Initiative for Global Road 
Safety (BIGRS) aims to save lives and reduce injuries from 
traffic crashes by supporting the implementation of road 
safety interventions. The program entails conducting 
assessments on high-risk roads, offering recommendations 
for safety enhancements, providing technical expertise on 
street design, advocating for effective speed-management 
strategies, capacity building, and aiding governments in the 
implementation of crash data management systems. In 2015, 
BIGRS funded 10 cities globally to implement the program. 
Mumbai was one of the cities that secured high-level buy-in 
from the state and national governments. Several projects 
under this program triggered street transformations in the 
city and helped further the road safety agenda from an urban 
design lens with the BMC as the implementing partner. Notable 
projects included improving various junctions and streets 
through design, a Safe Access to School project to make 
walking to school safer for children, the Mumbai Street Lab 
initiative for securing inputs from urban designers on street 
design, and the empanelment of urban design consultants by 
the BMC to work on future street design projects.
B. Informal rules
CITIZENS DRIVING ACTION AT THE LOCAL LEVEL 
FOR BETTER STREETS
There are several examples of citizens petitioning their local 
elected representatives and ward offices to demand better 
streets and public spaces. The improvements to Bandra 
Bandstand and Carter Road are examples of such citizen 
demand for reclaiming public spaces along the coastline and 
providing walkways, seating areas, and recreational spaces, 
offering residents and visitors a place to relax, exercise, and 
enjoy the coastal views. Similarly, multi-stakeholder demand 
for safe mobility led to the Equal Streets movement in 2014 that 
temporarily closed selected roads on Sundays to make car-free 
zones for walking, jogging, cycling, and engaging in outdoor 
recreational activities. Equal Streets was a collaboration 
between various organizations, including non-governmental 
organizations (NGOs), corporate entities, and citizens’ groups 
to reclaim the streets of Mumbai and create safe and inclusive 
spaces for pedestrians and non-motorized transport. Mumbai 
also has several activist architects who are vocal about their 
criticism of automobile-centric projects. The recent uproar 
against the Coastal Road projects is a case in point.
SUMMARY OF RULES OF THE GAME
While there are multiple sets of overlapping rules that 
govern drivers, the fact that all have a few common rules 
that drive action is notable. These include the city’s statutory 
Development Plan 2014–34 and the City Mobility Plan (2016), 
both of which have proposed new roads and missing links; 
corporator’s funds and MP and MLA local area development 
funds, which are leveraged beyond beautification to provide 
safer and better streets and public spaces in local areas; and 
the BMC circulars that guide the process of delivering improved 
streets. These range across the policies, plans, and programs 
of national, state, regional, and local governments, as well as 
interventions by courts and initiatives taken by media, civil 
society, and citizens.
Source: Authors.
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 225

APPENDIX C
Key actors and their roles in 
transforming streets in Mumbai
TABLE C-1 |  Details of the roles played by key institutions, organizations, and groups
ACTORS INSTITUTIONS, ORGANIZATIONS, GROUPS ROLE
Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) Plays a pivotal role in shaping and enhancing urban 
infrastructure across India through the promotion of national 
programs such as the Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban 
Transformation and JNNURM. Mumbai leveraged JNNURM to 
strengthen its public transportation system by acquiring BEST 
buses. 
National
Ministry of Road Transport and Highways 
(MORTH)
Plays a crucial role in overseeing and enhancing road safety 
in India. MoRTH develops the IRC guidelines to control 
quality and ensure the safety of roads and highways in India. 
It provides various planning-level guidelines and design 
standards for safe pedestrian infrastructure and public spaces 
in urban areas, but these are not legally binding regulations.
MoRTH is also responsible for standardizing the data 
collection process for road crashes across India. This 
includes the promotion of detailed road accident recording 
forms intended to capture comprehensive data about crash 
incidents. To address the limitations of traditional FIR-based 
reporting, MoRTH introduced the Electronic Detailed Accident 
Report (eDAR) system. This system is designed to capture 
detailed information about road crashes, including those that 
do not result in FIRs, thereby providing a more holistic view of 
road safety incidents. eDAR also includes a hospital module 
aimed at integrating data from different sources to improve 
accuracy and comprehensiveness.
All India Institute of Local Self-Government 
(AIILSG)
Works on improving local governance and building the 
capacity of urban local bodies for improved service delivery 
and localization of national and international development 
goals. AIILSG has been an instrumental consultant in revising 
the DP , a key part of the committee to set up the Mumbai 
Parking Authority and also drives many tactical urbanism 
projects within the BMC.
226 I wri-india.org

ACTORS INSTITUTIONS, ORGANIZATIONS, GROUPS ROLE
State
Public Works Department (PWD), Maharashtra Involved in planning and implementing big infrastructure 
projects. The PWD is the nodal agency for planning, tendering, 
construction, and maintenance of state highways, bridges, 
government buildings, and roads.
Transport Department, Government of 
Maharashtra
As lead agency for urban transport in the state of Maharashtra, 
the Transport Department is responsible for the enforcement 
of provisions under the Motor Vehicles Act & Rules and the 
licensing, registration, maintenance, and testing of vehicles. It 
holds the road safety fund for the state and runs events and 
campaigns for raising awareness about road safety.
Urban Development Department, Government of 
Maharashtra
State-level nodal ministry for planning and coordinating urban 
transport.
Maharashtra State Road Development 
Corporation Limited (MSRDC)
MSRDC is an independent state-level corporation that has 
overseen the construction of road overpasses in the city. 
MSRDC awards road-building contracts to private construction 
companies and to itself.
Maharashtra District Road Safety Committee 
(MDRSC)
Presided over by the District Collector, the committee takes 
stock of road safety updates from the BMC, MTP , and the 
transport department.
Regional   
Mumbai Metropolitan Region Development 
Authority (MMRDA)
The MMRDA has proposed, planned, and implemented a 
series of urban development initiatives that seek to decongest 
the island city and promotes transportation infrastructure 
projects to better connect the suburbs.
Unified Mumbai Metropolitan Transport Authority 
(UMMTA) under MMRDA
UMMTA coordinates all institutional actors involved in 
transport and oversees the integrated development of various 
modes of transport in Mumbai.
Brihanmumbai Municipal Corporation (BMC) The BMC is responsible for creating Mumbai’s development 
plan, construction and maintenance of roads, managing 
parking, providing vending licenses, coordinating with 
corporates for CSR funding for municipal projects, and 
redressing various public grievances, including about 
conditions of streets. Several kms of major and minor streets 
in Mumbai are improved every year by the BMC. The scope for 
such improvements is clearly defined by technical documents, 
and they typically prioritize smooth, unobstructed vehicle 
movement. Funds for improvements are allotted in the BMC 
annual budget.
TABLE C-1 |  Details of the roles played by key institutions, organizations and groups (cont’d.)
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 227

ACTORS INSTITUTIONS, ORGANIZATIONS, GROUPS ROLE
City
Mumbai Traffic Police (MTP) The MTP enforces traffic rules in Greater Mumbai and 
manages on-road traffic in the city. They also conduct road 
safety awareness campaigns through all forms of media.
Elected representatives (MP/MLA/MLC/
Councilors)
Mumbai elects 227 municipal councilors (called corporators) 
for the electoral wards every five years. Every corporator 
gets INR 10 million ($118,944) as an infrastructure fund (not 
limited to streets) and an additional INR 6 million ($71,367) 
as councilor funds. These funds get routed through the BMC 
wards based on proposals forwarded by the councilor and 
approved by the ward committee. Councilors can also secure 
additional funding from the municipal corporation based on 
the extent of the work proposed. These projects are executed 
by the BMC. Each MP is allocated INR 50 million ($594,722), 
and each MLA INR 20 million ($237,889) every year by the 
district authority for development projects. These projects 
are generally implemented by state agencies like the SRA or 
MHADA. 
High-Powered Executive Committee (HPEC) Responding to a PIL in 2010, the Bombay High Court directed 
the Government of Maharashtra to constitute a high-powered 
executive committee to suggest long-term, comprehensive, 
legal, corrective, and remedial measures to ensure smooth 
and disciplined vehicular and pedestrian movement in 
the city. This committee was set up in 2013 under the 
chairmanship of the Additional Chief Secretary (Home) and 
included members from the Transport Department, Urban 
Development Department, BEST, MMRDA, BMC, MTP , and civil 
society stakeholders, such as the Western India Automobile 
Association, Mumbai Environment Social Network, and 
EMBARQ India. Their report acknowledged for the first time, 
among other things, how “the lack of good street design 
features, including proper sidewalks and crosswalks, forces 
pedestrians to use the carriageway meant for vehicles,” 
thereby endangering their safety as well as the safety of 
other road users across Mumbai city. In its 52-point action 
plan, citing the Comprehensive Traffic and Transport Study of 
Mumbai (CTTS 2008) and its findings, particularly that 42% of 
all trips in Mumbai are walking trips and 80% of people access 
public transport by walking, it not only mandated the provision 
of adequate and contiguous pedestrian infrastructure, 
including sidewalks, crosswalks, and associated signage and 
markings, but also proposed, for the first time, that the BMC 
adopt a policy to re-design existing junctions to make them 
pedestrian-friendly (Rajan et al. 2013).
Brihanmumbai Electric Supply and Transport 
(BEST)
This is a civic transport and electricity provider public body. It 
installs streetlights as well as bus stops on footpaths. When 
street improvement projects disrupt traffic, BEST is consulted 
by the BMC to reroute buses and relocate utilities.
Advanced Locality Management (ALM) This is an institutional arrangement promoted by the BMC 
to partner with citizens to solve the city’s solid waste 
management problems. Nodal officers are appointed by the 
BMC and are responsible for conducting monthly meetings 
with ALM representatives drawn from various Residential 
Welfare Associations (RWAs) and housing societies in an 
area regarding various civic issues but primarily the correct 
disposal of waste to make streets and public spaces safe and 
clean.
TABLE C-1  |   Details of the roles played by key institutions, organizations and groups (cont’d.)
228 I wri-india.org

ACTORS INSTITUTIONS, ORGANIZATIONS, GROUPS ROLE
Ward offices Wards are the lowest level of decentralization of urban local 
governments. Ward offices are present in all 24 administrative 
wards of the BMC and the ward officers are responsible for all 
municipal services in their areas. Every ward has a specific 
time allotted during the week where citizens can meet the 
ward officer regarding grievances such as bad footpaths, 
crashes, potholes, and drainage issues, which are then 
addressed by the BMC. The wards usually undertake projects 
related to beautification, projects initiated by the ward 
councilors, proposals initiated by ALMs and RWAs, and CSR 
initiatives.
Mumbai Transformation Support Unit (MTSU) This is a think tank that was established in 2005 by the World 
Bank, the Cities Alliance, USAID, and the Government of 
Maharashtra under the aegis of AIILSG and was shut down 
in 2019 by the state government. It served as an interface 
between the government and all other stakeholders and 
worked on diverse projects ranging across physical and 
social infrastructure, environment, housing, governance, and 
strategic planning. Transportation system improvements were 
an area of interest for MTSU, and they conducted a few pilot 
studies for transit-oriented development and improving roads 
in connection with large infrastructure projects.
Courts
Supreme Court of India In 2014, the Supreme Court issued a notice to the national 
and all state governments seeking their response on steps 
taken by them to remove encroachments from pavements 
and footpaths based on a PIL that sought the Supreme Court’s 
direction for framing guidelines for the same.
Bombay High Court The Bombay High Court has repeatedly upheld the rights of 
citizens to good and obstruction-free footpaths (including 
obstructions posed by vendors) and good-quality roads and 
held the BMC accountable for securing these rights for the 
people of Mumbai.
Civil society organizations
Local organizations Efforts by the likes of the Horniman Circle Association and the 
Kala Ghoda Association led to streetscape revitalization, a 
makeover of iconic promenades, and street art festivals, with 
the goal of improving the city’s image as a welcoming and 
culturally vibrant city.
Global organizations These supply knowledge and expertise on road safety and 
funding. The Global Road Safety Initiative by Bloomberg 
Philanthropies works at both the national level to strengthen 
road safety legislation and the city level to implement proven 
road safety interventions. Mumbai was one of the cities 
chosen by them to participate in the global road safety 
program. WRI India supported the initiative by providing 
technical support to the BMC for implementing  road safety 
pilot projects and building the capacity of BMC staff to scale 
up safe-street designs.
NGOs and think tanks Local NGOs and think tanks like WRI India, the Global 
Designing Cities Initiative (GDCI), National Association of City 
Transport Officials (NACTO), and the Institute for Transportation 
and Development Policy (ITDP) provide technical support to 
city government agencies to address capacity gaps. They also 
contribute time and resources to support local governments 
in executing projects more efficiently.
TABLE C-1  |   Details of the roles played by key institutions, organizations and groups (cont’d.)
Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 229

ACTORS INSTITUTIONS, ORGANIZATIONS, GROUPS ROLE
Corporates
Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) Many street beautification projects, maintenance work for 
traffic islands and medians, and street art installations are 
carried out through CSR initiatives. Some street furniture and 
street amenities are also developed using CSR funds.
Media Print media, digital media, social media, TV and 
radio
These have played an important role in shaping public opinion 
on the condition of the city’s streets; analyzing expenditures 
on street improvements in terms of quality achieved; 
publicizing data on road crashes, fatalities, and injuries; and 
holding the government accountable for street works.
Citizens The initiatives undertaken by the Bandra West Residents’ 
Association, Bandra Bandstand Residents’ Trust, veteran 
actor Shabana Azmi, and architect P . K. Das brought together 
residents, activists, and professionals to collaborate and 
work toward a common goal of reclaiming public spaces 
along the coastline and creating safe and pleasing walking 
environments. This community-driven approach fostered a 
sense of ownership and pride among the local community, 
leading to long-term stewardship of the public spaces. Citizen-
initiated campaigns led to the Equal Streets movement, as 
well as the Ranwar Village festival when the residents of 
Ranwar Street in Bandra (West) celebrate the Portuguese 
heritage of Ranwar Village with a pre-Christmas street festival 
by temporarily making the street car-free.

7 Tabletop 
crossings 
and speed 
humps
Tabletop 
crossing: 
A raised 
pedestrian 
crossing that 
slows vehicles 
down as 
pedestrians 
cross, either at 
an intersection 
or a midblock 
location. 
Speed hump: 
Artificial 
elevations 
on the street 
ideally 
designed to 
enable vehicles 
to travel at 
a consistent 
target speed 
along a road, 
rather than 
slowing down 
and speeding 
up before and 
after each 
hump. They 
can also help 
to reduce 
speeds to a 
certain limit 
based on the 
height and 
length of the 
hump.
Studies from Norway 
show that humps 
reduce the number 
of crashes causing 
injury for a given 
amount of traffic by 
around 50 percent 
(Welle et al. 2015, 31).
Traffic volume goes 
down where humps 
are introduced. 
Studies show that 
on average, the 
reduction in traffic is 
around 25 percent 
(Welle et al. 2015, 31).
On average, newly 
installed humps 
reduce mean vehicle 
speeds from 47.7 to 
36.6 km/hr (Elvik et 
al. 2009, 455).  
In Seoul, Korea, 
crashes decreased 
by 39 percent in 
school zones after 
improved design 
and traffic calming 
measures were put 
in place (Welle et al. 
2015, 59).

10 Road 
markings
Painted markings 
on the street to 
communicate 
information to the 
users to facilitate 
their smooth, 
orderly, and 
safe movement 
across the street. 
They are most 
commonly used to 
define travel lanes, 
indicate stop lines 
for vehicles before 
a pedestrian 
crossing, indicate 
direction of 
movement, 
prohibit certain 
movements, and 
mark facilities 
such as bus stops 
and parking, etc.
Road markings 
perform an 
important function 
of guiding and 
controlling traffic 
on a highway. The 
markings serve as 
a psychological 
barrier and thus 
help to signify the 
delineation of traffic 
paths and their 
lateral clearance 
from traffic hazards, 
facilitating safe 
movement. They are 
recommended by 
IRC guidelines (IRC 
2015, 2)  to ensure 
the smooth and 
orderly flow of traffic 
and to promote road 
safety.

locations along 
the street to 
give instructions 
or provide 
information to 
the users. They 
are used to 
notify users of 
regulations and 
provide warning 
and guidance 
needed for the 
street to function 
safely and 
efficiently.
A study of 119 
residential traffic 
circles installed in 
the city of Seattle 
between 1991 
and 1994 found 
that reported 
crashes in those 
areas declined 
from 187 before 
installation to 11 
after installation, 
and injuries 
declined from 153 
to 1 in the same 
period (Welle et al. 
2015, 38).
