APPENDIX F
World Resources Institute Lives Saved 
methodology for Bloomberg Initiative 
for Global Road Safety
WRI presents the Lives Saved Report annually to showcase 
the progress of monitoring and evaluation efforts from WRI’s 
work on safer streets in 16 cities as part of BIGRS. This internal 
report for a major donor presents and highlights the estimated 
numbers of lives saved by specific road safety interventions, 
including (i) physical changes to street infrastructure; (ii) road 
safety guidelines and publications; (iii) training/workshops/
capacity building; and (iv) road safety plans/policies and laws. 
These lives-saved estimations for site-specific infrastructure 
interventions are based on several variables, including 
site-level historical crash data, citywide collision trends, 
baseline traffic counts in each city, and the type of new road 
safety infrastructure built as a result of WRI’s support. While 
challenges of availability, quality, and completeness of collected 
data persist, methods using variables of completed projects are 
applied to estimate the number of lives saved by 2040. 
This is calculated in two ways: (i) using Crash Reduction 
Factors (CRFs), a multiplicative factor used to compute 
and estimate the percentage reduction in crashes after 
implementing a specific type of countermeasure at a specific 
site; or (ii) using Crash Modification Factors (CMFs), the relative 
change in crash frequency due to a change in one specific 
condition, while all other conditions and site characteristics 
remain constant. As the actual effectiveness will vary from 
site to site, CMFs are regarded as generic estimates of 
the effectiveness of a countermeasure. The relationship 
between CMFs and CRFs can be demonstrated with the 
equation: CMF = 1 −  CRF
Appropriate CRFs are determined through continuous literature 
reviews of evidence-based studies that compare site collision 
data or number of crashes at a treated site before and after 
implementation for each given type of road safety intervention 
under multiple case studies and road environments. 
Where possible, WRI selects CRFs that were developed in 
environments similar to the project sites.
A selected CRF is then multiplied against the fatalities that were 
recorded at a specific site over several years (at least three) 
before the project under evaluation was implemented. In the 
absence of site-specific historical collision data, data from other 
similar road environments are transferred and applied to the 
site in the form of fatalities per km per year. When applicable, 
citywide fatality data are also used to estimate the expected 
reduction in fatalities from projects lacking site-specific 
collision data. A sample formula of this method is as follows:
LSIN = [CRF1*Im1*SF/km/y*km1*Yn1]+[CRF2* Im2*SF/
km/y*km2*Yn2] + + + [CRFx * Imx * SF/km/y * kmx * Ynx]
Where:
LSIN = Total lives saved in relation to 
infrastructure improvements 
CRFx = Crash Reduction Factor for the xth intervention 
Imx = Number of implementations (Im) for the xth intervention 
SF/km/y = Existing site fatalities per km per year 
kmx = Length in kms implemented for the xth intervention 
Ynx = Assessment period of n number of years for the 
xth intervention
x = No. of interventions at site to avoid different types of 
collisions (without overlapping effects). 
Under the use of this “Additive Method” formula, each equation 
inside a set of square brackets represents one countermeasure 
in the same geographic location.
As per FHWA guidelines, the total value of the lives saved 
estimate using CRFs cannot exceed 100 percent of the potential 
reduction of site fatalities. In other words, the total site fatalities 
projected over the intervention area and time frame must be 
equal to or greater than the total-lives-saved estimate (SF/km/y 
* km * Yn ≥ LSIN). If the projected lives saved value is greater 
than the 100 percent potential reduction, the latter  value is 
adopted as the projected site fatalities.

Safer Streets in Mumbai  I 243

CMFs are developed from the results of evaluation studies that 
measure the effectiveness of intervention(s) by comparing the 
change in the value of the safety performance measure (e.g., 
frequency of crashes) with the change that would be expected 
if the site had not been treated. If properly calculated, local 
CMFs are more likely to account for actual characteristics 
in the locality, such as driver, roadway, traffic, and climate 
characteristics. There are several ways to develop site-specific 
CMFs that include three types of before-after study methods—
simple or  naïve, before-after study with comparison group, 
and before-after study with the empirical Bayes method—and 
cross-sectional analysis. Before-after studies focus on the 
changes in safety over time, while the cross-sectional analysis 
focuses on the differences in safety between locations.
A CMF with a value of less than 1.0 indicates that a treatment 
has the potential to reduce crashes, while a CMF with a value 
greater than 1.0 indicates that a treatment has the potential 
to increase crashes. For example, a CMF value of 1.11 for a 
particular countermeasure means that there would be an 
11 percent increase in the estimated total frequency if the 
intervention were to be implemented.
However, both factors, and by extension, both measures, 
represent a significant drawback typical to traditional road 
safety analysis—reliance solely on traffic crash data. Crash-
based approaches are reactive as a significant number of 
crashes need to occur and be recorded before action can 
take place—a minimum of three to five years of crash data 
before and after implementation is needed for a robust 
intervention evaluation. The following paradox ensues: road 
safety practitioners must wait for crashes to occur to prevent 
them, hampering safety analysis at a greater scale in countries 
with poor quality and unreliable crash data. In recent decades, 
progress in sensing technologies and statistical modeling 
has made widespread adoption of traffic conflict analysis 
as a surrogate for crashes feasible and inexpensive. The 
underlying assumption here is that the process of a serious 
conflict is almost identical to that of a serious crash, and 
therefore traffic conflicts provide insight into the mechanism 
that leads to road collisions. However, WRI is yet to deploy 
the use of traffic conflict analysis using surrogate indicators 
such as (i) time-to-accident (TTA); (ii) time-to-collision (TTC); 
(iii) post-encroachment-time (PET); (iv) gap-time (GT); and (v) 
deceleration-to-safety time (DST).

 I 255

ABOUT WRI INDIA
WRI India, an independent charity legally registered as the India 
Resources Trust, provides objective information and practical 
proposals to foster environmentally sound and socially equitable 
development. Our work focuses on building sustainable and 
liveable cities and working towards a low carbon economy. 
Through research, analysis, and recommendations, WRI India puts 
ideas into action to build transformative solutions to protect the 
earth, promote livelihoods, and enhance human well-being. We are 
inspired by and associated with World Resources Institute (WRI), a 
global research organization. Know more: www.wri-india.org
Our challenge
Natural resources are at the foundation of economic opportunity 
and human well-being. But today, we are depleting Earth’s 
resources at rates that are not sustainable, endangering economies 
and people’s lives. People depend on clean water, fertile land, 
healthy forests, and a stable climate. Livable cities and clean 
energy are essential for a sustainable planet. We must address 
these urgent, global challenges this decade.
Our vision
We envision an equitable and prosperous planet driven by the wise 
management of natural resources. We aspire to create a world 
where the actions of government, business, and communities 
combine to eliminate poverty and sustain the natural environment 
for all people.
Our approach
COUNT IT
We start with data. We conduct independent research and draw on 
the latest technology to develop new insights and recommenda -
tions. Our rigorous analysis identifies risks, unveils opportunities, 
and informs smart strategies. We focus our efforts on influential 
and emerging economies where the future of sustainability 
will be determined.
CHANGE IT
We use our research to inform government policies, business 
strategies, and civil society action. We test projects with 
communities, companies, and government agencies to build a 
strong evidence base. Then, we work with partners to deliver 
change on the ground that alleviates poverty and strengthens 
society. We hold ourselves accountable to ensure our outcomes 
will be bold and enduring.
SCALE IT
We don’t think small. Once tested, we work with partners to adopt 
and expand our efforts regionally and globally. We engage with 
decision-makers to carry out our ideas and elevate our impact. We 
measure success through government and business actions that 
improve people’s lives and sustain a healthy environment.
Each WRI India report represents a timely, scholarly treatment of a subject of public concern. WRI India takes responsibility for choosing the study topics and guaranteeing its authors 
and researchers freedom of inquiry. It also solicits and responds to the guidance of advisory panels and expert reviewers. Unless otherwise stated, however, all the interpretation and 
findings set forth in WRI India publications are those of the authors. 
 
Maps are for illustrative purposes and do not imply the expression of any opinion on the part of WRI India, concerning the legal status of any country or territory or concerning the 
delimitation of frontiers or boundaries.
